	<item>
		<title>Building an Agentic AI Platform on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/building-an-agentic-ai-platform-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Omar Elkharbotly]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 28 Oct 2026 18:28:26 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8bd827344f7a730b420aced0542c5d0dd666efbf</guid>

					<description>Stand up a governed, multi-account Agentic AI Platform on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. This post walks through the architecture and an open-source reference blueprint: the operating model, control plane, per-Workstream cells, separate inference and tool gateways, and a CI/CD evaluation pipeline that gates each agent before production.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you’ve tried to get an agent into production, the model and tool use are not the blocker. The main bottleneck is everything around them: how many teams can build within a secure environment, which team owns each model and tool, how AgentOps and error handling stay inside a pre-defined boundary, and how platform, Workstream, and finance teams see cost and health across accounts. For a Platform team that wants to support many agentic use cases, that’s a platform product to design and operate. In this post, we refer to the team that manages the platform as the Platform team, and to the teams that build on it as Workstream teams. Workstream teams are the domain or product teams that build and own agents on the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each Workstream team needs a paved road with approved models and tools, short-lived workload identity, isolated runtime and memory, automated evaluation, rollback, telemetry, and cost attribution. These controls need to remain consistent as Workstream teams customize prompts, tools, data access, and application behavior. When every team assembles them, controls drift, shared services become a bottleneck, and ownership is difficult to audit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the architecture and reference implementation of an enterprise Agentic AI Platform on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/a&gt; We cover the multi-account operating model, the Platform control plane, a repeatable per-Workstream deployment pattern, separate inference and tool gateways, and an evaluation pipeline that gates each agent before promotion to production. By the end, you should be able to deploy a governed Workstream from the companion blueprint, validate its security and lifecycle contracts, and use it as the foundation for your own agentic AI platform in the organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; provides modular services for building, deploying, and operating agents at scale, including runtime, gateway, identity, memory, policy, registry, and evaluations. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-ai-agent-factory/tree/main/enterprise-agentic-ai-platform-blueprint" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Enterprise Agentic AI Platform Blueprint&lt;/a&gt;, built on AgentCore, adds the surrounding enterprise contracts as deployable infrastructure as code: ownership, account boundaries, software delivery, observability, cost governance, and retirement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architectural-principles"&gt;Architectural principles&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before diving into the solution details, it helps to understand the organizational needs that drive the platform architecture. The common challenges we have heard from customers who establish an agent platform fall into these five categories:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governed model access:&lt;/strong&gt; Every untrusted inference request must pass through an approved inference gateway. The gateway applies the organization’s guardrails (for example, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; or a third-party provider), restricts which models and inference AWS Regions each Workstream can use, and enforces per-Workstream rate limits, token quotas, and budgets so that no single team can exhaust shared capacity. It also carries workload identity and emits attribution data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governed tool access:&lt;/strong&gt; Agents must discover and invoke only approved tools through an authenticated Model Context Protocol (MCP) gateway, with exact targets, least-privilege execution roles, policy decisions, and an auditable owner.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Delegated delivery:&lt;/strong&gt; Workstream teams (for example, product teams) need to ship and roll back independently inside pre-approved boundaries, without waiting for the platform team to deploy each application or granting developers a direct infrastructure write path.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fleet operations and cost:&lt;/strong&gt; Several personas need visibility across accounts without gaining write access to team workloads. Agent operations engineers need logs, metrics, traces, and quotas. Workstream owners need cost per agent against their budgets. FinOps and finance teams need chargeback by team or business unit through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-explorer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-and-usage-reporting/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Report (CUR)&lt;/a&gt;. Security and governance leads need evidence of which workload called which model and tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bounded evidence and lifecycle:&lt;/strong&gt; Every agent release needs proof at each boundary it touches: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model access:&lt;/strong&gt; An approved model call succeeds, and a call to an unapproved model or Region is denied.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool access:&lt;/strong&gt; A subscribed tool call succeeds, and a call to a tool outside the agent’s subscription is denied with the expected error.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity:&lt;/strong&gt; A request from another workstream’s account or identity is rejected.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Release:&lt;/strong&gt; The previous agent runtime version can be restored.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Retirement:&lt;/strong&gt; Teardown removes the agent’s runtime, memory, tool gateway, and tool grants in dependency order, and confirms nothing is left behind.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The blueprint addresses these needs through four connected planes: developer experience, platform control, Workstream execution, and assurance and operations. The unit of scale is the Workstream cell, not a manually configured agent or one shared workload account. The blueprint &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-ai-agent-factory/tree/main/enterprise-agentic-ai-platform-blueprint" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;README&lt;/a&gt; documents the responsibilities of each plane in detail. And you will read the main planes: &lt;strong&gt;Agentic AI Platform control plane&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;Repeatable Workstream cell&lt;/strong&gt; in the later paragraphs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operating-models-and-platform-components"&gt;Operating models and platform components&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enterprises usually adopt one of three operating models for agentic AI:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Centralized:&lt;/strong&gt; A single artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML) team builds and runs every agent. Governance is consistent, but that team quickly becomes a bottleneck as the number of use cases grows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decentralized:&lt;/strong&gt; Each business unit builds and operates its own agents. Teams can move quickly, but without shared standards, controls tend to drift, effort gets duplicated, and cost visibility suffers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Federated:&lt;/strong&gt; A central Platform team provides shared, governed services while domain teams retain ownership of their agents, tools, and data. This model balances team autonomy with consistent guardrails and is where most enterprises land.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For assistance choosing, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/govern-architect-agentic-ai/governance-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Prescriptive Guidance page on Governance Models&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Following patterns we have observed in customer engagements, the blueprint implements the federated model. The Platform team is the hub, typically a Platform product manager, architect, platform engineers, agent operations engineers, and a security and governance lead. Workstream teams are the spokes, made up of agent developers, a domain tech lead, and AI/ML engineers. An executive sponsor and the CISO sit above both.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this blueprint, the Platform team owns the definition of each component: templates, versions, policies, and admission. Each Workstream account runs its own instance of the per-team components (Runtime, Memory, Tool Gateway), so a failure or rollback stays inside one team’s boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution delivers a governed, multi-account Agentic AI Platform that platform teams operate, and workstream teams such as internal product and engineering teams consume through versioned, self-service interfaces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At enterprise scale, the developer experience plane is a catalog of organization-approved, versioned agent templates. The blueprint ships reference templates for &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.langchain.com/langgraph" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://crewai.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CrewAI&lt;/a&gt;, but you can use your choice of framework when the template preserves the same inference gateway, tool gateway, identity, policy, evaluation, and delivery contracts. Application teams own prompts, agent logic, tools, and evaluation cases. The Platform team owns the common controls and release requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Out of the four planes, two carry most of the architecture:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agentic AI Platform control plane:&lt;/strong&gt; The Platform account publishes versioned templates and interfaces, operates AWS Agent Registry governance records and shared Inference Gateways, admits models and Guardrails, orchestrates reviewed releases, and aggregates fleet signals. Teams consume these capabilities through code, descriptors, and pipelines rather than administrator sessions or deployment tickets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Repeatable Workstream cell:&lt;/strong&gt; Each product or domain receives an isolated boundary with a team-owned source repository, a Workload pipeline, and three Amazon Bedrock AgentCore capabilities: AgentCore Runtime, actor-scoped AgentCore Memory, and an AgentCore Gateway that uses AWS_IAM authorization and exposes only the approved tool targets. The cell also holds local customer managed AWS KMS keys, tags, alarms, budgets, and a CloudWatch Observability Access Manager (OAM) source link. One cell can deploy, fail, roll back, or retire without coordinating with another team’s release.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-components"&gt;Key components&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the operating model and governed flow of the solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21798-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21798-1.png" alt="Operating model linking the governance foundation, Platform control plane, and team-owned Workstream cells" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Enterprise Agentic AI Platform operating model, showing the governance foundation, the Platform control plane, repeatable team-owned Workstream cells, and the software delivery, inference, tool, telemetry, and ownership flows between them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture consists of the following account layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Management and governance foundation.&lt;/strong&gt; AWS Organizations, AWS IAM Identity Center, preventive policy, audit, retention, and cost policy establish the enterprise boundary. These are logical responsibilities that can map to an existing AWS Control Tower landing zone rather than requiring a duplicate account structure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security, audit, and observability layer.&lt;/strong&gt; AWS CloudTrail, AWS Config, AWS Security Hub, Amazon GuardDuty, log archive, and an Amazon CloudWatch OAM sink provide independent control-plane evidence and fleet visibility. Central operators can query shared signals without gaining write access to Workstream resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Platform layer of Agentic AI Platform&lt;/strong&gt;. Separate Platform environments own golden paths, the governance catalog, Guardrails, shared Inference Gateways, exact tool aliases, pipelines, compatibility, quotas, and common operational interfaces. Platform changes are tested against representative cells before wider rollout.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workstream fleet layer.&lt;/strong&gt; Each product, domain, or regulated boundary receives one or more nonproduction and production cells. Teams own Runtime, Memory, Tool Gateway, tools, data, prompts, evaluation cases, application incidents, and service-level objectives (SLOs). The same baseline can be repeated across many cells while preserving account-level isolation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-walkthrough"&gt;Solution walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections trace the key design decisions: account topology, preventive and detective controls, the AgentCore execution path, and the delivery pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="account-topology-and-organizational-units"&gt;Account topology and organizational units&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The blueprint defines logical account roles that separate enterprise governance, shared Platform services, and team-owned execution:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Landing-zone placement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Management foundation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Root / Security and Management&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Organizations, Identity Center, preventive policy, audit, retention, and cost policy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Security and log archive&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Security&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudTrail, AWS Config, Security Hub, GuardDuty, and retained audit data&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Observability&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Security or Operations&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch OAM sink and fleet logs, metrics, traces, and cost views&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agentic AI Platform&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent Platform&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Golden paths, AWS Agent Registry, Guardrails, Inference Gateways, pipelines, and fleet controls&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Workstream cell&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent Workstreams&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Team-owned Runtime, Memory, Tool Gateway, tools, data, evaluation, and application SLOs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Additional Workstream cells&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent Workstreams&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Repeat the same baseline per product, domain, environment, or regulated boundary&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This topology integrates into an existing landing zone, a pre-configured, secure, and scalable multi-account AWS environment, where the Management, Platform, and Workstream labels describe responsibilities rather than a fixed three-account estate. An organization can operate multiple validated Platform environments and many Workstream account pairs. Start with one representative cell and prove onboarding, production promotion, rollback, centralized observability, and zero-residual retirement before repeating the baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="service-control-policies"&gt;Service control policies&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Service control policies constrain the maximum permissions available to Platform and Workstream accounts. They complement, rather than replace, IAM, gateway authorization, Guardrails, resource policies, network controls, and pipeline evidence. The blueprint renders and reviews the effective policies for the exact account and Region instead of relying on source-code intent alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model and Region admission:&lt;/strong&gt; Organization policy and Gateway execution roles restrict inference to approved models and the supported Region. Adding a model or Region requires service availability, residency, IAM, quality, cost, rollback, observability, and denial validation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrail and inference enforcement:&lt;/strong&gt; Agents do not call model providers directly. Inference crosses the governed gateway, where the stage Guardrail and model allow-list are applied.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pipeline-only Workstream mutation:&lt;/strong&gt; Workstream teams open pull requests and use the Workload pipeline. Direct developer deployment into Workstream accounts is outside the supported operating model, which keeps the reviewed release and evidence path non-bypassable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Registry and Gateway governance:&lt;/strong&gt; Workstream Gateway synthesis fails when a subscribed Registry record is missing, changed, or not approved.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scoped deployment roles:&lt;/strong&gt; Bootstrap policies are generated per account and Region, validated with AWS Identity and Access Management Access (IAM) Analyzer, and checked through effective-policy simulation. Administrator access is reserved for initial setup rather than routine pipeline execution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Separation of duties and adversarial proof:&lt;/strong&gt; Platform controls model, Guardrail, Registry, and shared-service admission. Workstream teams control application behavior. Every denied path is paired with an authorized positive call and a mutation that proves the intended control caused the denial.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An administrator inside a Workstream account can’t create a supported fast path around these controls. New or changed organization policies need a sandbox or representative-cell soak, exact allow and deny tests, and a rollback plan before broad attachment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cicd-pipeline-with-evaluation-gates"&gt;CI/CD pipeline with evaluation gates&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The blueprint uses reviewed Platform and Workload pipelines to enforce the following promotion sequence:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Source and review:&lt;/strong&gt; A pull request establishes the immutable source revision. The pipeline pins the intended branch so an unmerged change can’t silently build the repository default branch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build and synth:&lt;/strong&gt; The pipeline compiles code, runs tests and linting, creates the agent manifest, scans the digest-bound image, validates policies, resolves approved Registry descriptors, and inspects synthesized AWS CloudFormation and cdk-nag output.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stable roles and permission handoff:&lt;/strong&gt; The Workload pipeline creates stable Workstream roles and pauses. The Platform pipeline grants exact tool aliases to the exact Gateway role, live policies are verified, and only then does the Workload pipeline continue.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deploy to nonproduction:&lt;/strong&gt; The pipeline deploys Runtime, Memory, Tool Gateway, observability, alarms, budgets, tags, and application resources into the cell’s nonproduction boundary.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluate the deployed agent:&lt;/strong&gt; The gate invokes the real Runtime and measures regression, quality, tool success, refusal behavior, first-token latency, and cost. Every authorized call is paired with an adversarial twin: the gate calls an approved model and confirms success, then calls an unapproved model and confirms the denial. The same pattern applies to tools, cross-account identity, and throttling. If an expected denial is missing, the gate fails the build.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human approval:&lt;/strong&gt; A reviewer examines the deployed evidence, known limitations, and residual risk. A generic green build is not enough when a required probe, resource, or expected denial is missing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Promote, observe, and roll back:&lt;/strong&gt; Production promotion uses the reviewed revision and continuity checks. Runtime sessions are sampled through update, cancellation, rollback, restore, and re-run so the pipeline proves both the forward path and recovery path.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;No Workstream deployment step has a manual fast track. The Platform team owns the release contract, but Workstream teams operate their own pipeline instances and deployment cadence. Retirement follows a strict dependency order: Platform alias grants are revoked first, then Workstream Gateway and tool roles, then Runtime, Memory, and application resources. Each step is verified before proceeding, and a final inventory confirms zero residual resources remain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-governance"&gt;Security and governance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The blueprint implements defense in depth through reinforcing identity, policy, delivery, data, and evidence controls:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity and trust boundaries:&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and Amazon Cognito provide short-lived inference credentials. AWS_IAM and SigV4 authenticate tool traffic. Exact Runtime and Gateway roles, issuer and audience checks, account and Region context, and scoped bootstrap trust limit each principal to its intended path.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model safety and admission:&lt;/strong&gt; Inference flows through the governed gateway described in the Solution overview, applying Guardrails and model allow-lists before every call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool authorization:&lt;/strong&gt; The Tool Gateway invokes only Registry-approved aliases, as detailed in the Solution overview. Cedar policies provide authorization, with a retained AWS Lambda wrapper for defense in depth.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tenant data protection:&lt;/strong&gt; Workstream accounts isolate application state. AgentCore memory uses actor-scoped namespaces and customer-managed KMS keys. Tools and application data remain team-owned, and retention, deletion, and access policies are explicit rather than inherited from a shared mutable workload account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Software supply chain and release:&lt;/strong&gt; Reviewed source, manifest hashing, reproducible builds, digest-bound image scanning, policy validation, cdk-nag checks, deployed-runtime evaluation, human approval, rollback, and dependency-ordered teardown form one release contract. A missing check fails closed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audit, observability, and cost governance:&lt;/strong&gt; CloudTrail and retained control-plane data corroborate pipeline evidence. OAM shares logs, metrics, and traces with central operators. Standard allocation tags, budgets, quotas, and AWS Cost and Usage Report reconciliation assign ownership without giving the agent permission to rewrite its own attribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository is a reference implementation. Developers can adjust it to address their needs. The deployment and available features are based on the eu-west-1 (Ireland) Region. A substituted component, another Region, or a wider organizational rollout requires its own availability, security, load, rollback, observability, and teardown evidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walked through an enterprise Agentic AI Platform built on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The architecture separates a centrally governed Platform control plane from repeatable, team-owned Workstream cells, with distinct inference and tool gateways, and production promotion gated by deployed-runtime evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The companion repository includes a blueprint that validates these controls end to end, from pipeline gates and adversarial boundary tests through zero-residual teardown. The support claim stops at that tested boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, deploy one representative Workstream cell from the companion repository, prove its full lifecycle, and then automate additional cells from the same versioned baseline.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Distributed training with Ray Train DLCs and EFA on EKS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/distributed-training-with-ray-train-dlcs-and-efa-on-eks/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sunita Koppar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 28 Oct 2026 18:19:54 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ad7c54a5a92c3c3ab550b696c83e5204108e2c13</guid>

					<description>The Ray Train Deep Learning Container ships the full CUDA-through-EFA stack, so you build no image. This post walks through a reproducible sample that fine-tunes Qwen2.5-1.5B with PyTorch FSDP across eight GPUs on two EFA-enabled g6.12xlarge instances on Amazon EKS, using the container exactly as published.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;What usually stops &lt;a href="https://pytorch.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PyTorch&lt;/a&gt; training from scaling out to a cluster is the container, not the model code. Every container needs the same &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-programming-guide/01-introduction/cuda-platform.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUDA runtime&lt;/a&gt;, a PyTorch build compiled against it, &lt;a href="https://developer.nvidia.com/nccl" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NCCL&lt;/a&gt; (NVIDIA Collective Communications Library), the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/hpc/efa/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA)&lt;/a&gt; userspace libraries, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/aws-ofi-nccl" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS OFI NCCL&lt;/a&gt; plugin that connects NCCL to EFA, and a &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray&lt;/a&gt; version the cluster operator agrees with. Building that image and rebuilding it each time one of those pieces moves is a project of its own, and it has nothing to do with the model you set out to train. The &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/ray-train/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Train Deep Learning Container&lt;/a&gt; (DLC) is that image, built by AWS. AWS assembles the stack, tests it as one unit, publishes it in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) Public and keeps it patched.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-1-1.png" alt="EKS cluster with a CPU system node running KubeRay and the Ray head, and two EFA-enabled g6.12xlarge GPU worker nodes in one Availability Zone" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: One EKS cluster with a control plane managed by AWS, a CPU system node (m7i.xlarge) running the KubeRay operator and Ray head, and two EFA-enabled g6.12xlarge nodes in one Availability Zone. KubeRay builds it from one RayCluster manifest, Ray Train places eight workers, one per GPU, and inter-node collectives cross over EFA.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ray runs a job across machines as a single cluster with two roles. The Ray head coordinates and runs the global control store that tracks cluster state, the dashboard and job server you submit jobs to. Ray worker nodes supply the GPUs and run the training processes. Each worker joins the cluster by connecting to the head’s address when it starts. On Kubernetes, each of these is a pod. Kubernetes has no built-in idea of a Ray cluster, so something must create the head and worker pods, start Ray in the right role in each, point the workers at the head, and replace pods that fail. &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/ray-train/deployment/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KubeRay&lt;/a&gt; does this. It’s an open source Kubernetes operator that adds a RayCluster resource: you describe a head group and a worker group in one manifest, and KubeRay builds and maintains the cluster from it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single tagged image runs as both the Ray head and the Ray workers, whether KubeRay starts it on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt; or you start it yourself on Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2). What you add is configuration: which instances, how many GPUs each pod gets, which network transport NCCL uses, and which extra Python packages a job needs. All of it is set from outside the image.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post walks through a sample that uses the DLC exactly as published to fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B with PyTorch Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) across eight NVIDIA L4 GPUs on two g6.12xlarge instances. The training code is one function, the cluster is one RayCluster manifest plus a file of variables, and no image is built at any point. Because the DLC already carries the EFA stack, collectives between the two instances run over the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/efa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EFA&lt;/a&gt; as soon as the pods request it. The entire solution is in this &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-aws-deep-learning-containers/tree/main/training/ray-train" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;, and it uses the same &lt;code&gt;KubeRay-on-EKS&lt;/code&gt; foundation as our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/run-multi-node-inference-workloads-using-ray-llm-deep-learning-containers/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;multi-node Ray Serve walkthrough&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-you-get-without-building-anything"&gt;What you get without building anything&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Ray Train DLC is the PyTorch training image with Ray’s training libraries on top. The tag this sample pulls, &lt;code&gt;public.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers/ray:train-ml-cuda-v1.1&lt;/code&gt;, replaces a build that would otherwise look like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you would otherwise build and keep aligned&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In the Ray Train DLC&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Distributed runtime&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ray with the dashboard and job server, at a version KubeRay supports&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ray 2.58.0 with Train, Tune and Data&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Framework&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PyTorch compiled for your CUDA version&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PyTorch 2.13.0, torchvision 0.28.0&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPU runtime&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CUDA, cuDNN and NCCL releases that agree with each other&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CUDA 13.0.2, cuDNN, NCCL 2.29.7&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Networking&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EFA installer, libfabric and &lt;code&gt;aws-ofi-nccl&lt;/code&gt;, built against that NCCL&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EFA latest, AWS OFI NCCL plugin, GDRCopy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Training libraries&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DeepSpeed, flash-attn and Transformer Engine compiled for your CUDA&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DeepSpeed, flash-attn, Transformer Engine, PyTorch Lightning, Hugging Face libraries&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Diagnostics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NCCL tests built from source&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;all_reduce_perf&lt;/code&gt; already on the path&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The networking row is the one most teams would least like to own. A mismatch there doesn’t produce an error. NCCL drops to TCP sockets, and the job runs to completion at a fraction of the speed. In the DLC, those libraries are built and tested together, and AWS gates each release on multi-node EFA and multi-node KubeRay training tests, which are exactly the paths this sample uses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few more properties remove work that you would otherwise do per cluster:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No registry setup –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The image is public, so nodes pull it without credentials and nothing is built or pushed on the way.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No driver matching –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;On startup the entrypoint checks the host’s NVIDIA driver and, if it is older than the image’s CUDA, turns on the bundled CUDA forward-compatibility libraries automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No head-versus-worker images –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The image has no fixed role. Whatever starts the container supplies the &lt;code&gt;ray start&lt;/code&gt; command, so one tag serves every pod.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No separate inference image family –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Ray Train shares the &lt;code&gt;ray&lt;/code&gt; ECR repository with the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/ray/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Serve DLC&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/ray-llm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray LLM DLCs&lt;/a&gt;, under the &lt;code&gt;train-ml&lt;/code&gt; tag prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configure-it-dont-rebuild-it"&gt;Configure it, don’t rebuild it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Everything that differs between one training job and the next is set around the image. The sample uses each of these mechanisms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you control&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How you set it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In this sample&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Head or worker role&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KubeRay &lt;code&gt;rayStartParams&lt;/code&gt; per pod group&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Same &lt;code&gt;DLC_IMAGE&lt;/code&gt; for both. Head runs with &lt;code&gt;num-gpus: "0"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPUs and EFA per pod&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Kubernetes resource requests&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;nvidia.com/gpu: 4&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;vpc.amazonaws.com/efa: 1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Network transport&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Environment variables on the pod or the job&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;FI_PROVIDER&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;NCCL_NET_PLUGIN&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;NCCL_DEBUG&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Extra Python packages&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ray &lt;code&gt;runtime_env&lt;/code&gt; at submit time&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Any package a job needs, installed at submit time&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Image version&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Versioned or digest-pinned tag in &lt;code&gt;DLC_IMAGE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;train-ml-cuda-v1.1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cluster shape&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Variables in scripts/&lt;code&gt;env.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Instance type, node count, GPUs per node, Region, AZ&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Training job&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Arguments to &lt;code&gt;train.py&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model, steps, sequence length, batch size&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Packages per job.&lt;/strong&gt; The image ships the HuggingFace training stack, so this sample’s job needs nothing added. When an experiment does need a library the image does not carry, Ray installs it for that job at submit time through &lt;code&gt;runtime_env&lt;/code&gt; and the image is unchanged. No new image, no new tag.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Transport per job.&lt;/strong&gt; The image defaults to &lt;code&gt;FI_PROVIDER=efa&lt;/code&gt;, and its NCCL configuration excludes the loopback and Docker bridge interfaces. On EKS you point &lt;code&gt;NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME&lt;/code&gt; at the pod’s &lt;code&gt;eth0&lt;/code&gt;. The sample sets all four networking variables in the manifest anyway, so the behavior is visible in one file and doesn’t depend on image defaults. Because these are plain environment variables, a single job can override them through its &lt;code&gt;runtime_env&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Versions you choose.&lt;/strong&gt; The sample pins &lt;code&gt;train-ml-cuda-v1.1&lt;/code&gt;, the current minor release, so a cluster is reproducible from one run to the next. The &lt;code&gt;vX.Y&lt;/code&gt; tags move with security rebuilds. The fully qualified &lt;code&gt;vX.Y.Z&lt;/code&gt; tags stay frozen at the original release build, so pin a digest when you need a byte-identical run. The same image is also available from private ECR URIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-the-cluster-is-laid-out"&gt;How the cluster is laid out&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-2-1.png" alt="Sample cluster: a CPU system node runs the KubeRay operator and a Ray head with no GPUs, and two EFA-enabled g6.12xlarge nodes each run one four-GPU Ray worker pod" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The sample’s cluster. Every pod runs the same Ray Train DLC from Amazon ECR Public. The CPU system node runs the KubeRay operator and a Ray head with no GPUs. Each EFA-enabled g6.12xlarge, both in one Availability Zone, runs a single four-GPU Ray worker pod.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All nodes sit in private subnets of one virtual private cloud (VPC) and reach the internet only through a NAT Gateway, which is how every node pulls the DLC from Amazon ECR Public and how workers download model weights from Hugging Face. One m7i.xlarge forms the system node group, hosting kube-system add-ons, the KubeRay operator and the Ray head. The head runs the job server on port 8265 and Ray’s control store on 6379, and it advertises no GPUs, so no training work lands on it. Jobs are submitted from inside the head pod with &lt;code&gt;kubectl exec&lt;/code&gt;, through the EKS API, so port 8265 is never exposed outside the cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training runs on two &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/g6/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;g6.12xlarge&lt;/a&gt; instances, each with four NVIDIA L4 GPUs, 48 vCPUs, 192 GiB of memory, 40 Gbps of networking and one EFA interface. Each runs one Ray worker pod that claims all four GPUs and the EFA interface, and Ray Train places four workers in each pod: ranks 0–3 on one instance, 4–7 on the other. Because EFA traffic doesn’t cross Availability Zones, the scripts place both instances in a single zone that has been checked to offer g6.12xlarge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-ray-train-does-inside-the-container"&gt;What Ray Train does inside the container&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The DLC provides the stack. &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/train/train.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Train&lt;/a&gt; provides the part that would otherwise be launcher code. Multi-node PyTorch normally means starting a process per GPU on every host and giving each one its rank, the world size and a rendezvous address. With Ray Train you write the training loop as a function and pass it to &lt;code&gt;TorchTrainer&lt;/code&gt; with a &lt;code&gt;ScalingConfig&lt;/code&gt;. Ray starts one worker per GPU wherever the cluster has GPUs, forms the &lt;code&gt;torch.distributed&lt;/code&gt; process group, and runs your function on each worker.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This sample asks Ray Train for &lt;a href="https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/fsdp.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;FSDP&lt;/a&gt;, which splits the model’s weights, gradients and optimizer state across all eight GPUs rather than copying them to each one. Qwen2.5-1.5B in bfloat16 needs about 12 GB for those four tensors. Sharded eight ways, each L4 GPU holds roughly 1.5 GB. The price is communication: FSDP gathers each layer’s weights before using it and reduce-scatters gradients afterwards, so the whole model crosses between ranks on every step. Between the two instances, that traffic is what the DLC’s EFA stack carries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="before-you-start"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You must have the following in place before you start:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools:&lt;/strong&gt; AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) v2 signed in to the target account, eksctl, kubectl, Helm, and &lt;code&gt;envsubst&lt;/code&gt; from GNU gettext.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quota:&lt;/strong&gt; 96 vCPUs of G-family On-Demand capacity for the two g6.12xlarge instances, in an AWS Region where at least one zone offers them. If you already hold an On-Demand Capacity Reservation for the instances, set &lt;code&gt;CAPACITY_RESERVATION_ID&lt;/code&gt; instead. The scripts launch the node group into it and skip the capacity search.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Budget:&lt;/strong&gt; about $9.50 an hour while the GPU instances are up, covering both instances, the EKS control plane and the NAT Gateway.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A quick way to confirm that the CLI is pointed at the account that you expect:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sts get-caller-identity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-training-code-one-function-and-a-trainer"&gt;The training code: One function and a trainer&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;code/train.py&lt;/code&gt; is the whole job, and it contains nothing specific to the container, the cluster or the network. Ray runs &lt;code&gt;train_func&lt;/code&gt; once per GPU. It asks Ray for its rank, loads the model, hands it to &lt;code&gt;prepare_model&lt;/code&gt; for FSDP, and runs an ordinary PyTorch loop:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;code/train.py&lt;/code&gt; — the per-GPU function (abridged)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def train_func(config):
    ctx = ray.train.get_context()
    world_size, rank = ctx.get_world_size(), ctx.get_world_rank()
    device = ray.train.torch.get_device()

    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(config["model_id"], torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16)

    # Any submodule above 10M parameters becomes its own FSDP unit. Wrapping by
    # size, not by a model-specific layer class, keeps the script model-agnostic.
    auto_wrap_policy = partial(size_based_auto_wrap_policy, min_num_params=int(1e7))
    model = ray.train.torch.prepare_model(
        model,
        parallel_strategy="fsdp",
        parallel_strategy_kwargs={"auto_wrap_policy": auto_wrap_policy},
    )
    optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=config["learning_rate"])

    for step in range(config["steps"]):
        input_ids = torch.randint(0, model.config.vocab_size,
            (config["batch_size"], config["seq_len"]), device=device)
        optimizer.zero_grad()
        loss = model(input_ids=input_ids, labels=input_ids).loss
        loss.backward()  # parameters gathered again, gradients reduce-scattered
        optimizer.step()  # local: each rank updates only its own slice
        ray.train.report({"step": step, "loss": loss.item()})&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The driver joins the running cluster, counts its GPUs and starts the trainer. Because the worker count comes from the cluster, the same script runs unchanged on one node or many:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;code/train.py&lt;/code&gt; — the driver (abridged)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;ray.init(address="auto")  # join the RayCluster
num_workers = int(ray.cluster_resources().get("GPU", 0))  # 8 here

trainer = TorchTrainer(
    train_func,
    train_loop_config={...},
    scaling_config=ScalingConfig(num_workers=num_workers, use_gpu=True),
    run_config=RunConfig(name="ray-train-fsdp-llm"),
)
result = trainer.fit()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The batches are random token IDs so that the run depends on nothing but the cluster. The sample is judged on whether real FSDP collectives crossed both nodes and the job completed. For a real fine-tune, replace the random tensor with a Ray Data or DataLoader pipeline. Nothing else in the function changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-manifest-the-only-place-the-dlc-is-configured"&gt;The manifest: The only place the DLC is configured&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;manifest/raycluster.yaml&lt;/code&gt; is where the image meets the cluster, and it is short because the image needs so little. The head’s only special setting is &lt;code&gt;NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=void&lt;/code&gt;, which stops the container runtime’s NVIDIA hook from failing on a node that has no GPU. The worker group holds everything else:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;code&gt;manifest/raycluster.yaml&lt;/code&gt; — worker group (abridged)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;workerGroupSpecs:
  - groupName: gpu-workers
    replicas: 2
    rayStartParams:
      num-gpus: "${GPUS_PER_NODE}"  # keeps Ray's GPU count equal to the pod's
    template:
      spec:
        nodeSelector: { role: gpu-worker }
        containers:
          - name: ray-worker
            image: "${DLC_IMAGE}"  # the DLC as published; same tag as the head
            env:
              - { name: NCCL_DEBUG, value: "INFO" }  # print the chosen transport
              - { name: NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME, value: "eth0" }  # pod network on EKS
              - { name: FI_PROVIDER, value: "efa" }  # libfabric: EFA, nothing else
              - { name: NCCL_NET_PLUGIN, value: "ofi" }  # load the AWS OFI plugin
            resources:
              limits: { nvidia.com/gpu: "${GPUS_PER_NODE}", vpc.amazonaws.com/efa: "1",
                cpu: "40", memory: 160Gi }
              requests: { nvidia.com/gpu: "${GPUS_PER_NODE}", vpc.amazonaws.com/efa: "1",
                cpu: "40", memory: 160Gi }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Read it as three decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Which image:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;${DLC_IMAGE}&lt;/code&gt;, unmodified, for both head and workers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Which hardware:&lt;/strong&gt; four GPUs and the node’s single EFA interface per pod. Requesting &lt;code&gt;vpc.amazonaws.com/efa&lt;/code&gt; is what makes the EFA device plugin mount the interface into the container. Without it, NCCL can’t see EFA and falls back to sockets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Which transport:&lt;/strong&gt; four environment variables, so nothing is left to auto-detection.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two smaller details keep a multi-node job stable. First, requests should equal limits, which places the workers in Kubernetes’ Guaranteed Quality of Service (QoS) class. This matters because a single throttled or evicted rank stalls the other seven. Second, &lt;code&gt;num-gpus&lt;/code&gt; is derived from the same variable as the GPU request, so Ray’s view of capacity always matches Kubernetes’.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="bringing-it-up-one-command-for-the-infrastructure-then-the-job"&gt;Bringing it up: One command for the infrastructure, then the job&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configuration lives in one place, &lt;code&gt;scripts/env.sh&lt;/code&gt;: cluster name, Region, Kubernetes version, node types and counts, the image, pinned versions of KubeRay and both device plugins, and the training parameters. Export any of them to change it for a run. All five scripts are idempotent and safe to re-run. &lt;code&gt;deploy_cluster.sh&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;deploy_node_group.sh&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;deploy_ray_train_job.sh&lt;/code&gt; each prompt before creating or touching cluster resources. &lt;code&gt;install_gpu_plugins.sh&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;install_kuberay.sh&lt;/code&gt; are reconcilers and run straight through.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd training/ray-train/scripts
source ./env.sh&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;./deploy_all.sh&lt;/code&gt; – Finds where the capacity actually is, then builds the infrastructure there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;g6.12xlarge capacity shifts between Regions and Availability Zones day to day, so this is the starting point rather than a Region guessed up front in &lt;code&gt;env.sh&lt;/code&gt;. It first runs &lt;code&gt;find_gpu_capacity.sh&lt;/code&gt;, which checks, per candidate Region: whether g6.12xlarge is offered in each Availability Zone, whether the account’s On-Demand G/VT-family vCPU quota has room for the requested node count, and the EC2 Spot placement score as a capacity signal. With &lt;code&gt;--probe&lt;/code&gt;, which &lt;code&gt;deploy_all.sh&lt;/code&gt; passes by default, it gets a definitive answer by creating and immediately canceling a real Capacity Reservation in each candidate zone. It then shows the plan and asks once:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;Proceed with the whole chain without further prompts? (y/N):&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Answering &lt;strong&gt;y&lt;/strong&gt; runs, in order, &lt;code&gt;deploy_cluster.sh&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;deploy_node_group.sh&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;install_gpu_plugins.sh&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;install_kuberay.sh&lt;/code&gt; in the recommended Region, each skipping its own repeat confirmation because this one already asked (&lt;code&gt;-y&lt;/code&gt; skips this prompt too, and &lt;code&gt;--plan&lt;/code&gt; shows the plan without creating anything). If the GPU node group can’t launch in the best zone, the script removes it and tries the next zone the probe confirmed. &lt;code&gt;GPU_AZ&lt;/code&gt; is also cross-checked against the cluster’s actual private subnets, not only where g6.12xlarge happens to be offered, so the GPU node group can’t end up pinned to an Availability Zone the cluster has no subnet in. The chosen Region and zone are saved to &lt;code&gt;scripts/.placement.env&lt;/code&gt;, which every other script reads, so later commands target the same place without re-exporting anything. Together the four steps take 20 to 30 minutes on a fresh Region. An existing cluster or node group is detected and reused.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;deploy_cluster.sh&lt;/code&gt; creates the EKS control plane, VPC, OpenID Connect (OIDC) provider, core add-ons, and the m7i.xlarge system node group, choosing subnets in zones that actually offer g6.12xlarge. &lt;code&gt;deploy_node_group.sh&lt;/code&gt; adds the two g6.12xlarge instances with EFA turned on, labeled &lt;code&gt;role=gpu-worker&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;nvidia.com/gpu.present=true&lt;/code&gt;. The second label is one that teams often overlook. Without it, the NVIDIA device plugin chart’s DaemonSet runs on zero nodes, with no error pointing at the cause. &lt;code&gt;install_gpu_plugins.sh&lt;/code&gt; makes sure both device plugins are present. Recent eksctl bundles them into an EFA-enabled node group, so on a fresh cluster it finds them and skips. Otherwise it installs them at pinned chart versions. Either way it verifies &lt;code&gt;nvidia.com/gpu&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;vpc.amazonaws.com/efa&lt;/code&gt; are allocatable on every GPU node rather than trusting a successful Helm release. &lt;code&gt;install_kuberay.sh&lt;/code&gt; installs the KubeRay operator onto the system node, keeping it off the GPU instances.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What &lt;code&gt;deploy_all.sh&lt;/code&gt; does not do by default is run the training job. That stays a separate step you can re-run as often as you like, because it is a real job run rather than infrastructure, and a failed job then never sends you back through cluster setup. Pass &lt;code&gt;--train&lt;/code&gt; to chain it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;./deploy_ray_train_job.sh&lt;/code&gt; &lt;strong&gt;— the RayCluster, the job, and the checks.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Renders the manifest with &lt;code&gt;envsubst&lt;/code&gt; and applies it. It then re-reads each GPU node’s allocatable resources before waiting on any pod: if a device plugin didn’t come up, you get a clear error here rather than a pod that sits in Pending until a timeout. Once the head is Ready and both workers are Ready, it copies &lt;code&gt;code/&lt;/code&gt; onto the head and, through &lt;code&gt;kubectl exec&lt;/code&gt;, submits from inside it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ray job submit --address http://localhost:8265 --working-dir /tmp/ray-train-code \
python3 train.py --model_id Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The submit call streams logs from all eight ranks and returns when the job does. The script then inspects which transport NCCL used and exits with an error if the job failed or EFA wasn’t in the path, which also makes it usable as a continuous integration (CI) gate. &lt;code&gt;./deploy_ray_train_job.sh status&lt;/code&gt; prints the RayCluster state, which instance hosts each pod, and GPU capacity. A correctly provisioned cluster looks like this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;$ kubectl get nodes -l role=gpu-worker \
-o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,GPU:.status.allocatable.nvidia\.com/gpu,EFA:.status.allocatable.vpc\.amazonaws\.com/efa'
NAME               GPU   EFA
ip-192-168-70-51   4     1
ip-192-168-92-26   4     1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22002-3-1.png" alt="One FSDP step showing weights gathered in the forward and backward passes and gradients reduce-scattered, with traffic between instances taking the NCCL, AWS OFI NCCL plugin, libfabric, and EFA path" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: One FSDP step on this cluster. Weights are gathered in the forward and backward passes and gradients are reduce-scattered. Traffic between the instances takes the path the DLC ships, NCCL → AWS OFI NCCL plugin → libfabric → EFA, or falls back to TCP sockets when that path is missing.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="adapting-it-to-your-workload"&gt;Adapting it to your workload&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the image is used as published and the job learns its scale from the cluster, most changes touch exactly one file:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;You want to change&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Where&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No changes needed to&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;MODEL_ID&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code, manifest, image&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Steps, sequence length, batch size&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;STEPS&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;SEQ_LEN&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;BATCH_SIZE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code, manifest, image&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The training loop itself&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Body of &lt;code&gt;train_func&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Trainer, manifest, image, scripts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;An additional Python package&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;--runtime-env-json&lt;/code&gt; pip list&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Image&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A pinned DLC release&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;DLC_IMAGE&lt;/code&gt; to a versioned tag&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Everything else&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The GPU instance family&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GPU_NODE_TYPE&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;GPUS_PER_NODE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code, image&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;How many GPU instances&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GPU_NODE_COUNT&lt;/code&gt;, plus &lt;code&gt;replicas&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;minReplicas&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;maxReplicas&lt;/code&gt; in the manifest&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Code (it counts the GPUs)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Region or zone&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;REGION&lt;/code&gt;, optionally &lt;code&gt;GPU_AZ&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Everything else&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Variables in the table live in &lt;code&gt;env.sh&lt;/code&gt; unless stated otherwise. When switching instance families, &lt;code&gt;GPUS_PER_NODE&lt;/code&gt; has to match the hardware, and the family needs EFA support plus a place on the EFA device plugin’s list of allowed instance types. The plugin version pinned here is known to include g6.12xlarge. Moving to p5 or another GPUDirect-capable family needs no change to the image or the training code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="tearing-it-down"&gt;Tearing it down&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The GPU instances are the expensive part, at around $9.50 an hour together with the control plane and NAT Gateway. Remove things in the opposite order to how they were created:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./delete_ray_train_job.sh        # RayCluster
./install_kuberay.sh cleanup     # KubeRay operator
./install_gpu_plugins.sh cleanup # NVIDIA and EFA device plugins
./delete_node_group.sh           # GPU instances
./delete_cluster.sh              # EKS cluster, VPC and system node&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-to-go-next"&gt;Where to go next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two instances and eight GPUs ran a sharded fine-tune on EKS with a container nobody built. The Ray Train DLC supplied a tested stack from CUDA through EFA. KubeRay started it as head and workers from one tag. Ray Train placed the workers and formed the process group. Everything that varied, from hardware to transport to Python packages, was set from outside the image. The EFA path the image ships engaged with four environment variables and doubled throughput over TCP on unchanged code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Natural next moves:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Replace the random batches with a real dataset, and checkpoint to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon FSx for Lustre.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Pin &lt;code&gt;DLC_IMAGE&lt;/code&gt; to a versioned tag for reproducible runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on &lt;code&gt;FailureConfig&lt;/code&gt; so long runs survive a lost instance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Move to a larger model on a GPUDirect RDMA family such as p5. The image and code stay the same.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
