		<item>
		<title>Accelerating software delivery with agentic QA automation using Amazon Nova Act – Part 3</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-software-delivery-with-agentic-qa-automation-using-amazon-nova-act-part-3/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vinicius Pedroni]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 28 Oct 2026 18:12:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Nova]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e072f14329aeef9ebf20997fc4d53f2767473ad7</guid>

					<description>As QA test automation spreads across an organization, organizing tests and controlling who owns them becomes the real work. Part 3 of the QA Studio series on Amazon Nova Act adds applications that organize tests and report health metrics, plus team-based user management that controls who can see and change what.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;As quality assurance (QA) automation adoption grows inside an organization, a new problem appears that has nothing to do with writing tests. A platform team stands up test automation, then a second team wants to join, then a dozen more. Soon hundreds of test cases belong to different products and squads, and everyone can see everyone else’s tests. No one can answer a basic question at a glance: is this application’s test coverage healthy right now? Test authoring stops being the bottleneck. Organizing tests and controlling who can touch them becomes the work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A second, related question follows close behind: who owns this application and its tests, and who is accountable when coverage slips? Without a clear owner, health problems have no one to land on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-software-delivery-with-agentic-qa-automation-using-amazon-nova-act/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;first post&lt;/a&gt;, we introduced QA Studio, a reference solution built with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/agentic-ai/nova-act/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Act&lt;/a&gt;. Teams use it to define tests in natural language and run them through AI-powered visual navigation. In the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-software-delivery-with-agentic-qa-automation-using-amazon-nova-act-part-2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;second post&lt;/a&gt;, we added test suites and a CLI for batch regression testing in continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines. In this post, we cover two capabilities that help QA Studio scale across an organization. The first, &lt;strong&gt;applications&lt;/strong&gt;, organizes tests and reports health metrics. The second, &lt;strong&gt;team-based user management&lt;/strong&gt;, controls who can see and change what.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="organizing-tests-with-applications"&gt;Organizing tests with applications&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An application in QA Studio represents the system under test: a website, a web app, a service front end. It’s the container that gives a collection of tests, a home, and an owner. Use cases (the individual tests from Part 1) and test suites (the regression collections from Part 2) belong to an application, and they inherit its ownership. Instead of one flat list of every test in the deployment, each team works inside the applications they own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creating an application takes a name, a base URL, and an optional set of environments such as development, staging, and production. From the application page, teams create new use cases directly in context, add existing ones, and group them into test suites. This is also where QA Studio reports on the health of the tests it runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-1-1.png" alt="QA Studio application detail page showing pass rate, executions, and failures above the use cases and test suites tabs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The application detail page, showing pass rate, executions, and failures with the use cases and test suites tabs&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="understanding-application-health-with-metrics"&gt;Understanding application health with metrics&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tests produce results. Understanding those results over time is what tells a team whether the development lifecycle for an application is healthy, not only whether a single build is ready for release. QA Studio records the outcome of every execution as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; custom metrics, dimensioned by application and by environment. It turns these metrics into a health view on the application page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The top of the page answers the immediate question with three figures: the pass rate over the selected window, the total number of executions, and the current failure count. Alongside them, QA Studio computes a health score of 0–100. The score starts from the pass rate and subtracts a penalty for flaky tests. That way, an application whose tests pass but flip between pass and fail doesn’t present a falsely perfect picture. A team scanning a portfolio of applications can tell the stable ones from the ones that need attention without opening each result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The metrics tab moves from the current snapshot to trends. Teams choose a 7-day window with hourly granularity or a 30-day window with daily granularity, and QA Studio charts executions, successes, failures, and average duration across that period. Because the underlying metrics carry an environment dimension, teams filter the view to a single environment. For example, they can compare how staging behaves against production for the same set of tests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two views target the tests that cost teams the most time. The flaky tests view ranks use cases by how often they flipped between passing and failing over the last seven days. This surfaces the intermittent failures that erode trust in a suite before they turn into a production miss. The recent failures view lists the latest failed executions for quick triage. Each one links back to the Amazon Nova Act trajectory logs and session recordings described in Part 1. That way, moving from “this test is failing” to “here is exactly what the agent saw” takes one click.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-2-1.png" alt="QA Studio metrics tab with a daily success and failure chart, failure hotspots, and a flaky use case ranking" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The metrics tab: pass rate, executions, and average duration over the selected window, with the daily success-and-failure chart, failure hotspots, and the flaky use case ranking&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="controlling-access-with-teams-and-roles"&gt;Controlling access with teams and roles&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Metrics tell a team how their applications are doing. Team-based user management decides which applications are theirs in the first place. QA Studio organizes access around teams, and an application belongs to exactly one team. Members of that team work with its applications. Everyone else doesn’t see them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every team member holds one of five roles, and each role includes the permissions of the ones below it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Admin&lt;/strong&gt;: Studio-wide access to every team, application, and user, including creating teams and assigning applications. Admin is a studio-wide role rather than a per-team assignment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Team Lead&lt;/strong&gt;: Manages their own team members and creates applications for the team.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Editor&lt;/strong&gt;: Creates and edits use cases and test suites within the team’s applications.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Executor&lt;/strong&gt;: Runs use cases and suites but doesn’t modify them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Viewer&lt;/strong&gt;: Read-only access to the team’s applications and results.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because roles are cumulative, a Team Lead has everything an Editor has, and so on down to Viewer. Access to a use case or a test suite follows the application that contains it. If a member can work with the application, they can work with its tests, bounded by their role. This keeps permission management at the application level instead of on thousands of individual tests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An administrator sees the whole studio. The Users page lists every account with its team and role in one place. An admin can tell who holds Team Lead on which team and who carries Studio-wide Administrator rights. In the reference deployment shown here, four users span two teams: two Team Leads, one Viewer, and one Studio-wide Administrator. The Teams page rolls the same data up by team, showing each team’s member count and application count side by side. An admin can see which teams are expanding before opening any one of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-3-1.png" alt="QA Studio studio-wide Users page listing each account with its team and role, with team and role filters" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The studio-wide Users page: every account with its team and role, filterable by team and by role&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same membership data, rolled up by team, appears on the Teams page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-4-1.png" alt="QA Studio Teams page listing each team with its member count and application count" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The Teams page: each team with its member and application counts&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Switching from teams to applications, the studio-wide Applications list shows every application across all teams.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-5-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-5-1.png" alt="QA Studio studio-wide Applications list showing each application’s base URL, owning team, use-case count, and last execution status" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The studio-wide Applications list: every application with its base URL, owning team, use-case count, and last execution status&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sharing-applications-between-teams"&gt;Sharing applications between teams&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams rarely work in complete isolation. A platform team might own an application that a feature team needs to run against, without handing over the ability to change it. QA Studio supports this with shared access: the owning team grants another team access to an application at a view, execute, or full level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Shared access follows a ceiling rule. A user’s effective permission on a shared application is the lower of their own role and the level of the grant. A Viewer who is given full access to a shared application remains view-only, because their role is the ceiling. This way, an owning team can share widely without granting more than a recipient’s role allows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-a-team-lead-sees"&gt;What a team lead sees&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A team lead works inside a single team rather than the whole studio. Opening My Team presents four tabs that cover everything the lead controls: Members, Applications, Use Cases, and Shared Access. The Members tab lists the people on the team. The lead can change any member’s role inline or add a new one, so day-to-day access management stays with the person closest to the work. In the AWS Team shown here, the lead manages two members, one Team Lead and one Viewer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-6-1.png" alt="Team lead Members tab with an inline role selector for each team member" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The team lead’s Members tab, with inline role assignment for each member&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Applications tab shows only the applications the team owns, each with its test-case count and last-run time. This keeps the lead focused on their own surface rather than the full studio inventory. The Shared Access tab is where cross-team sharing becomes concrete. Here the AWS Team has granted the AMZN Team view access to an application, and the lead can grant, revoke, or change the level of that access. Because the grant was view level, an AMZN Team Editor still sees the shared application read-only: the ceiling rule from the previous section, applied in practice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-7-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-7-1.png" alt="Team lead Applications tab listing only the applications the team owns" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: The team lead’s Applications tab, scoped to the team’s own applications&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same Team Lead’s Shared Access tab records which other teams can reach the team’s applications, and at what level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-8-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/27/ML-21434-8-1.png" alt="Team lead Shared Access tab showing a view-level grant to another team" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: The team lead’s Shared Access tab, showing a view-level grant to another team&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;QA Studio builds team-based access control and metrics on the same serverless foundation described in the earlier posts, adding a few AWS services to the mix:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: Each team maps to a set of Cognito groups, one per role. An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; authorizer resolves a caller’s groups into API scopes and team context on every request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams, memberships, application ownership, and cross-team share grants are stored in the existing single-table design.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/strong&gt;: Every execution emits custom metrics in the QAStudio/Executions namespace, dimensioned by application and environment. The metrics API reads them back to build the health view and trend charts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon API Gateway&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;: The backend enforces access at two layers: the scope a caller’s role grants, and a resource check that confirms the caller’s team owns or has been shared with the application.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because access is derived from Cognito group membership, the OAuth client credentials flow from Part 2 fits the same model. A CI pipeline authenticates as a machine identity with the scopes it needs and no more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Applications, metrics, and team-based access control are part of the QA Studio reference solution, which you deploy in your own AWS account with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK)&lt;/a&gt;. All test data, results, and metrics stay within your account boundaries. After deploying, an administrator creates teams, assigns members and roles, and assigns applications to teams. Each team’s health metrics begin populating as their tests run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For deployment instructions and configuration details, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-qa-studio" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;QA Studio GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; and its documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how QA Studio scales agentic QA automation from individual tests to an organization. Applications give every team a home for their use cases and test suites. They report health through pass rate, trend charts, a flaky-adjusted health score, and failure triage built on Amazon CloudWatch metrics. Team-based user management, backed by Amazon Cognito, keeps each team working within the applications they own, with cross-team sharing bounded by a clear ceiling rule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These capabilities build on the natural language test authoring from Part 1 and the suites and CI/CD integration from Part 2. Together, they help QA automation grow across many teams without losing visibility or control. To try it in your own environment, deploy the reference solution from the QA Studio GitHub repository, create your first team and application, and run a few use cases to watch the metrics take shape. To learn more about the agent behind it, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/agentic-ai/nova-act/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Act product page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Pasquale DeMaio]]></dc:creator>

					<description>AWS has been named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner Magic Quadrant for Contact Center as a Service (CCaaS) for AWS Connect Customer. This recognition marks an important milestone for our team, and it reflects years of work pushing on what's possible in customer experience.&amp;nbsp;</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-142251" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/26/22275-Gartner.png" alt="" width="2893" height="3200"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;I’m proud to share that Amazon Web Services (AWS) has been named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner Magic Quadrant for Contact Center as a Service (CCaaS) for Amazon Connect for the fourth consecutive year. We believe this recognition reflects years of work pushing on what’s possible in customer experience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When we launched Amazon Connect Customer, we set out to evolve how businesses serve their customers: to make personalized, intelligent experiences possible without giving up efficiency, flexibility, or control. That belief still drives how we build today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customer expectations have shifted. People expect you to recognize them, understand what they need, and stay consistent across every channel. They don’t want to repeat themselves. They don’t want to navigate disconnected systems to solve a simple problem. They want interactions that feel natural, relevant, and genuinely helpful. Meeting that takes more than traditional contact center technology, it takes a comprehensive solution that brings together customer context, AI, and the flexibility to adapt. That’s what we’re building with Amazon Connect Customer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer experience without compromise&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What matters most to your customers hasn’t changed: they want to be known, they want their problems fully resolved, and they want to trust you in the moments that matter. Read the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resources/analyst-reports/gartner/global-mq-ardm-26-contact-center-as-a-service/?trk=21a9418a-9a81-4797-8372-7df087f349c5&amp;amp;sc_channel=el" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;2026 Gartner Magic Quadrant&lt;/a&gt; to know why we are recognized.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Experiences that start with knowing the customer. &lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Connect Customer builds a living picture of every customer: who they are, what they’ve been through, what they need next. Every touchpoint draws on that intelligence. Your customer feels known before a single word is exchanged. That’s what personal actually looks like.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resolution that carries all the way through. &lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most of the industry is still optimizing for deflection: getting customers away from humans as fast as possible. We took a different path. Connect Customer is built for the hard things: the disputed claim, the renegotiated mortgage, the disrupted itinerary that needs real action and can’t go wrong. We carry those interactions all the way through to resolution, and we make them feel natural and personal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generative and agentic AI push this further: understanding what a customer needs and acting on it. Every action is auditable. Humans step in when the moment calls for it. You get enterprise scale and the confidence that nothing slips through the cracks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Building for what’s next&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The companies that win the next decade of customer experience won’t be the ones with the most channels or the fastest bot. They’ll be the ones whose customers feel known, whose problems actually get resolved, and whose trust is earned in the moments that matter most. Connect Customer exists to make that real for businesses of every size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;I’m proud of the Connect Customer team for receiving this recognition for the fourth year in a row. Our engineers, product teams, partners, and customers shaped what we’ve built. This is a moment to celebrate how far we’ve come, and a signal of what’s ahead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;span data-contrast="auto"&gt;Every customer is unique. Every interaction should reflect that.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Read the 2026 Gartner Magic Quadrant for Contact Center as a Service:&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/resources/analyst-reports/gartner/global-mq-ardm-26-contact-center-as-a-service/?trk=21a9418a-9a81-4797-8372-7df087f349c5&amp;amp;sc_channel=el" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Gartner, Magic Quadrant for Contact Center as a Service, 19 October 2026, Drew Kraus Et Al&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Announcing SOCI-indexed images for faster SageMaker AI jobs</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/announcing-soci-indexed-images-for-faster-sagemaker-ai-jobs/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kanwaljit Khurmi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 27 Oct 2026 17:00:39 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4fef4e2052ceafe5be77506f74414eb6efbd5cd7</guid>

					<description>Seekable OCI (SOCI) indexing for Amazon SageMaker AI training and processing jobs takes full container image download off the critical path. SageMaker AI lazy-loads your image to start containers in seconds instead of minutes, with no changes to your job definition. See the benchmarks and how to adopt it.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Every minute your deep learning container image spends downloading is a minute you pay for and a minute your team waits. Container image download is pure overhead: billable job time that produces no model progress. For modern deep learning images, that overhead is several minutes on every single job. Today, we’re excited to announce &lt;em&gt;Seekable OCI (SOCI) indexing for Amazon SageMaker AI Training and Amazon SageMaker AI processing jobs&lt;/em&gt;, which takes full container image download off the critical path of every job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With SOCI indexing, SageMaker AI lazy-loads your container image. It fetches only the files needed to start the container and streams the rest in the background as your code runs, so your job begins in seconds instead of minutes. You adopt it by pointing to a pre-indexed AWS Deep Learning Container (DLC) or adding an index to your own image. Neither approach requires changes to your job definition, entry point, or data channels. The result is faster job starts, lower job cost, and more time spent building models rather than waiting on infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SOCI-indexed images start Amazon SageMaker AI training containers in seconds, with no impact on training performance. As we show in the &lt;em&gt;What we measured&lt;/em&gt; section, this reduced GPU idle time during image pulls by up to 90 percent in our benchmarks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to adopt SOCI indexing: what it is, the results we measured, and how to turn it on for your own jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;The challenge&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI training and processing jobs run inside container images stored in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR). Before a job can execute your code, the service downloads the entire image to the instance. It decompresses and writes every layer to disk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For small images this takes seconds. But deep learning images routinely exceed 10 GB or more. That download adds minutes to every job, and you pay for it as job time. The impact is largest exactly where iteration speed matters most: hyperparameter tuning sweeps for hundreds of jobs, quick experiments, and short processing jobs, where image download can dominate total runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;How SOCI indexing works&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/soci-snapshotter" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Seekable OCI (SOCI)&lt;/a&gt; is an open source technology that lazy loads container images. Instead of downloading the whole image before starting, the runtime uses a SOCI index to fetch only the files needed to launch the container immediately. It then streams the rest in the background as your code accesses it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A SOCI index is metadata that maps every file inside your image. You store it in Amazon ECR as an OCI-compliant artifact linked to your original image. Because the index is a separate artifact, your image digest and any existing signatures remain valid.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sagemaker-ai-checks-for-an-index-automatically"&gt;SageMaker AI checks for an index automatically&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you create a training or processing job, SageMaker AI checks whether the image you specified has a SOCI index and behaves accordingly:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What happens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The image includes a SOCI index&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SageMaker AI lazy-loads the image and starts your container before the full download completes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The image has no SOCI index&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Standard download, no change in behavior&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The index can’t be read, or lazy loading doesn’t succeed&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The job falls back to a standard download and continues running&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because jobs always fall back to a standard download when needed, adding a SOCI index never prevents your job from running. You only gain faster startup. You never risk breaking an existing workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;What we measured&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We pulled the same container images two ways on real SageMaker AI training jobs: standard download and SOCI-indexed lazy loading. We then ran full training jobs from both to measure the effect on training speed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="setup"&gt;Setup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Eight images&lt;/strong&gt;, 1.8–35.4 GB compressed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPU and CPU instances&lt;/strong&gt; ml.g4dn, ml.g5, ml.g6, ml.g6e, ml.g7, ml.g7e, ml.p4d, ml.p5, and ml.m7i, from xlarge to 48xlarge, in the US West (Oregon) Region (us-west-2) and the US East (N. Virginia) Region (us-east-1).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Download time&lt;/strong&gt; measured from the start of the image pull until the container is ready to run. All figures are medians of repeated runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="result-1-download-time-drops-from-minutes-to-seconds-at-every-image-size"&gt;Result 1: Download time drops from minutes to seconds at every image size&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Image size (compressed)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Standard download&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lazy loading&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improvement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1.8 GB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;48 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3-11 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~8x&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;13.2 GB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3 min 42 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~6 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~34x&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;35.4 GB&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7 min 17 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3-11 s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~38x&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following chart compares container image download time for a standard Docker pull against SOCI lazy loading across eight image sizes, from 1.8 GB to 35.4 GB.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each image size shows two bars. The Docker download time bar (blue) grows steadily with image size, from 48 seconds at 1.8 GB to 7 minutes 17 seconds at 35.4 GB.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A speedup label above each pair shows the ratio of Docker time to SOCI time. Speedups range from approximately 10x for the smallest image to over 35x for the largest.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-1-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-1-2.png" alt="Bar chart comparing Docker pull and SOCI lazy-loading download times across eight image sizes, with speedups from about 10x to over 35x" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Container image download time, standard Docker pull compared to SOCI lazy loading, across eight image sizes&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard download time grows with image size because every file is decompressed and written. Lazy loading stayed at 3–11 seconds regardless of size, an 8x–46x improvement. The bigger the image, the more minutes you save.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="result-2-the-saving-holds-on-every-instance-type-and-size"&gt;Result 2: The saving holds on every instance type and size&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running the 13.2 GB image across 13 instance type/size combinations, standard download ranged from 3 min 16 s to 4 min 20 s, while lazy loading held at 6-8 s. That is about 34x faster on every configuration. You don’t need to change instance type or size to benefit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-2-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-2-2.png" alt="Chart comparing standard download and SOCI lazy loading for a 13.2 GB image across 13 instance type and size combinations, about 34x faster on each" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Download time for a 13.2 GB image across 13 instance type and size combinations&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="result-3-training-performance-is-unaffected-beyond-a-first-epoch-penalty"&gt;Result 3: Training performance is unaffected beyond a first-epoch penalty&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran two kinds of full training jobs from the Docker-pulled and from the SOCI-indexed image and compared every epoch:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Image classification on ResNet-50 on &lt;a href="https://github.com/fastai/imagenette" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Imagenette&lt;/a&gt; (9,469 images) 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Container images: PyTorch (13.2 GB DLC) and TensorFlow (9.9 GB DLC)&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Instances: ml.p5.48xlarge, ml.p4d.24xlarge, ml.g7e.48xlarge, ml.g7.48xlarge, ml.g6e.12xlarge, ml.g6e.24xlarge, and ml.g6e.48xlarge.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Large language model (LLM) fine-tuning: full-parameter supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen2.5-0.5B&lt;/a&gt; on &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/yahma/alpaca-cleaned" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;alpaca-cleaned&lt;/a&gt; (51,760 examples) with the Hugging Face Trainer. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Container image: 9.4 GB Hugging Face PyTorch DLC.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Instances: ml.p5.48xlarge, ml.p4d.24xlarge, ml.g6.48xlarge.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following chart breaks job startup into image download time and first training epoch time, comparing a standard Docker pull with a SOCI-indexed image across GPU and CPU configurations for both workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-3-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-21882-3-2.png" alt="Chart breaking job startup into image download time and first training epoch time, comparing a standard Docker pull with a SOCI-indexed image across GPU and CPU configurations" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Job startup split into image download time and first training epoch time, standard Docker pull compared to a SOCI-indexed image&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;From the second epoch on, time per epoch is the same (within run-to-run variation) on every instance, for both workloads.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The first epoch takes 3–16 s longer from the lazy-loaded image, while files the framework touches for the first time are fetched. This first-epoch cost is the same on every instance size.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Net: each job finished 1-6 min sooner. That is the download saving minus the one-time first-epoch cost, a fixed saving per job.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="test-this-on-your-own-workload"&gt;Test this on your own workload&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These numbers are from the two workloads described earlier. The first-epoch cost depends on your image and what your code touches at startup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Lazy loading wins when the first training step needs only a fraction of the image, the rest streams in while you train.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If your entry point reads most of the image before training starts (for example, scanning, checksumming, or preloading its contents), the first epoch waits for roughly the same bytes a full pull would have fetched. The net improvement would be small.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Front-loaded starts still came out ahead in our tests: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;torch.compile and a just-in-time (JIT) built CUDA extension added 7-15 s to the first epoch (net saving still 3 min 25 s–3 min 56 s)&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;A 6 GB model baked into the image streamed its weights at load time, adding 2-3.5 min, but the overall job still finished 2-2.5 mins sooner on the indexed image.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Getting started&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can adopt SOCI indexing two ways: use a pre-indexed AWS Deep Learning Container or create an index for your own custom image.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="option-1-use-a-pre-indexed-aws-deep-learning-container"&gt;Option 1: Use a pre-indexed AWS Deep Learning Container&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS publishes SOCI-indexed variants of several popular Deep Learning Containers, identified by the -soci suffix on the image tag:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;763104351884.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/pytorch-training:2.9-gpu-py312-cu130-ubuntu22.04-sagemaker-v1-soci&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pre-indexed variants are available for PyTorch Training, TensorFlow Training, vLLM, and SGLang, including arm64 variants for some frameworks. To adopt lazy loading, update the image tag in your job definition to the -soci variant. Browse the full list in the &lt;a href="https://gallery.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers Gallery&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note: &lt;/strong&gt;If you build your own image FROM a DLC base image, you must create a SOCI index for your final image. The index from the base image does not carry forward to derived images.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="option-2-create-a-soci-index-for-your-own-image"&gt;Option 2: Create a SOCI index for your own image&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To index a custom image, you need a Linux host (such as an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance running Amazon Linux 2023) with &lt;a href="https://containerd.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;containerd&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/soci-snapshotter" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SOCI snapshotter&lt;/a&gt;, and either &lt;a href="https://runfinch.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Finch&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://github.com/containerd/nerdctl" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;nerdctl&lt;/a&gt; installed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Set your environment variables&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ACCOUNT_ID="111122223333"
REGION="us-east-1"
REPOSITORY_NAME="my-training-image"
ORIGINAL_IMAGE_TAG="v1.0"
SOCI_IMAGE_TAG="v1.0-soci"
REGISTRY="$ACCOUNT_ID.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Install the required tools&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;sudo dnf install -y containerd fuse soci-snapshotter runfinch-finch
sudo systemctl start soci-snapshotter
sudo systemctl restart containerd&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authenticate to Amazon ECR&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws ecr get-login-password --region "$REGION" \
  | sudo nerdctl login -u AWS --password-stdin "$REGISTRY"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pull your original image&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;sudo nerdctl pull "$REGISTRY/$REPOSITORY_NAME:$ORIGINAL_IMAGE_TAG"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create the SOCI-indexed image&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;sudo nerdctl image convert --soci \
  "$REGISTRY/$REPOSITORY_NAME:$ORIGINAL_IMAGE_TAG" \
  "$REGISTRY/$REPOSITORY_NAME:$SOCI_IMAGE_TAG"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Push the indexed image&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;sudo nerdctl push --platform linux/amd64 \
  "$REGISTRY/$REPOSITORY_NAME:$SOCI_IMAGE_TAG"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This creates two artifacts in your repository: the SOCI index and an OCI-compliant image index manifest linking your image to the index. Your original image and its digest remain unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="automate-index-creation-in-cicd"&gt;Automate index creation in CI/CD&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To generate indexes automatically on every push, deploy the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-ia/cfn-ecr-aws-soci-index-builder" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CFN ECR SOCI Index Builder&lt;/a&gt;. It uses an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/latest/userguide/eb-what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt; rule to detect Amazon ECR image push events and build the index. No changes to your build pipeline are required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Use the indexed image in your SageMaker AI job&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reference your SOCI-indexed image exactly as you would any other image. No API changes are needed. Only the tag differs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="training-example-sagemaker-python-sdk"&gt;Training example (SageMaker Python SDK)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.train import ModelTrainer
from sagemaker.train.configs import Compute

trainer = ModelTrainer(
    training_image="111122223333.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/my-training-image:v1.0-soci",
    role="arn:aws:iam::111122223333:role/SageMakerExecutionRole",
    compute=Compute(
        instance_type="ml.g5.12xlarge",
        instance_count=2,
    ),
)

trainer.train()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="verify-that-your-image-includes-a-soci-index"&gt;Verify that your image includes a SOCI index&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before launching, confirm your tag resolves to an indexed image:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws ecr batch-get-image \
  --repository-name "$REPOSITORY_NAME" \
  --image-ids imageTag="$SOCI_IMAGE_TAG" \
  --accepted-media-types \
  "application/vnd.oci.image.index.v1+json" \
  --query 'images[0].imageManifest' --output text \
  | jq '.manifests[] | {mediaType, artifactType}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Look for a child manifest whose artifactType begins with application/vnd.amazon.soci.index. alongside your platform manifest. If it isn’t there, the tag does not point to an indexed image.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Things to keep in mind&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon ECR private repositories only.&lt;/strong&gt; Images in other registries, or pulled through a private Docker registry (TrainingRepositoryAccessMode set to Vpc), are not eligible for lazy loading.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improvement scales with image size.&lt;/strong&gt; Images smaller than roughly 4 GB see relatively small improvements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check for Docker-specific assumptions.&lt;/strong&gt; SOCI-indexed images run on a containerd-based runtime. Review entry points and setup scripts for code that calls the Docker CLI, reads /var/run/docker.sock, or derives identifiers from cgroup paths. SageMaker AI mounts a /.dockerenv marker for backward compatibility, but avoid adding new dependencies on it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;SOCI indexing for SageMaker AI jobs is available across all AWS Regions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With SOCI indexing, you can minimize container image download time from your SageMaker AI training and processing jobs. Add a SOCI index to your images, or switch to a pre-indexed AWS Deep Learning Container. Either way, you cut startup time from minutes to seconds, reduce total job cost, and accelerate your machine learning (ML) iteration cycle. And your existing job definitions, API calls, and data pipelines stay exactly the same.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; and start using SOCI indexing, explore the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pick a SOCI-indexed Deep Learning Container.&lt;/strong&gt; Browse the &lt;a href="https://gallery.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deep Learning Containers Gallery&lt;/a&gt; and append -soci to your image tag. For framework-specific guidance, see &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/tutorials/SOCI/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SOCI index for AWS Deep Learning Containers&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Index your own custom image.&lt;/strong&gt; Follow the step-by-step walkthrough in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/soci-indexing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;, or refer to the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/soci-snapshotter/blob/main/docs/getting-started.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SOCI snapshotter getting-started guide&lt;/a&gt; for advanced options.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automate indexing in your CI/CD pipeline.&lt;/strong&gt; Deploy the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-ia/cfn-ecr-aws-soci-index-builder" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CFN ECR SOCI Index Builder&lt;/a&gt; to generate indexes automatically on every image push. No pipeline changes are required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Test locally with Finch.&lt;/strong&gt; Use the &lt;a href="https://runfinch.com/docs/container-images/lazy-loading/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Finch lazy-loading walkthrough&lt;/a&gt; to validate your image on a containerd-based runtime before you submit a job.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dive deeper into the technology.&lt;/strong&gt; Read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reducing-container-cold-start-times-using-soci-index-on-dlami-and-dlc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reducing container cold start times using SOCI index on DLAMI and DLC&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Machine Learning Blog for benchmarks, or explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/containers/under-the-hood-lazy-loading-container-images-with-seekable-oci-and-aws-fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Under the hood: Lazy loading container images with Seekable OCI and AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Containers Blog to understand the lazy-loading mechanism in detail.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Faster container startup is only one image tag away. Try it on your next SageMaker AI job and see the difference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Fix evaluation bias to unlock better models on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fix-evaluation-bias-to-unlock-better-models-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Khaled Thabit]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 27 Oct 2026 16:53:02 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2d980147c58e5ff4accadbbed4d31bf94e438c61</guid>

					<description>Property Finder found that 96.8% of their vision-AI evaluation labels were inherited from the incumbent model, hiding its true accuracy. See how they built unbiased ground truth, applied evolutionary prompt optimization, and selected a more accurate, cost-effective model on Amazon Bedrock, cutting cost by 86% across 11 image-quality attributes.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post was co-authored with Khaled Thabit, Meriem Boughrara, and Mohamed Desouky from Property Finder.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engineering team at &lt;a href="https://www.propertyfinder.ae/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Property Finder&lt;/a&gt;, a digital real estate application, discovered that 96.8 percent of their evaluation labels had been inherited directly from a single model’s predictions rather than verified by human experts. Their ground truth was systematically skewed toward the incumbent model, making every alternative appear to underperform. The labels also had errors, but the bias was what blocked objective model comparison. They uncovered this when migrating their vision AI workload to a more cost-effective option on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder processes tens of thousands of property images every day across the Middle East and North Africa (MENA). Their system evaluates 11 quality attributes per image. These cover watermark detection, room classification, furnishing status, photo quality, and construction indicators. That’s thousands of new property listings with an average of 15 images each. All need quality assessment before going live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder needed a more cost-effective option. But measured against those labels, any alternative was at a disadvantage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hardest part was not finding a better model. It was discovering that their evaluation methodology itself was the blocker.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we share how Property Finder identified evaluation bias, built unbiased ground truth, and applied evolutionary prompt optimization to select the right model on Amazon Bedrock, achieving higher accuracy at 86 percent lower cost. We walk through the methodology and iteration examples, with lessons you can apply to your own workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure illustrates the prompt optimization loop that Property Finder and the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center team used to iteratively improve classification accuracy on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/26/ML-21124-1.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/26/ML-21124-1.jpg" alt="Diagram of the prompt optimization loop: a property image goes to Amazon Bedrock, the predicted attributes are scored with Micro F1 against the expert-annotated development set, error analysis informs the next prompt version, and the loop repeats from v0 to v27." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Prompt optimization loop architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The diagram shows the iterative prompt optimization cycle. A property image is processed by Amazon Bedrock, which outputs the predicted attributes in JSON format. During optimization, these predictions are scored with Micro F1 against the 256-image expert-annotated development set. Error analysis then inspects failed images and identifies visual patterns, which inform the next prompt version. The loop ran from v0 to v27. AI assistants helped refine the prompts, and later versions used automated optimization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="your-evaluation-might-be-lying"&gt;Your evaluation might be lying&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many teams encounter this scenario. You want to compare vision models, so you build an evaluation dataset. The most common way to get labels is to run your current model and treat its outputs as ground truth. The model has been running in production and nobody complained, so the labels must be good enough.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;But this creates a circular evaluation where any alternative gets judged by how closely it matches the incumbent’s behavior, not by actual correctness.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder’s evaluation dataset had exactly this problem. Of their labeled samples, 96.8 percent came directly from the predictions of the existing vision language model (VLM) rather than independent human verification. Any alternative model was structurally disadvantaged before a single image was even processed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hidden errors confirmed the severity. Watermark text extraction had character-level mistakes marked as correct, including hallucinated brand names and missing characters. Photo classification forced single labels onto images containing multiple room types.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the remaining 3.2 percent of human-labeled samples, annotators had introduced categories outside the original specification, like &lt;em&gt;Office&lt;/em&gt; appearing where no such label existed in the prompt. A model comparison loses validity when the answer key was written by the model you are trying to replace.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-ground-truth-you-can-trust"&gt;Building ground truth you can trust&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To break the cycle, you need human experts with real domain knowledge doing fresh annotations independently. No reference to previous model outputs. No access to what the incumbent predicted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="dataset-scope"&gt;Dataset scope&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder’s team hand-annotated 356 UAE images and a separate set of Egypt images. The team selected these from unlabeled production listings across representative industry segments. This post reports results on the UAE images. The Egypt images served as a cross-regional check on 230 images and are not reported here. The first 256 UAE images to be annotated formed the development set for tuning. The evaluation set for the final model comparison uses all 356, adding 100 images annotated after the development runs. A set of 356 images is roughly sufficient to detect moderate differences between models. Rare classes within individual attributes have fewer examples, so smaller differences on those attributes might not be detectable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="expert-annotation"&gt;Expert annotation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A strategy and analytics expert annotated all images. They brought specialized knowledge of real estate listing quality standards. The AWS Generative AI Innovation Center team conducted systematic quality reviews through dedicated clarification sessions. Those reviews settled ambiguous cases and kept the labeling consistent across the dataset.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-label-transition"&gt;Multi-label transition&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Real estate images frequently show multiple rooms through doorways or display overlapping quality characteristics. The switch from single-label to multi-label classification alleviated a major source of artificial ambiguity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hallway photo showing a bedroom through a doorway is genuinely both. After you allow multiple labels, you measure what the model gets right rather than penalizing it for reasonable secondary predictions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hybrid approach could complement this methodology: models from different families surface candidate labels and disagreements, and human experts adjudicate every case, so no model output is used directly as a label. This would reduce the annotation burden for future dataset expansion.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="metrics-selection"&gt;Metrics selection&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Micro F1 served as the primary metric because it weights each prediction equally, which matches the business goal of overall label correctness. Macro F1 was reported alongside it because Micro F1 is dominated by frequent classes and can hide weak performance on rare attributes. Per-label precision and recall helped identify specific failure modes. Per-sample intersection over union (IoU) measured how closely each image’s predicted labels overlapped the expert’s labels. The nine single-label attributes, such as rotation or watermark detection, have one right answer per image. The AWS team also scored them with exact-match accuracy, the share of images where the answer matches the expert label.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prompt-optimization-as-reinforcement-learning"&gt;Prompt optimization as reinforcement learning&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With unbiased evaluation in place, you can start improving. You cannot improve what you cannot measure correctly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The optimization follows a loop analogous to reinforcement learning. Think of each prompt version as an agent, the Micro F1 score as the reward signal, and error analysis as the gradient for improvement. The idea resembles &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19457" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GEPA (Genetic-Pareto reflective prompt evolution)&lt;/a&gt;, where you mutate prompt components, evaluate fitness, select survivors, and iterate. In this engagement, the AWS team refined most versions from error analysis, with AI assistants reviewing failed images and proposing changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://dspy.ai/current/api/optimizers/MIPROv2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MIPROv2&lt;/a&gt;, an automated prompt optimization framework from the &lt;a href="https://dspy.ai/current/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DSPy&lt;/a&gt; library, complemented this manual process for the photo classification task in v27.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="where-it-started"&gt;Where it started&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first prompt (v0) used generic classification instructions, simplified here for illustration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;“Classify this image into one of the following categories: Living Room, Kitchen, Bedroom, Bathroom…”&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Proprietary model A, Property Finder’s production baseline, scored a Micro F1 of 0.55 on the 256-image development set. It used v22, a prompt tuned for its model family. Scores before v21 used the earlier single-label setup and aren’t directly comparable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="iteration-by-iteration"&gt;Iteration by iteration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AWS team tested early versions on several candidate models and added structured output formatting and attribute-specific definitions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The most instructive fix came from image-level error analysis. In the early single-label versions, the model consistently classified rooms visible through doorways as the secondary room type. The AWS Generative AI Innovation Center team added explicit instructions to classify based on the room occupying the majority of the frame. The later switch to multi-label output kept the main room as a label and allowed rooms visible through doorways as additional labels.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Regional customization produced the next gain. In some regions, listing photos carry decorative Arabic text overlays that are not watermarks. Rules for that text removed a whole category of false positives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The v21 prompt switched photo classification and room category to multi-label output and became the shared starting prompt for every model. With it, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-supported.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct&lt;/a&gt; from Meta scored a Micro F1 of 0.75 on the development set. From there, the AWS team tuned each model’s prompt separately. For this model, v24 targeted room size overestimation, multi-label handling, and output format. Then v25, an automatically optimized version, moved the critical tasks first. In v27, MIPROv2 restructured the photo classification task, and the model’s Micro F1 on that task reached 0.76.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="where-it-ended"&gt;Where it ended&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The final prompt, v27_PF, is v27 plus rules that Property Finder added for renders, images with borders or frames, and corridors. Like v27, it keeps the Arabic text rules and output format instructions. A normalization step after each model call handles remaining format differences.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With v27_PF, Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct scored a Micro F1 of 0.80 on the 356-image evaluation set, where proprietary model A scored 0.56 with v22. The evaluation set includes the 256 development images, and both models’ prompts were tuned on those images. These scores are therefore optimistic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The switch from proprietary model A to Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct, each on its own tuned prompt, lifted Micro F1 from 0.55 to 0.81 on the development set. That is a 47 percent relative gain. Prompt tuning alone, on Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct, raised it from 0.75 with the shared v21 prompt to 0.81 with v27.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-from-failed-approaches"&gt;Lessons from failed approaches&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Not everything succeeded. The &lt;em&gt;2-bucket&lt;/em&gt; approach put the watermark attributes and the other attributes in two separate API calls. It brought no meaningful accuracy gain. Boxed-image detection also dropped sharply. Without the watermark attributes in the same prompt, the models mistook watermark overlays for image borders.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generic prompts also failed regardless of which model you ran them on. Real estate imagery has too much domain specificity for a one-size-fits-all instruction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-cost-accuracy-and-speed"&gt;Results: Cost, accuracy, and speed&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AWS Generative AI Innovation Center team evaluated multiple models on Amazon Bedrock with the unbiased evaluation framework. All models started from the shared v21 prompt and were then tuned for their model family on the development set. Proprietary model A got one tuning round, v22. Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct got several, v24 to v27, plus Property Finder’s edge-case rules, so it had more tuning effort behind it. The final comparison against proprietary model A used the 356-image evaluation set. Property Finder selected Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct for their production workload based on accuracy across all 11 attributes and cost efficiency. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table shows the exact-match accuracy that Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct (v27_PF) reached on each single-label attribute, scored on the 356-image evaluation set. It scored 90 percent or higher on six of the nine, and matched or exceeded proprietary model A on all 11 attributes. On photo classification, which can have several labels per image, the two scored the same.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

 &lt;/tbody&gt;

&lt;h3 id="cost-per-1000-images"&gt;Cost per 1,000 images&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder scores tens of thousands of images a day, so per-image cost matters. Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct is a cost-effective open-weight model, and the pipeline gets all attributes for an image in a single call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Estimated cost per 1,000 images&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;$2.35&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost reduction compared with Property Finder’s previous setup&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;86%&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These figures are estimates as of December 2025 in the US East (Ohio) Region. The Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct figure assumes 9,000 input tokens and 200 output tokens per image, from the original two-call cost analysis. A single call with the production prompt carries about 7,500–8,000 input tokens, so this is an upper estimate. The 86 percent compares it with the cost analysis estimate for Property Finder’s previous setup, which used the v21 prompt. The accuracy comparison used v22, which was tuned from v21, so its cost might differ. For current pricing, refer to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inference-speed"&gt;Inference speed&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Property Finder runs this optimized pipeline in production, processing tens of thousands of images daily with Llama 4 Maverick 17B Instruct on Amazon Bedrock. In their own monitoring, Property Finder observed a 40–60 percent reduction in inference latency compared with their previous setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="apply-this-to-your-workloads"&gt;Apply this to your workloads&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start by auditing your evaluation labels. If they came from model predictions rather than human experts, you have the same bias problem Property Finder found.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Build domain-expert ground truth for a few hundred representative samples, and keep a separate test split that you never use for tuning. Adopt multi-label metrics where your classification task involves overlapping attributes. Then iterate on prompts using image error analysis, treating each iteration as a generation in an evolutionary optimization loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you deploy a pipeline like this, add responsible AI controls. For example, you can use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; to enforce content policies and filter inappropriate model outputs. The attribute predictions in this pipeline don’t come with a calibrated confidence score, so use a practical signal to choose images for human review. Model disagreement is one such signal. When two models give different answers for an attribute, the image is often ambiguous. A second model on every image adds cost, so you can start with a sample. Check how often the signal catches real errors on your development set before you rely on it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walked through how Property Finder identified evaluation bias, built unbiased ground truth, and applied evolutionary prompt optimization to achieve better accuracy at lower cost through model selection on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three elements needed to come together for this result: unbiased evaluation to measure honestly, the flexibility to evaluate and switch between model families without re-platforming, and carefully evolved prompts tailored to the domain. Amazon Bedrock provided the foundation. It gave Property Finder access to multiple model families through a single API, inherited AWS governance and security controls, and the ability to adopt new models as they become available without changing their infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with model evaluation and selection on Amazon Bedrock, visit the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt;. If you process vision workloads at scale and want help building an unbiased evaluation framework, reach out to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/innovation-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Share your experiences with model evaluation and prompt optimization in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="acknowledgments"&gt;Acknowledgments&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Additional contributors from AWS include Yousuf Albannai, Amine Sehili, Thomas Ounnas, Aris Tsakpinis, Rafik Soliman, Nikhil Ulhas Pandit, and Sneh Sharma.&lt;/p&gt;
