&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Exowatt beats frontier models on accuracy with SageMaker serverless model customization</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/exowatt-beats-frontier-models-on-accuracy-with-sagemaker-serverless-model-customization/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Purna Sanyal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 27 Oct 2026 16:50:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">1e1c377670e004fec0787c3710e67b118ba3c87f</guid>

					<description>In this post, we show how Exowatt used Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization to build a repeatable AI pipeline that produces task-specific fine-tuned models for two deployment targets, edge and cloud, achieving higher accuracy than frontier models at up to 49x lower cost.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Exowatt builds modular, dispatchable solar systems that deliver reliable power for energy-intensive applications such as AI data centers. Its flagship product, the Exowatt P3, captures solar energy through optical lenses, stores it as heat in a long-duration thermal battery, and dispatches electricity on demand through a heat engine. Unlike grid-tied photovoltaic solar systems, P3 powers data centers directly behind the meter, helping data centers come online without waiting in multi-year grid interconnection queues. Exowatt builds a fleet of AI agents that monitor and triage live alarms and support operational decisions for solar systems, often at the edge, with limited or no connectivity. As its fleet scales from test benches toward manufacturing and thousands of deployed field units, the intelligence layer that operates these systems must scale with it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show how Exowatt used Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization to build a repeatable AI pipeline. The pipeline produces task-specific fine-tuned models for two deployment targets, edge and cloud, achieving higher accuracy than frontier models at up to 49× lower cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two models described here are the first outputs of that pipeline rather than the end goal. The more durable result of the engagement was the pipeline itself: a repeatable path from raw operational data to a certified training set, a fine-tuned model, and an evaluated deployment artifact. Exowatt can now run this path in days rather than months. Alarm triage and energy-market analysis are two of many narrow, high-value tasks where the same approach applies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-ai-on-physical-energy-infrastructure"&gt;The challenge: AI on physical energy infrastructure&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt’s energy management system (EMS) runs across a growing fleet of operational sites. Each site continuously produces alarms: a hardware component operating out of spec, a dropped connection, an out-of-range voltage reading, and similar events. As the fleet scales toward thousands of field units, a human operator can’t monitor every site in real time. Exowatt needed an AI agent that could read the alarm state on each site and take or recommend the correct next action autonomously and reliably.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A frontier model couldn’t solve this problem for three reasons:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The task is narrow and proprietary. Alarm triage on Exowatt’s EMS depends on their specific hardware, data, and operating logic. A general-purpose frontier model isn’t specialized for it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;It must run at the edge, with no GPU and often no internet. Many sites have limited or intermittent connectivity, so an agent that depends on a cloud call to a frontier model leaves the operator without coverage when the connection drops.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Cost doesn’t scale at fleet level. Routing every operational decision to a frontier model across thousands of sites is not economical for a decision that repeats continuously.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is where Exowatt turned to model customization. Exowatt’s initial fine-tuning workflow relied on selecting an open-weight model, building a container, and creating a custom training recipe before any experiment could run. This was a major blocker for model experimentation. Additionally, they had to decide GPU hardware requirements of model fine-tuning. As the team increased the pace of experimentation, each run first required choosing a specific GPU instance type and then waiting for that self-selected capacity to become available. When a chosen instance type was unavailable, this could delay the start of a run or block it entirely.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;They needed a model small enough to run on a no-GPU edge controller, yet accurate enough compared to the frontier model on the specific task to act autonomously. Fine-tuning closed that gap: a compact, task-specialized model that is both deployable in the cloud and at the edge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt addressed these challenges by adopting a cloud-native, serverless model customization approach on Amazon SageMaker AI. The same training pipeline produces fine-tuned models for two deployment targets: the edge and the cloud.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt implemented this approach on the Qwen3-1.7B model for the first use case, edge alarm triage, then applied the same approach to the Qwen3-14B model for the second use case, cloud energy-market analysis. The company plans to reuse this solution to build smaller, task-specific models across the fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22072-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-22072-1.png" alt="Exowatt fine-tuning and deployment architecture across build, cloud-serve, and edge-serve stages" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Exowatt fine-tuning and deployment pipeline, spanning the build, cloud-serving, and edge-serving stages&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following seven AWS services power the AI pipeline, with no instances, clusters, or endpoints to manage:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Service&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Data, manifests, model artifacts, and test sets&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI serverless fine-tuning&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every fine-tune run, launched from Public Hub recipes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI serverless evaluation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2,000 answers per candidate scored as a managed job&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SageMaker Model Registry&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every completed fine-tune with its evidence and metrics&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hosts the fine-tuned Qwen3-14B for Cloud EMS, invoked privately from the Cloud EMS service. No idle cost&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock (Claude, Qwen3)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;Paraphrase (three rewrites per answer in the training data) and frontier model benchmarking&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Alarm-triage paraphrases use Qwen3-VL-235B on Amazon Bedrock. Energy-market paraphrases use Claude Sonnet 4.6 on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda + Amazon EventBridge&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Serverless-evaluation scorer and a scheduled keep-warm invocation of the custom model&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow is organized into three stages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model customization (cloud):&lt;/strong&gt; Exowatt fine-tunes open-weight base models using serverless model customization, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on SageMaker AI. Because serverless training allocates capacity on demand, the team runs multiple experiments per day rather than waiting for self-selected GPU. After evaluation, the best model is registered in the SageMaker AI Model Registry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Edge deployment:&lt;/strong&gt; For latency-sensitive, connectivity-constrained work such as alarm triage, the fine-tuned Qwen3-1.7B model is frozen, quantized to the GGUF format, and run with llama.cpp inside Kubernetes pods at the edge using on-site controllers. This path requires no GPU and operates without internet connectivity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cloud deployment (elastic):&lt;/strong&gt; For internet-connected, fleet-level analysis, the fine-tuned Qwen3-14B energy-market model is served through Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import, which runs with no idle cost and bills only while the model is active.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-sagemaker-ai-serverless-model-customization"&gt;Why Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization service provides a wide selection of open-weight models with ready-made fine-tuning recipes from the SageMaker Public Hub, so the team avoided building and optimizing a custom training container, work that can otherwise take weeks. For Exowatt, this turned fine-tuning from a scarce, scheduled event into a routine, on-demand one. This makes a rapid experimentation-and-test loop practical while keeping experimentation cost low.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fine-tuning on provisioned GPUs requires choosing a specific instance type, requesting that capacity, waiting for it to become available, running the job, and paying for the instance for as long as it is held. The held time typically includes the idle time between experiments when a dedicated instance is kept running. For a small team running many experiments, this slows down innovation and increases cost. Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization automatically manages and orchestrates the infrastructure required to run the fine-tuning job, removing the pain of capacity management, and charges only for the time the fine-tuning job runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-case-1-edge-alarm-triage-with-qwen3-1.7b"&gt;Use case 1: Edge alarm triage with Qwen3-1.7B&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt sites raise alarms around the clock. A compact on-site model reads each alarm together with the site’s own status and telemetry and returns one of a small fixed set of classifications with cited evidence (such as an offline system, derated component, or sensor mismatch) or an explicit abstention when the evidence is insufficient.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="model-selection-and-training-data"&gt;Model selection and training data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team first tried Qwen3-8B with prompting and guardrails, which plateaued below the target (0.735 macro-F1). A fine-tuned Qwen3-1.7B changed that: it outperformed the Qwen3-8B plus prompting setup on this narrow, single-turn task at roughly one-fifth the parameters, while staying small enough for GPU-free edge inference. Thirteen fine-tuned Qwen3-8B candidates across 12 dataset versions never beat the prompted 8B baseline. The smaller model with more varied data did.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The training set was small and curated: 2,522 examples built from 684 verified decisions on generated site scenarios plus 1,838 certified paraphrases that kept each decision fixed while rewriting the prose. The paraphrases were generated by Qwen3-235B on Amazon Bedrock for about $3.72. Each paraphrase was kept only if deterministic checks confirmed that the decision, labels, evidence citations, and every number were unchanged (1,838 of 2,218 candidates passed). One fault family was held out of training entirely, and 320 cases were reserved for testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="training-and-deployment"&gt;Training and deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training completed in about 32 minutes on SageMaker serverless fine-tuning for roughly $20–$25. Quantized to 8 bits (about 1.71 GiB), the model runs offline in a 6 GiB llama.cpp pod on the site’s existing CPU server. It also runs on a t3.2xlarge instance type.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-case-2-cloud-energy-market-analysis-with-qwen3-14b"&gt;Use case 2: Cloud energy-market analysis with Qwen3-14B&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dispatchable storage turns timing into an operational decision: each site must continually decide how much stored energy to hold for its load and when to release it, and for grid-connected sites market conditions feed into that choice. The fine-tuned model reads a six-hour packet of live market data (about 90 rows of real-time prices, day-ahead forecasts, and grid notices) and returns one of five market events (such as a price spike or scarcity adder) in about four seconds. Every claim is tied to a specific data row to inform the dispatch strategy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-qwen3-14b"&gt;Why Qwen3-14B&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Qwen3-14B was chosen because it is supported by Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import, it ships as a SageMaker Public Hub model with a ready LoRA recipe (no custom container or instance type to manage), and at about 28 GiB it is small enough to serve cost-effectively.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="training-data-and-process"&gt;Training data and process&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt fine-tuned the model on public Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT) market data drawn from five years of history (about 163 million rows across 12 tables). The team rule-labeled this data into 25,000 examples (22,000 for training, 1,000 for validation, and 2,000 held out for testing). They then expanded the training set to 86,281 rows by having Anthropic’s Claude Sonnet 4.6 on Amazon Bedrock rewrite the prose of each example while its labels and numeric fields stayed fixed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training was a single LoRA epoch (rank 16, alpha 32), completed in about 8.5 hours of managed serverless fine-tuning with up to four jobs running at once. The team ran 23 jobs and about 155 hours of managed training in the six days from initial plan to a champion model serving in Cloud EMS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The decisive improvement came from paraphrase diversity: adding three rewrites per answer lifted macro-F1 from 0.950 to 0.975. The base model scored only 0.415 macro-F1. It produced valid JSON but did not yet know the market. Fine-tuning fixed that.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deployment"&gt;Deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The merged checkpoint was imported to Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import in about 14 minutes. The model runs with no idle cost and bills at $6.86 per hour only while active.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="benchmark-results"&gt;Benchmark results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the edge alarm-triage task the fine-tuned Qwen3-1.7B went from a model that couldn’t produce a valid answer to 0.911 macro-F1 with 320 test cases, ahead of every frontier baseline. Applying the identical fine-tune-and-evaluate loop to the energy-market task with 2,000 test cases, the Qwen3-14B reached 0.975 macro-F1, again ahead of the frontier baseline and at a fraction of the cost. In both cases a small, fine-tuned model beat far larger general-purpose models on the specific task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both models return strict JSON in which every claim cites a specific input data row. Any unsupported claim is dropped before it reaches an operator, which is what lets the team trust small models in autonomous, physical settings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;$ / 1,000 decisions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Offline?&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: Edge alarm triage results, fine-tuned Qwen3-1.7B compared to frontier models&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;$0 per 1,000 decisions means no metered API or GPU inference cost. The quantized model runs on the site’s existing CPU.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;0.983&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 2: Cloud energy-market analysis results, fine-tuned Qwen3-14B compared to frontier models&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Costs are Amazon Bedrock list prices times measured tokens per answer. Fine-tuning lifted an open model from last place to first: +0.35 macro-F1 over Claude Opus 4.7 (95 percent confidence interval 0.324 to 0.371) at about 49× lower cost per 1,000 answers ($0.60 compared to $29.26). The model held 0.968 macro-F1 on a second, later test set.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="business-benefits"&gt;Business benefits&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Faster iteration and faster time to market:&lt;/strong&gt; Exowatt moved its customized model from experimentation to production in only nine days. This process could have taken months if the team had to build and optimize custom containers and training recipes, provision GPUs, and manage infrastructure. With SageMaker AI serverless model customization, the team trained Qwen3-1.7B in about 32 minutes and Qwen3-14B in about 8.5 hours, with no capacity requests and up to four training jobs running concurrently. For the energy-market use case, Exowatt ran 23 jobs totaling about 155 hours of managed training and moved from the base model to a fine-tuned model serving in Cloud EMS in six days. The merged checkpoint was imported into Amazon Bedrock in about 14 minutes. The end-to-end pipeline used seven AWS serverless services, with no instances, clusters, or endpoints for Exowatt to manage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Higher accuracy compared to frontier models:&lt;/strong&gt; Both fine-tuned models beat the larger frontier models on their own task: Qwen3-1.7B at 0.911 macro-F1 (compared to 0.698 for Claude Sonnet 4.6) and Qwen3-14B at 0.975 (compared to 0.625 for Claude Opus 4.7).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lower cost across both deployments:&lt;/strong&gt; In the cloud, the Qwen3-14B serves at $0.60 per 1,000 answers versus $29.26 for Claude Opus 4.7 (about 49× lower), with no idle cost and $6.86 per hour only while active on Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import. At the edge, the quantized Qwen3-1.7B runs on the site’s existing CPU with no per-inference cost and no dependency on connectivity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Grounded, low-risk outputs:&lt;/strong&gt; Across both models, the evidence fence removed every uncited claim (any evidence citation whose source, field, or value does not literally match the input), and the edge model abstains rather than guesses when unsure. This is what let the team run it autonomously in shadow mode on a cloud-hosted replica of a site.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned"&gt;Lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;For specific tasks, smaller fine-tuned large language models can outperform large frontier models:&lt;/strong&gt; Fine-tuned Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-14B models each outperformed frontier models on the target task, delivering higher accuracy at a fraction of the cost with significantly smaller models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data quality and variety outweigh raw volume and extra epochs:&lt;/strong&gt; For tightly scoped tasks, data quality mattered more than model size or data volume. On the alarm-triage model, more verified training variety moved macro-F1 from 0.584 (trained on the 684 verified decisions alone) to 0.911 (with the paraphrase-expanded 2,522-row set). Simply running more epochs over the same rows did worse: a control run that trained on the 684 original rows for 11 epochs (the same number of steps) reached only 0.879. Curated data mattered more than dataset size throughout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Removing the provisioning step changes how a team works:&lt;/strong&gt; When a training run no longer requires waiting for GPU capacity, fine-tuning shifts from a rare event to a routine, iterative one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One training pipeline serves two deployment targets:&lt;/strong&gt; Separating model training from deployment lets Exowatt use the same pipeline for both offline edge deployment and connected fleet-level analysis, simplifying operations and accelerating model deployment in the cloud.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Exowatt now has one reusable AI pipeline that took a task from model specific data preparation to model deployment. Rather than tuning one model for different tasks, Exowatt plans to apply the same fine-tuning principles to different tasks, each with its own data, specific models, and evaluations, across the fleet as new device types and market tasks come online.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern generalizes beyond energy industry segments. AI engineers, data scientists, and customers across industry segments can adopt the same principles. Use Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization as a low-overhead AI pipeline to fine-tune small, task-specific models, then deploy them where the work happens: at the edge when connectivity or latency requires it, and through a managed cloud endpoint such as Amazon SageMaker AI for elastic workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/model-customization/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI serverless model customization&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Fleet-wide computer vision lifecycle at the edge with SageMaker SDK v3</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fleet-wide-computer-vision-lifecycle-at-the-edge-with-sagemaker-sdk-v3/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kiowa Jackson]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 17:56:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">24d8d5b36e78caf18670ef6d83892be19ec25356</guid>

					<description>Fleet-wide computer vision needs a model per camera, not one model for the whole fleet. This post presents a working Amazon SageMaker SDK v3 reference architecture that trains, quality-gates, compiles per-camera models with SageMaker Neo, and versions thousands of them, with an AWS Step Functions layer pacing fleet-scale retraining within account quotas.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Fleet-wide computer vision model lifecycle management is a problem that shows up the moment a quality inspection proof of concept succeeds. One camera and one model work fine. Then the plant asks for the same result on 40 lines, each with several cameras, and every camera sees the world differently. Each has a different angle, different glare off the same stainless surface, and different ambient light depending on which bay door is open. In production deployments we have worked on, a single model trained on pooled footage underperforms enough individual cameras that each camera ends up with its own fine-tuned model. Now the real problem is no longer training one model well. It’s training, evaluating, versioning, and shipping thousands of them without a human in the loop for each one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post updates the pattern introduced in the 2023 series &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-an-end-to-end-mlops-pipeline-for-visual-quality-inspection-at-the-edge-part-1/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build an end-to-end MLOps pipeline for visual quality inspection at the edge&lt;/a&gt; for the current &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker&lt;/a&gt; Python SDK v3. It replaces the since-deprecated Amazon SageMaker Edge Manager with Amazon SageMaker Neo for edge optimization. I walk through a dual-branch Amazon SageMaker Pipeline that trains an anomaly detector and a defect classifier per camera, with quality gates that block underperforming models. An in-pipeline SageMaker Neo compilation step ensures every registered model ships with a device-optimized artifact, and an AWS Step Functions orchestration layer paces fleet training. You deploy everything with the AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK), and a full pipeline run on synthetic data costs about $0.07 in compute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete sample is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-fleet-cv-pipeline" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the sample’s fleet-wide model lifecycle. An orchestration layer queues re-training requests and limits concurrent work. A per-camera training pipeline prepares data, trains and evaluates an anomaly detector and a classifier, and produces versioned model artifacts for edge devices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-20483-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-20483-1-1.png" alt="Solution architecture: SQS FIFO queue triggers AWS Lambda, which starts a Step Functions state machine with an Amazon DynamoDB semaphore for concurrency control. The state machine launches a 13-step SageMaker Pipeline with parallel entry steps (Preprocess, Fetch Latest Anomaly, Fetch Latest Classifier), dual training branches with ONNX evaluation, quality gates, Neo compilation, and Model Registry registration. Compiled artifacts land in S3. Completions and failures converge on an SNS topic." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The training half is a single SageMaker Pipeline, parameterized by camera, that runs 13 steps per execution. Three entry steps run in parallel: Preprocess, Fetch Latest Anomaly, and Fetch Latest Classifier. The pipeline preprocesses that camera’s images and fetches the camera’s most recent approved model for each branch, then trains two models. The first is a small convolutional autoencoder that learns what normal product looks like and flags anomalies by reconstruction error. The second is a ResNet18 classifier fine-tuned to confirm the defect. Each branch exports its model to ONNX, evaluates the exported artifact against held-out data, and passes through a quality gate. Past the gate, the model is compiled with SageMaker Neo for the configured edge target, and only then registered in the SageMaker Model Registry. As a result, every approved package has a device-ready compiled artifact. Packages carry the camera ID as metadata, and each branch’s warm-start fetch filters on it, so re-training always resumes from that camera’s own history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The orchestration half paces the fleet. Re-training requests land on an Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) FIFO queue, and an AWS Lambda function starts a Step Functions execution per request. Each execution acquires a slot through a DynamoDB semaphore before starting its pipeline, so a burst of requests drains at a steady rate instead of all launching at once. Completions publish to Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS), and operational failures reach the same topic through an Amazon EventBridge rule with the original error preserved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The output of the system is versioned, quality-gated, per-camera model artifacts. The Model Registry holds the portable ONNX, and S3 holds the Neo-compiled build of that model, which is a device-optimized shared library loaded by the DLR runtime rather than ONNX. Both are ready for whatever delivery mechanism your plant uses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy the sample you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The SageMaker AI, AWS Step Functions, AWS Lambda, Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS), Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS), Amazon EventBridge, and Amazon DynamoDB resources in the stacks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10, 3.11, or 3.12, Docker.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS CDK CLI version 2.1131.0 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Previous CLI versions fail with a cloud assembly schema mismatch against recent versions of &lt;code&gt;aws-cdk-lib&lt;/code&gt;. No service quota increase is needed. The orchestration layer admits at most five pipeline executions at a time by default, which keeps concurrent SageMaker training jobs inside the standard account quota.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deploy to an AWS Region other than your CLI default, set all three of &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;AWS_DEFAULT_REGION&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;CDK_DEFAULT_REGION&lt;/code&gt; on the deploy command itself rather than in your shell profile, for example, &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION=us-east-2 AWS_DEFAULT_REGION=us-east-2 CDK_DEFAULT_REGION=us-east-2 cdk deploy --all&lt;/code&gt;. The CDK CLI, Boto3, and the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) resolve the Region from overlapping but not identical variable sets, and a mismatch produces confusing partial deployments. Prefixing the command sets all three for that invocation only, so your own default Region is left unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="defining-the-training-pipeline-with-sdk-v3"&gt;Defining the training pipeline with SDK v3&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The code in this section is excerpted from the CDK application in the repository rather than a standalone script, and it runs during cdk deploy, when the stack synthesizes the pipeline definition. Environment setup and the deploy commands come later, under Deploying and running the sample. SDK v3 reorganized the SageMaker Python SDK into modular packages, and the imports show the new shape of the library:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.train import ModelTrainer
from sagemaker.train.configs import InputData, Compute, OutputDataConfig
from sagemaker.core.training.configs import MetricDefinition
from sagemaker.serve.model_builder import ModelBuilder
from sagemaker.mlops.workflow.pipeline import Pipeline
from sagemaker.mlops.workflow.steps import ProcessingStep, TrainingStep
from sagemaker.mlops.workflow.model_step import ModelStep
from sagemaker.mlops.workflow.condition_step import ConditionStep
from sagemaker.core.workflow.conditions import ConditionGreaterThanOrEqualTo
from sagemaker.core.workflow.functions import JsonGet&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Installing &lt;code&gt;sagemaker&amp;gt;=3.0.0,&amp;lt;4&lt;/code&gt; pulls in &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-core&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-train&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-serve&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-mlops&lt;/code&gt; as separate packages. Training configuration that v2 spread across &lt;code&gt;Estimator&lt;/code&gt; constructor arguments now lives in typed config objects passed to &lt;code&gt;ModelTrainer&lt;/code&gt;. This is the anomaly branch’s trainer, verbatim from the sample:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;anomalib_trainer = ModelTrainer(
    training_image=custom_training_image_uri,
    compute=Compute(
        instance_type=training_instance_type.to_string(),
        instance_count=1,
    ),
    base_job_name="cv-pipeline-anomalib-train",
    sagemaker_session=pipeline_session,
    role=role_arn,
    hyperparameters={
        "program": "train_anomalib.py",
        "model_name": "autoencoder",
        "image_size": "256",
        "num_epochs": "3",
        "batch_size": "16",
        "learning_rate": "0.001",
    },
    input_data_config=anomalib_input_data,
    output_data_config=OutputDataConfig(
        s3_output_path=run_prefix("anomalib-training")
    ),
).with_metric_definitions(
    [
        MetricDefinition(
            name="image_auroc",
            regex=r"validation:image_auroc=([0-9]*\.?[0-9]+)",
        ),
    ]
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;anomalib_input_data&lt;/code&gt; wires four channels (&lt;code&gt;train&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;validation&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;test&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;pretrained_model&lt;/code&gt;) from upstream step properties, and &lt;code&gt;run_prefix&lt;/code&gt; scopes every S3 path to the pipeline execution ID so concurrent fleet runs never collide. Both are in the repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two v3 behaviors matter when the pipeline lives inside a CDK application, and neither appears in the changelog as a breaking change. First, SDK v3 validates the pipeline’s execution role against live IAM while building the pipeline definition. This validation happens at synthesis time rather than execution time. A CDK app can’t create the role and the pipeline in one deployment, because the role doesn’t exist yet when the SDK validates it. This is separate from cdk bootstrap, which provisions the CDK toolkit resources once per account and Region and might already be done in your account. The sample’s own two-phase deploy is driven by a context flag that happens to share that name. An initial pass with &lt;code&gt;-c bootstrap=true&lt;/code&gt; creates the role, bucket, and ECR repository, and the second pass synthesizes the pipeline against the now-existing role. The role Amazon Resource Name (ARN) embedded in the pipeline definition must also be a concrete string, not an unresolved CDK token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, the v3 role validator simulates ECR pull permissions without a resource ARN, so an execution role scoped to a single ECR repository fails validation even though it would work at runtime. The sample’s role carries a broader ECR grant for this reason.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lastly, one v3 habit the sample uses: constructing the pipeline with &lt;code&gt;PipelineSession(default_bucket=your_bucket)&lt;/code&gt; routes SDK-managed uploads, such as step code, into the pipeline’s own bucket, so every artifact lives in one place and the execution role’s permissions cover a single bucket. (Granting the role access to the SDK’s default bucket works too. This approach is tidier.)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="quality-gates-that-match-how-defect-data-behaves"&gt;Quality gates that match how defect data behaves&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every trained model is evaluated before registration, and the evaluation runs against the exported ONNX artifact rather than the in-memory PyTorch model. That distinction matters: the artifact you gate is the artifact you ship, so an export problem can never slip a broken model past a healthy-looking training run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The anomaly branch gates on AUROC of at least 0.8. The classifier branch gates on two metrics at once, and the choice of metrics is shaped by the defining property of defect data: heavy class imbalance. In this sample roughly 90 percent of images are good, which is realistic for a production line. Against that distribution, plain accuracy is meaningless, because a model that predicts “good” for every image scores about 0.9 while catching zero defects. Balanced accuracy, the mean of per-class recall, corrects for that, but it has its own blind spot. A model with perfect recall on good parts and only 20 percent recall on defects still scores (1.0 + 0.2) / 2 = 0.6. So the gate requires balanced accuracy of at least 0.6 and defect recall of at least 0.5, together:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;step_check_classifier = ConditionStep(
    name="CheckClassifierQuality",
    conditions=[
        ConditionGreaterThanOrEqualTo(
            left=JsonGet(
                step_name=step_eval_classifier.name,
                property_file="evaluation",
                json_path="balanced_accuracy",
            ),
            right=min_accuracy,
        ),
        ConditionGreaterThanOrEqualTo(
            left=JsonGet(
                step_name=step_eval_classifier.name,
                property_file="evaluation",
                json_path="defect_recall",
            ),
            right=min_defect_recall,
        ),
    ],
    if_steps=[step_compile_classifier, step_register_classifier],
    else_steps=[step_fail_classifier],
    depends_on=[step_eval_classifier],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;code&gt;ConditionStep&lt;/code&gt; ANDs its conditions, so both must pass. The thresholds are pipeline parameters, adjustable per execution without redeploying. If a gate fails, a &lt;code&gt;FailStep&lt;/code&gt; marks the execution failed and that branch’s model never reaches the registry (the two branches gate independently, so the other branch’s model may still register). The fleet keeps serving the previous approved model for that camera.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The training code addresses the same imbalance from the other side. The classifier is a fine-tuned &lt;code&gt;resnet18&lt;/code&gt; with ImageNet weights and a two-class head. At per-camera dataset sizes, fine-tuning pretrained weights is what keeps the model from collapsing onto the majority class. The training code balances training batches with a &lt;code&gt;WeightedRandomSampler&lt;/code&gt; using inverse class frequencies, while the loss stays unweighted. Combining a weighted loss with weighted sampling over-corrects toward the minority class.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="compiling-for-the-edge-with-sagemaker-neo-inside-the-pipeline"&gt;Compiling for the edge with SageMaker Neo, inside the pipeline&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Notice the gate’s &lt;code&gt;if_steps&lt;/code&gt; in the preceding code: past the gate, compilation runs first and registration depends on it. Both training scripts export ONNX with dynamic batch axes. The quality gates score those exported artifacts under ONNX Runtime, and then a &lt;code&gt;CompileAnomalibNeo&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;CompileClassifierNeo&lt;/code&gt; step hands the gated artifact to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/neo.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Neo&lt;/a&gt;. The invariant is gate, then compile, then register. A model only becomes an Approved package if it also compiled for the fleet’s edge target, so the registry never advertises a model the devices cannot run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compile step encapsulates three error-prone details. Neo requires the input tarball to contain only the model file, but training jobs emit &lt;code&gt;model.tar.gz&lt;/code&gt; with the checkpoint and metrics alongside the ONNX model. The step repackages an ONNX-only tarball first. Neo also requires concrete input shapes while the exports declare a dynamic batch axis, so batch size 1 is pinned in the compilation request. And because pipeline steps can retry, the compilation job name derives deterministically from the execution ID, so a retry resumes polling the job it already started instead of orphaning it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The target is a pipeline parameter: &lt;code&gt;NeoTargetDevice&lt;/code&gt; selects a named device such as &lt;code&gt;jetson_xavier&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;rasp4b&lt;/code&gt;, and its default compiles for the &lt;code&gt;NeoTargetOs&lt;/code&gt;/&lt;code&gt;NeoTargetArch&lt;/code&gt; platform, LINUX/X86_64 by default. The compiled package contains the optimized &lt;code&gt;compiled.so&lt;/code&gt; plus the &lt;a href="https://github.com/neo-ai/neo-ai-dlr" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DLR runtime&lt;/a&gt; for loading it. The default output runs on any x86_64 Linux host, so you can validate it before committing to device-specific targets. Compilation itself incurs no charge. You pay for the processing instance that drives it and the S3 storage of the outputs. For compiling an already-registered model for additional device types later, without re-training, the repository includes an ad-hoc script that runs the same flow against the Model Registry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="per-camera-models-in-the-model-registry"&gt;Per-camera models in the Model Registry&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each branch registers into its own model package group, and every package carries the camera ID and its evaluation metrics. From the sample, with the metrics wiring elided:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;classifier_model_builder = ModelBuilder(
    s3_model_data_url=classifier_model_data,
    image_uri=custom_training_image_uri,
    sagemaker_session=pipeline_session,
    role_arn=role_arn,
)
step_register_classifier = ModelStep(
    name="RegisterClassifierModel",
    depends_on=[step_compile_classifier],
    step_args=classifier_model_builder.register(
        model_package_group_name="sm1k-classifier-model-group",
        approval_status="Approved",
        model_metrics=classifier_metrics,
        customer_metadata_properties={"CameraId": camera_id},
    ),
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Camera identity in &lt;code&gt;customer_metadata_properties&lt;/code&gt; is what turns two shared package groups into a fleet catalog. Packages from all cameras live in the same groups, and per-camera history is recovered by filtering on the metadata. Each branch has a fetch step that pages through approved packages newest-first until it finds the requesting camera’s latest model, because &lt;code&gt;ListModelPackages&lt;/code&gt; cannot filter on customer metadata server-side. On a camera’s first run the fetch finds nothing and training falls back to its cold-start initialization (ImageNet weights for the classifier, random initialization for the autoencoder). Every later run resumes from that camera’s own prior model, which is what makes frequent, cheap, incremental re-training viable across a fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note what these registry entries are and are not: they catalog versioned, quality-gated artifacts, but their container reference is the training image, which serves no inference endpoints. They exist for a fleet workflow where a downstream system pulls artifacts and ships them to devices, not for &lt;code&gt;CreateEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="orchestrating-fleet-training-requests"&gt;Orchestrating fleet training requests&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every AWS account has service quotas on concurrent SageMaker training jobs, and each pipeline execution here launches several jobs, so fleet re-training needs pacing. Something has to keep a burst of 500 requests from becoming 500 simultaneous pipeline launches. The sample uses a semaphore, a counter of available slots that works like a parking lot with five spaces. Before the Step Functions state machine starts a pipeline, it takes a slot by atomically incrementing a counter in DynamoDB, with a condition that refuses the increment once the cap is reached:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;acquire_semaphore = tasks.DynamoUpdateItem(
    self, "AcquireSemaphore",
    table=semaphore_table,
    key={"LockName": tasks.DynamoAttributeValue.from_string("pipeline-concurrency")},
    update_expression="SET ActiveCount = if_not_exists(ActiveCount, :zero) + :one",
    condition_expression="attribute_not_exists(ActiveCount) OR ActiveCount &amp;lt; :limit",
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If all slots are taken, the execution waits and retries. When its pipeline finishes, it decrements the counter to release the slot. One detail is worth carrying into your own designs. The DynamoDB condition expression enforces the cap in a single atomic operation, rather than the state machine reading the counter and deciding, which would leave a race window between the read and the write. The repository implements the rest of the operational hardening, including releasing slots on every failure path and forwarding failed executions to Amazon SNS through Amazon EventBridge. Its README covers sizing the wait bounds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cap of five is a stack parameter, set low so the sample deploys and runs in any standard account without requesting quota increases. Adjust it to whatever your account’s training-job quotas support. Past a single account’s quotas, scaling fleet training is its own topic. The standard levers are raising the service quotas, spreading training across instance types or Regions, and distributing the fleet across multiple accounts. You can also hold the backlog in SQS and admit work only as capacity frees up. Those choices depend on your fleet’s size and shape. The sample gives you the working skeleton to extend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploying-and-running-the-sample"&gt;Deploying and running the sample&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough in the repository README is the authoritative version. The following short form starts from a clean checkout and sets up the environment before the first deploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-fleet-cv-pipeline
cd sample-sagemaker-fleet-cv-pipeline &amp;amp;&amp;amp; python3 -m venv .venv &amp;amp;&amp;amp; source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
cdk bootstrap                                           # once per account/region
cdk deploy SageMakerCVPipelineStack -c bootstrap=true   # roles, bucket, ECR
cdk deploy --all                                        # pipeline + orchestration
./scripts/push_ecr_image.sh                             # build and push training image&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By default the pipeline generates synthetic production line images, so you can validate the whole system end to end with no dataset and no data approvals. A full run executes all 13 steps, including both Neo compilations, in about 15 minutes and costs roughly $0.07 in billed &lt;code&gt;ml.m5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; time (US East (Ohio) Region (us-east-2), July 2026, on-demand). The orchestration resources together cost well under $0.10 per month at sample scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To exercise the pipeline on real images, the repository includes a downloader for the &lt;a href="https://github.com/Shenggan/BCCD_Dataset" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BCCD dataset&lt;/a&gt;. The dataset is a collection of 364 annotated blood smear microscopy images originally open sourced on GitHub and reorganized into Pascal VOC format under an MIT license. The same dataset is also distributed by &lt;a href="https://public.roboflow.com/object-detection/bccd" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Roboflow&lt;/a&gt; as public domain. The script maps platelet presence to the defect class, optionally splits the images per camera to simulate a fleet, and uploads training tarballs to the pipeline bucket. The accompanying notebook runs the full pipeline on the real data and does side-by-side ONNX inference on a normal and an anomalous sample. Treating platelets as defects is an illustrative stand-in for a manufacturing defect distribution, not a clinical claim.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model packages are created at pipeline runtime, so AWS CloudFormation cannot delete them. Remove them with the AWS CLI first, then destroy the stacks with &lt;code&gt;cdk destroy --all -c bootstrap=true --force&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-fleet-cv-pipeline" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;README&lt;/a&gt; lists the exact commands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, I showed a working SageMaker SDK v3 reference architecture for the fleet-scale computer vision lifecycle. The architecture includes a dual-branch training pipeline where every model is quality-gated on its exported ONNX artifact, compiled with SageMaker Neo, and only then registered. A Model Registry layout uses camera metadata to turn shared package groups into per-camera warm-startable history, and a Step Functions orchestration layer paces fleet training within account quotas. For teams whose needs are met by managed options such as Amazon Lookout for Vision, that is the simpler path. This pattern targets the per-camera, fleet-scale constraint that managed options do not cover. The design rules transfer beyond computer vision: gate on balanced accuracy plus minority-class recall rather than accuracy, evaluate and compile the artifact you ship, and make operational failures notify rather than terminate silently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample deploys in minutes and a full validation run costs about seven cents of compute, so a low-cost next step is to clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-fleet-cv-pipeline" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt; and run it. From there, point the BCCD downloader at a real bucket, set &lt;code&gt;NeoTargetDevice&lt;/code&gt; for your hardware, or size the orchestration bounds to your fleet. For the SDK v3 migration itself, the &lt;a href="https://sagemaker.readthedocs.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Python SDK documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the two-part series announcing the new SDK are the references I kept open throughout.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you want help adapting this pattern to your production environment, reach out to your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
