			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Groundhog Day for AI agents: Prompt caching patterns for using OpenAI models on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/groundhog-day-for-ai-agents-prompt-caching-patterns-for-using-openai-models-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Claudio Mazzoni]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 17:27:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">de80c503415138bb46de169401d016e12857bb15</guid>

					<description>Every time an agent takes a step, it re-reads its entire context before acting. Here is how prompt caching for OpenAI models on Amazon Bedrock lets your agent remember, cutting input cost and latency without resending the same tools, instructions, and history every turn.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Every time you spin off your agent, it has to re-read its whole life story before performing an action. Here’s how to make it remember without having to feed it the same context again.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the movie &lt;em&gt;Groundhog Day&lt;/em&gt;, Bill Murray’s character re-lives the same February 2 every day. He wakes up to the same song playing on the radio, in the same inn, and the same small town, again and again for what some movie fans estimate to be 34 years.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nothing he accomplished the day before carries over unless he works it out again from scratch. AI agents live in a version of Groundhog Day. Every time an agent takes a step (calls a tool, reads the result, decides what to do next), it has to send the model its entire story so far to maintain continuity: the tool definitions, the instructions, every previous tool call, and every result. The model only predicts the next token based on the input you provide. It has no internal memory between requests, so it must read all of it again. By turn 20 of a coding session, the model re-reads turns 1–19, and it’s you who pays for every one of those tokens, again and again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prompt caching breaks the cycle. The service stores your input prompt (instructions, tools, and content) and reuses it on the next request, at a fraction of the input price.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, I walk you through an agent’s life, from its first turn to when it gets new tools and spawns helpers. Along the way, I show you how prompt caching keeps costs down and keeps agents responsive and consistent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-an-agent-sends-to-its-llm"&gt;What an agent sends to its LLM&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before talking about caching, it helps to look at what a typical agent’s first invocation to the large language model (LLM) contains:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool list&lt;/strong&gt; – The definitions of every function the agent can call. Changes when you ship a new release.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instructions&lt;/strong&gt; – The developer prompt: rules, persona, policies, examples. Also changes per release.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;History&lt;/strong&gt; – Everything that happened in this session so far: the task, the model’s tool calls, and the tool results (which grows every turn).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New turn&lt;/strong&gt; – The latest tool result or user message (which might be different every time).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What an agent sends to the model, turn after turn if you don’t set up caching:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-1-1.png" alt="Five growing agent requests, with tools, instructions, and earlier history marked in red and each new turn in yellow." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: What an agent sends to the model on every turn&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-prompt-caching-works-under-the-hood"&gt;How prompt caching works under the hood&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a model reads a prompt, it first does a write called &lt;em&gt;prefill&lt;/em&gt;. During prefill, it processes every input token and builds its key and value vectors (also known as key-value, or KV), which form the model’s working state. This happens before the model writes its first token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prompt caching saves that state, which contains the system prompt, tools, content, history, and so on. When a later request starts with the same input tokens (that is, the same text), the prompt caching service loads the saved state instead of computing it again. It then processes only what is new in the next payload to the model. Prompt caching can be implicit, where Amazon Bedrock selects where the reusable prefix ends, or explicit, where your code marks that endpoint using &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_breakpoint&lt;/code&gt;. You can use both approaches together, and we recommend doing so.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Turn 1 compared to turn 2&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-2-1.png" alt="Turn 1 writes the tools and instructions through a breakpoint. Turn 2 reads that same prefix from cache and processes its new question." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Turn 1 saves the prompt prefix up to the breakpoint, and turn 2 reuses it and pays full price only for the new question&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Implicit or explicit, and why do we need both?&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For agentic workloads, implicit caching lets Bedrock choose what to cache as the conversation grows. With explicit caching, you can mark shared content, such as tools and instructions, for reuse across sessions and when spinning up helper agents. You don’t always need both forms of caching, but combining them gives you a reusable starting point while Bedrock manages the growing conversation automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;New sessions can also reuse that shared starting point while each conversation builds on its own cached history. In this post, we start with the first turn, then show how to carry cached content through later turns, new tools, and across subagents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="hello-cached-world"&gt;Hello cached world&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first win is to cache the static part of the prompt: the tools and instructions that stay the same across requests. These examples use your existing Amazon Bedrock client, &lt;code&gt;TOOLS&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;task&lt;/code&gt;. Replace the shortened &lt;code&gt;INSTRUCTIONS&lt;/code&gt; string with your real policies and examples. The reusable prefix must reach 1,024 tokens to qualify.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When caching static content, use explicit mode: choose where the saved prefix ends.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;KEY = "support-agent:v7:gpt-6-astra"
INSTRUCTIONS = "Your full support policies and examples..."

# Rendered context: [provider context][TOOLS][INSTRUCTIONS] | [task]
# Cache starts at the beginning; the marker below chooses its end.
resp = client.responses.create(
    model="us.openai.gpt-6-astra",
    store=False,  # Response storage; prompt caching still works.
    include=["reasoning.encrypted_content"],
    prompt_cache_key=KEY,
    prompt_cache_options={"mode": "explicit", "ttl": "30m"},
    tools=TOOLS,  # Rendered before instructions; keep order stable.
    input=[
        {
            "role": "developer",
            "content": [{
                "type": "input_text",
                "text": INSTRUCTIONS,
                # Save through this whole block, including TOOLS.
                "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"},
            }],
        },
        # User message is processed but not cached.
        {"role": "user", "content": task},
    ],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt; tells the service which requests are meant to share a cache. We come back to how to use it when we get to reading the cache in new sessions. In one of our tests using explicit caching, the roughly 4,000-token tools-and-instructions prefix wrote 4,120 tokens on the first request and read those 4,120 tokens on later requests. The &lt;code&gt;task&lt;/code&gt; (or user request) comes after the cache boundary, so this doesn’t trigger a write to the cache.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep in mind that prompt caching works only when you pass exactly the same text. Make sure that the agent’s instructions and tool lists are serialized the same way every time. Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers and other forms of tool delivery need to be indexed to make sure they stay consistent. Keep anything that varies, such as the date, the user’s name, or a request ID, after the cached instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As the content grows and varies (different tools and content), use implicit mode to let the service follow the conversation. Here, the first request has no explicit marker. Its latest eligible message is the user’s task, so the automatic breakpoint comes after that task and covers the tools, instructions, and task together. The mode is shown explicitly in the following example for clarity, even though implicit is the default. Setting &lt;code&gt;store=False&lt;/code&gt; leaves conversation history in your application. It doesn’t disable caching.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def ask_with_implicit_cache(history):
    return client.responses.create(
        model="us.openai.gpt-6-astra",
        store=False,
        include=["reasoning.encrypted_content"],
        prompt_cache_key=KEY,
        prompt_cache_options={"mode": "implicit", "ttl": "30m"},
        tools=TOOLS,
        input=history,  # Everything gets cached
    )

history = [
    {"role": "developer", "content": INSTRUCTIONS},
    {"role": "user", "content": task},
]
# Automatic endpoint: the end of the user message.
resp = ask_with_implicit_cache(history)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is useful when the next turn continues the same task. A different task doesn’t match the prefix through the old task, and the implicit write doesn’t necessarily create a separate entry for only the tools and instructions. Use an explicit marker there when you need that shorter shared prefix to be reusable across tasks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use both when the agent needs a shared starting point and a growing history. Start with the marked developer block in the following code example and call the function &lt;code&gt;ask_with_implicit_cache&lt;/code&gt;. The request-level mode stays implicit. The block-level marker adds an explicit save point after the instructions. The service can also save through the latest eligible message. This keeps the shared prefix available while the conversation grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;history = [
    {
        "role": "developer",
        "content": [{
            "type": "input_text",
            "text": INSTRUCTIONS,
            "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"}, 
        }],
    },
    {"role": "user", "content": task},
]
# Still implicit mode, now with an explicit startup save point in the history.
resp = ask_with_implicit_cache(history)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="caching-conversation-history-and-tool-results"&gt;Caching conversation history and tool results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model requests tools by name and supplies their arguments. Your application must execute them. &lt;code&gt;execute_function&lt;/code&gt; is a helper you build to route those requests to the matching Python functions and return their results. The following loop uses this helper to run each requested tool. It appends the model’s output and all tool results to the &lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt; list, then sends that list again until the model returns a final answer. In implicit mode, Amazon Bedrock can reuse the unchanged prefix and cache the added content through the last tool result. Keep earlier messages and the initial &lt;code&gt;TOOLS&lt;/code&gt; unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

# Map tool names to their Python implementations.
TOOL_HANDLERS = {
    "get_owner": get_owner,
    # Register your other initial tools here.
}

def execute_function(name, arguments):
    """Run the requested tool and return its result."""
    handler = TOOL_HANDLERS.get(name)
    if handler is None:
        return {"error": f"Unknown tool: {name}"}
    return handler(**arguments)

while True:
    # Preserve all output items, including encrypted reasoning.
    history.extend(resp.output)
    calls = [x for x in resp.output if x.type == "function_call"]
    if not calls:
        break
    for call in calls:
        result = execute_function(call.name, json.loads(call.arguments))
        history.append({
            "type": "function_call_output",
            "call_id": call.call_id,
            "output": json.dumps(result),
        })
    # The last result in this group supplies the automatic endpoint.
    resp = ask_with_implicit_cache(history)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For follow-ups, append a new user message and call the helper again. Keep earlier messages unchanged so their cached endpoints remain available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rolling breakpoints: Each turn reads everything before it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-3-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-3-1.png" alt="Rolling breakpoints, where each turn reads all earlier turns and writes only its own tool result." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Rolling breakpoints, where each turn reads everything before it and writes only its own tool result&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a separate explicit-only run, recreate the two-message history from the combined Hello cached world example. This loop wraps tool results in &lt;code&gt;input_text&lt;/code&gt; blocks and marks the final result in each batch. Earlier markers stay in &lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt;. See the &lt;a href="https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/prompt-caching" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;current lookup rules&lt;/a&gt; for long conversations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json

while True:
    resp = client.responses.create(
        model="us.openai.gpt-6-astra", store=False,
        include=["reasoning.encrypted_content"],
        tools=TOOLS, prompt_cache_key=KEY, input=history,
        prompt_cache_options={"mode": "explicit", "ttl": "30m"},
    )
    d = resp.usage.input_tokens_details
    print("read", d.cached_tokens, "write", d.cache_write_tokens)
    history.extend(resp.output)  # Includes encrypted reasoning.
    calls = [x for x in resp.output if x.type == "function_call"]
    if not calls:
        break
    batch = []
    for call in calls:
        result = execute_function(call.name, json.loads(call.arguments))
        batch.append({
            "type": "function_call_output", "call_id": call.call_id,
            "output": [{"type": "input_text", "text": json.dumps(result)}],
        })
    # Add the newest boundary; retain every earlier marker.
    batch[-1]["output"][-1]["prompt_cache_breakpoint"] = {"mode": "explicit"}
    history.extend(batch)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-6 Astra tied for the best implicit result in this six-turn workload: about 62 percent lower input cost than running without caching. Explicit rolling breakpoints saved about 61 percent on the same model. Both reused more history as the conversation grew. These are workload results. Measure your own agent’s savings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-4-1.png" alt="Bar chart comparing the input cost of each caching setup on GPT-6 Astra, relative to no caching." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Input cost of every caching setup on GPT-6 Astra, relative to no caching&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Watch out for:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Only a static breakpoint in explicit-only mode&lt;/strong&gt; – The tools and instructions are cached, but the growing history is processed again on every turn (0.61). This limitation doesn’t apply to the combined example, which also saves through eligible conversation endings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Moving a single breakpoint&lt;/strong&gt; can prevent cache reuse and cause repeated writes in explicit-only mode. Keep the previous turn’s breakpoint too.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Only an implicit write through a changing task&lt;/strong&gt; – A new task might share the tools and instructions but not the previously saved endpoint. Add an explicit marker after the shared instructions if different tasks need to reuse that shorter prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="when-tools-change"&gt;When tools change&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sometimes an agent’s tool list changes, or new tools get added. Keep the initial list stable: adding, removing, or reordering definitions can invalidate the cached prefix. Choose the approach that fits how the tool becomes available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;When known tools are invoked in different steps of the agent’s flow:&lt;/strong&gt; Send the same sorted &lt;code&gt;TOOLS&lt;/code&gt; and use &lt;code&gt;allowed_tools&lt;/code&gt; to limit what the model can call. With &lt;code&gt;allowed_tools&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;"mode": "auto"&lt;/code&gt; lets the model decide whether to call an allowed tool, and &lt;code&gt;"mode": "required"&lt;/code&gt; requires at least one allowed tool call. Pass this as &lt;code&gt;tool_choice&lt;/code&gt;. The restriction doesn’t change the cached catalog:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;tool_choice={"type": "allowed_tools", "mode": "auto",
             "tools": [{"type": "function", "name": "get_owner"}]}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New tools during a session.&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Both Amazon Bedrock Runtime and Amazon Bedrock Mantle endpoints accept an input item type called &lt;code&gt;additional_tools&lt;/code&gt; for GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, and GPT-5.6 Luna. For model availability by AWS Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. Register the new tool &lt;code&gt;get_due_date&lt;/code&gt; in the helper function &lt;code&gt;execute_function&lt;/code&gt;, then append its schema to the completed history from the agent loop. Keep the initial &lt;code&gt;TOOLS&lt;/code&gt; unchanged:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;TOOL_HANDLERS["get_due_date"] = get_due_date
NEW_TOOL = {
    "type": "function", "name": "get_due_date",
    "description": "Return the due date for a support case.",
    "strict": True,
    "parameters": {
        "type": "object",
        "properties": {"case_id": {"type": "string"}},
        "required": ["case_id"], "additionalProperties": False,
    },
}
history.append({
    "type": "additional_tools", "role": "developer",
    "tools": [NEW_TOOL],
})
history.append({
    "role": "user", "content": "Find the due date for case C-123.",
})
# Implicit mode saves through this new user message, including NEW_TOOL.
resp = ask_with_implicit_cache(history)
d = resp.usage.input_tokens_details
print("old prefix read", d.cached_tokens, "new write", d.cache_write_tokens)

# Example output:
# old prefix read 2048 new write 256&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A new tool arrives mid-session&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-5-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-5-1.png" alt="Native additional_tools loading preserves the earlier cached prefix. The GPT-6 Astra Runtime test reads 2,068 tokens and writes a 145-token extension. Editing the initial catalog causes a cache miss." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Appending a tool keeps the earlier prefix reusable, with example counts from GPT-6 Astra on Amazon Bedrock Runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Continue the tool-execution loop with the new response. The loop adds the model’s output to &lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt;, runs any requested tools, and appends their results. Keep the &lt;code&gt;additional_tools&lt;/code&gt; item in later requests so the new tool remains available. In implicit mode, Amazon Bedrock can cache through the new user message, including the tool definitions that precede it. In explicit-only mode, retain earlier breakpoints and add one to an &lt;code&gt;input_text&lt;/code&gt; block in that user message. The &lt;code&gt;additional_tools&lt;/code&gt; item itself can’t hold a breakpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Different roles, different catalogs.&lt;/strong&gt; When different agent roles need different tools, choose a stable tool profile, such as support or billing, at the start of each session. Each profile gets its own cached prefix. Mark the shared instructions so later tasks with that profile can reuse them, then use either mode as the conversation grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cached tool profiles&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-6-1.png" alt="Support and billing sessions each reuse the cache for their fixed tool profile." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Each tool profile has its own cached prefix, reusable by matching sessions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="subagents-and-side-calls"&gt;Subagents and side calls&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Delegating shouldn’t make every helper reread the same background. Share the prefix, then add each helper’s task at the end. Be aware that when you use caching with AI agents, tools and system prompts can be leaked or exploited. Establish responsible AI practices that include your prompt caching implementation as part of that governance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Warm once, then fan out.&lt;/strong&gt; Send an ordinary request first and wait for it to finish before launching helpers. Mark the shared context explicitly in every request, even when the mode is implicit:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;shared = {"role": "developer", "content": [{
    "type": "input_text", "text": INSTRUCTIONS,
    "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"},
}]}
warm = client.responses.create(
    model="us.openai.gpt-6-astra", store=False,
    tools=TOOLS, prompt_cache_key=KEY,
    prompt_cache_options={"mode": "implicit", "ttl": "30m"},
    max_output_tokens=16,
    input=[shared, {"role": "user", "content": "Reply READY."}],
)
d = warm.usage.input_tokens_details
if not (d.cache_write_tokens or d.cached_tokens):
    raise RuntimeError("Shared prefix was not cached.")
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

# helper_tasks contains the task text for each helper.
helper_inputs = [
    [shared, {"role": "user", "content": task}]
    for task in helper_tasks
]
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as pool:
    helpers = list(pool.map(ask_with_implicit_cache, helper_inputs))&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The helpers use the same &lt;code&gt;TOOLS&lt;/code&gt;, model, and &lt;code&gt;KEY&lt;/code&gt; as the warm-up. A warm-up that ends at the output cap can still populate the cache. The usage check confirms cache activity before the helpers start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Subagents: Warm the shared prefix once, then fan out&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-7-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-7-1.png" alt="An ordinary warm-up request populates the shared prefix before four subagents start." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Warm the shared prefix before launching helpers, and each helper then adds its own task&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Give side calls the same beginning.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent loop builds &lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt; by appending every &lt;code&gt;resp.output&lt;/code&gt; and its matching tool results, including encrypted reasoning. After the parent finishes, copy that complete list for a summarizer or judge. The same helper keeps the tools, model, and key unchanged:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from copy import deepcopy

# history is the completed history built by the agent loop above.
# Copy it so the helper can add instructions without changing the parent.
helper_history = deepcopy(history)
helper_history.append({
    "role": "developer",
    "content": "Summarize this conversation in one sentence. Do not call tools.",
})
summary = ask_with_implicit_cache(helper_history)
d = summary.usage.input_tokens_details
print("history read", d.cached_tokens, "write", d.cache_write_tokens)
print(summary.output_text)

# Example output (assumed values and conversation):
# history read 4096 write 32
# Case C-123 is due on October 31, 2026.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The helper can reuse the cached portion of &lt;code&gt;history&lt;/code&gt;, but the appended developer message doesn’t automatically extend the cache to include the summary instruction. If later requests reuse the same history and instruction, place the instruction in an &lt;code&gt;input_text&lt;/code&gt; block with &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_breakpoint&lt;/code&gt;. For a one-time summary, no additional marker is needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="pauses-restarts-and-the-cache-key"&gt;Pauses, restarts, and the cache key&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pauses.&lt;/strong&gt; Cached prefixes have a minimum 30-minute time to live (TTL), refreshed by successful reuse, and might remain longer. If a session resumes soon, an ordinary warm-up can refresh the prefix before it expires. Compare that request’s cost with letting the cache expire. For the TTL rules, see the Amazon Bedrock documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New sessions.&lt;/strong&gt; Keep &lt;code&gt;prompt_cache_key&lt;/code&gt; stable for requests that are meant to share a prefix. A session-specific key isolates sessions even when their prompts match. In the following example, switching to a shared key let sessions reuse the prefix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New sessions reuse the cache only if they share the key&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-8-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-8-1.png" alt="The original experiment compares per-session cache keys with a stable shared key." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: A session-specific key separates cache entries, and a shared key lets matching sessions reuse them&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reuse of existing cache content requires the same model, AWS account, and Region, and an available matching prefix. The following example combines an explicit startup marker with implicit mode. Each session starts with the same instructions and its own task. Both use the same computed key:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import hashlib

def cache_key(app, model, prompt_version, instructions):
    digest = hashlib.sha256(instructions.encode()).hexdigest()[:12]
    return f"{app}:{prompt_version}:{model}:{digest}"

KEY = cache_key("support-agent", "us.openai.gpt-6-astra", "v7", INSTRUCTIONS)
prefix = {"role": "developer", "content": [{
    "type": "input_text", "text": INSTRUCTIONS,
    "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"},
}]}
for label, question in [
    ("session A", "Explain the refund policy without calling tools."),
    ("session B", "Explain the escalation policy without calling tools."),
]:
    # A fresh conversation for each session; the startup prefix matches.
    session_history = [prefix, {"role": "user", "content": question}]
    result = ask_with_implicit_cache(session_history)
    d = result.usage.input_tokens_details
    print(label, "read", d.cached_tokens, "write", d.cache_write_tokens)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On a cold start, session 1 writes the shared prefix. Session 2 can read it even though its task can be different. Each task can also be cached implicitly. Check the usage counters: matching keys and markers make reuse possible, but a cache entry still has to be available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-prompt-caching-delivers"&gt;What prompt caching delivers&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost.&lt;/strong&gt; For a GPT-6 Astra powered agent with an 8,000-token startup prefix and six turns adding 2,000 tokens each, one-time static caching reduced cost to $0.50, and rolling breakpoints reduced modeled input cost by 64 percent: from $0.87 to $0.32 per session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-9-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/23/ML-22246-9-1.png" alt="Line chart of cumulative input cost per session for the support-agent example on GPT-6 Astra." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Cumulative input cost per session for the support-agent example, using Amazon Bedrock on-demand prices for GPT-6 Astra&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-tell-caching-is-working"&gt;How to tell caching is working&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with response usage. &lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt; reports input read from the cache, and &lt;code&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/code&gt; reports input written to it. As the conversation grows, check that later turns reuse earlier history and that new writes reflect the content being added.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production monitoring on the Amazon Bedrock Runtime endpoint, use the &lt;code&gt;CacheReadInputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;CacheWriteInputTokenCount&lt;/code&gt; metrics in Amazon CloudWatch. Enable model invocation logging when you need individual request records, then query them with Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights. For the Amazon Bedrock Mantle endpoint, record the response usage fields in your application logs. Its Amazon CloudWatch namespace currently has no separate cache counters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To check costs, filter &lt;strong&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/strong&gt; to Amazon Bedrock and group by &lt;strong&gt;Usage type&lt;/strong&gt;. Cache reads, cache writes, and uncached input appear as separate entries per model. Exclude output-token entries when calculating their shares of input cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-short-checklist"&gt;A short checklist&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep the reusable prefix stable.&lt;/strong&gt; Put shared tools and instructions first, then append conversation history and new turns. Changing a timestamp, message, or tool definition prevents reuse beyond that change. A shorter prefix can still be reused if it has its own saved endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choose boundaries around content you want to reuse.&lt;/strong&gt; Use implicit caching for growing history, explicit markers for shared prefixes, or both. Explicit-only mode avoids writing disposable suffixes. When extending an explicit-only conversation, retain earlier markers so later requests can find the saved prefixes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep the initial tool catalog unchanged.&lt;/strong&gt; Use &lt;code&gt;allowed_tools&lt;/code&gt; to restrict which tools are callable. Append known definitions with &lt;code&gt;additional_tools&lt;/code&gt;, or discover them through namespaced client-side &lt;code&gt;tool_search&lt;/code&gt;. Choose a stable tool profile when different agent roles need different catalogs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Check prefix size and lifetime.&lt;/strong&gt; A cacheable prefix must contain at least 1,024 tokens. Cached prefixes remain eligible for reuse for at least 30 minutes after their latest write or successful reuse.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Warm shared content before launching helpers.&lt;/strong&gt; Mark the shared prefix in both the warm-up and helper requests, including when using implicit mode. Finish the warm-up request before starting parallel calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep cache keys stable within each sharing group.&lt;/strong&gt; Requests intended to reuse a prefix must use the same key. Separate keys can keep cache accounting distinct between customers or users.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Measure savings across the workflow.&lt;/strong&gt; Cache writes cost 1.25 times the normal input rate. Read prices depend on the model. Track write costs alongside read savings, using response usage during development and monitoring and billing data in production.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="waking-up-on-february-3rd"&gt;Waking up on February 3rd&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bill Murray only escapes Groundhog Day once he stops repeating the same mistakes and starts carrying what he learned from one day to the next. Agents get there in a similar, if less romantic, way: by keeping the start of their prompt byte-for-byte stable and using automatic or explicit cache boundaries as the history grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we covered how to cache shared tools and instructions, grow the cache across agent turns, and preserve reuse when adding tools, launching helpers, or starting new sessions. Keep the shared prefix stable and append new information as the workflow progresses. Use implicit caching for growing conversations and explicit breakpoints for shared content. Combine them when you need both, or choose explicit-only mode when a changing suffix is unlikely to be reused. Compare cache reads, writes, total input cost, and latency to find what works best for your agent. To apply these patterns, follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html#prompt-caching-openai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching documentation for OpenAI models&lt;/a&gt; and check &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model availability by AWS Region and endpoint&lt;/a&gt;. Start with one workflow and measure the savings as your conversations grow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
