 &lt;div class="blog-author-box" style="padding-top: 2.0em"&gt; 
  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21614-20.jpg" alt="Siddhant Sahu" width="100" height="133"&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Siddhant Sahu&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Siddhant is a Senior Product Manager at AWS, where he leads product development for Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base. He focuses on building and rolling out infrastructure software services that handle multi-modal, multi-lingual enterprise data through fully managed ingestion, parsing and agentic search pipelines, in a cost-efficient, accurate and secure way, leveraging state-of-the-art capabilities such as hybrid search, multi-shot retrieval, tool use and embedding techniques. His work enables thousands of enterprise customers and ISVs to build agents at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning on Amazon SageMaker AI: A hands-on guide</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/multi-turn-reinforcement-learning-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai-a-hands-on-guide/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Daniel Quang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 23 Oct 2026 17:48:54 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d4a0021627e124e1cbf32bd2a1426e1abfda6b33</guid>

					<description>Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning (MTRL) on Amazon SageMaker AI trains models on the outcome of a whole multi-step task rather than labeled steps. This hands-on guide walks the full pipeline using Wordle: deploying the agent and reward on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, preparing the dataset, training, and evaluating at serving settings.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Most fine-tuning teaches a model to produce a better answer to a single prompt. Agentic tasks are different. An agent that files an expense report or triages a support ticket takes a sequence of actions, and each step depends on what came before. There’s rarely one correct sequence to imitate, and the only reliable feedback is whether the whole sequence succeeded. Supervised fine-tuning has no labels for that, and single-turn reinforcement learning cannot credit a decision made three steps before the outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multi-Turn Reinforcement Learning (MTRL) on Amazon SageMaker AI trains models on exactly that signal. SageMaker AI sends prompts to an agent you host on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, or on your own infrastructure, such as Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS), Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2), or AWS Fargate, through an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-agent.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda forwarder&lt;/a&gt;. The example here uses AgentCore. The agent calls the policy model being trained, acts in your environment over as many turns as the task needs, and reports one reward when the episode ends. You don’t specify the right trajectory, only how to score the result.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post uses &lt;a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wordle" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Wordle&lt;/a&gt;, a game in which a player has six tries to guess a hidden five-letter word, as the example. After each guess, every letter turns green if it is in the right position, yellow if it’s in the word but in a different position, and gray if it isn’t in the word. The game suits MTRL because the rules are few, each guess depends on the feedback from the last, and the outcome is scored objectively. Wordle is a teaching example, and frontier models already play it well. In practice, you would use MTRL to make a small, low-cost model good at a multi-step task specific to your business, such as an internal workflow that public models were never trained on. The same pipeline applies to that setting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our run, MTRL took gpt-oss-20b from a 61 to an 84 percent solve rate on held-out words at its default reasoning effort, and from 15 to 69 percent at the low effort it trained at, for about $339 in training tokens. In this post, we walk through the pipeline end-to-end: deploying the agent and reward to AgentCore, preparing the dataset, training, monitoring in MLflow, and evaluating at serving settings. We also share the reward-design mistakes and training failures we hit. Your datasets, agent, and trained model stay in your AWS account and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;are not used to train the base models&lt;/a&gt;. All code is in the accompanying &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system has two halves. SageMaker AI owns the training job and the policy model. AgentCore hosts the agent, the Wordle environment, and the reward function. The following diagram shows how they connect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-1.png" alt="SageMaker AI training job connected to the AgentCore-hosted agent, Wordle environment, and reward function" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: MTRL on SageMaker AI, where the training job invokes the AgentCore-hosted agent once per rollout to sample the policy model, play the game, and report a reward&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For every rollout, the MTRL service invokes the AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with one training row, which holds one secret word. A Strands agent in the runtime calls the policy model through the SageMaker Job Runtime’s OpenAI-compatible endpoint. The model plays up to six guesses through one tool, &lt;code&gt;guess_word&lt;/code&gt;, and after each guess the environment returns per-letter feedback and a summary of what is known. When the game ends, the agent returns one reward. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03300" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)&lt;/a&gt; uses the spread of rewards across several rollouts of the same word to update a Low-Rank Adaptation (&lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LoRA&lt;/a&gt;) adapter on the policy model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three design points matter for what follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The agent and reward live in one place.&lt;/strong&gt; A single Python file deployed to AgentCore holds the environment, the tool, and the scoring function.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The dataset carries only the secret word.&lt;/strong&gt; The word lists bundled with the agent are built from the open-source &lt;a href="http://wordlist.aspell.net/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SCOWL&lt;/a&gt; word lists and are used only for scoring.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The reward is outcome-dominant.&lt;/strong&gt; A solve pays 1.05–1.55 depending on speed, a loss pays -0.5, and never making a valid guess pays -1.5. A small shaping term rewards good strategy within each tier.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="supported-models-and-regions"&gt;Supported models and AWS Regions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MTRL is serverless: you pick a base model and the service provisions the training and sampling infrastructure. At the time of writing, it supports the following base models, each trained with a LoRA adapter:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Base model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Regions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Nova 2 Lite&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US East (N. Virginia) (us-east-1), US West (Oregon) (us-west-2)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;GPT-OSS-20B&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US East (N. Virginia) (us-east-1), US West (Oregon) (us-west-2)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gemma 4 31B (instruction-tuned)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US West (Oregon) (us-west-2)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Qwen 3.6 27B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;US West (Oregon) (us-west-2)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We chose &lt;code&gt;GPT-OSS-20B&lt;/code&gt; because it has the lowest per-token price of the four and its reasoning effort is controllable per request. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MTRL documentation&lt;/a&gt; for the current list.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pricing"&gt;Pricing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You don’t choose instances or size a cluster. You pay per million tokens in three dimensions, priced by base model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prefill:&lt;/strong&gt; input tokens the policy model processes. These accumulate in a multi-turn task, because every turn re-reads the history.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sample:&lt;/strong&gt; tokens the policy model generates, including reasoning tokens.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Train:&lt;/strong&gt; tokens processed in the backward pass that updates the LoRA adapter.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The 100-step run consumed 491.6 million prefill, 159.7 million sample, and 643.3 million train tokens across 105,486 rollouts in 12 hours 40 minutes. At &lt;code&gt;GPT-OSS-20B&lt;/code&gt; list prices in the US East (N. Virginia) Region (us-east-1) at the time of writing, that is about $339:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tokens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Price per 1M&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prefill&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;491,616,806&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.12&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$58.99&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sample&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;159,737,098&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.30&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$47.92&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Train&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;643,308,554&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.36&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$231.59&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Total&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1,294,662,458&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;$338.50&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The train dimension dominates and scales with trajectory length, so shorter games and less verbose reasoning cut the bill. The AgentCore runtime bills separately for the vCPU and memory the agent uses. For this run, that was about $79, from the runtime’s Amazon CloudWatch usage metrics at us-east-1 list prices. &lt;code&gt;DescribeJob&lt;/code&gt; reports the token counts under &lt;code&gt;BillableTokenUsage&lt;/code&gt;. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt; for current rates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you need the following. See also the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-prereqs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MTRL prerequisites&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10 or later, uv, Node 20 or later, and the AgentCore CLI (&lt;code&gt;npm install -g @aws/agentcore&lt;/code&gt;). See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Get started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; for installation and the IAM permissions the CLI needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;botocore 1.43.37 or later, on the machine that launches the job. This is the first version that includes the &lt;code&gt;AgentRFT&lt;/code&gt; &lt;code&gt;CreateJob&lt;/code&gt; operation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Two AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles, described in Step 1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket in the same Region as the agent. The training job must also run in that Region, because &lt;code&gt;CreateJob&lt;/code&gt; rejects a cross-Region runtime ARN.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-1-create-the-iam-roles"&gt;Step 1: Create the IAM roles&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MTRL uses two roles, and one of them has a trust policy that the general SageMaker AI documentation doesn’t mention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent runtime role.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt; creates this role for you. It trusts &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt; and needs the &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerJobRuntimeAccess&lt;/code&gt; managed policy, which lets the agent call the policy model and report rewards. The CDK stack in the sample repository attaches this policy automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Training job role.&lt;/strong&gt; You create this one. It must trust &lt;code&gt;job.sagemaker.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt; with both &lt;code&gt;sts:AssumeRole&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;sts:TagSession&lt;/code&gt;, and it needs the &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerJobFullAccess&lt;/code&gt; managed policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [{
        "Effect": "Allow",
        "Principal": {"Service": "job.sagemaker.amazonaws.com"},
        "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"]
    }]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The trust principal is &lt;code&gt;job.sagemaker.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;, not &lt;code&gt;sagemaker.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;. With the generic principal, &lt;code&gt;CreateJob&lt;/code&gt; fails with “Could not assume role,” and the message names the correct principal only after retries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-2-deploy-the-agent-to-amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Step 2: Deploy the agent to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;, set your account and Region in Wordle/agentcore/&lt;code&gt;aws-targets.json&lt;/code&gt;, and deploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle
cd sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle/Wordle
agentcore validate
agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The CLI builds the agent’s dependencies for the runtime’s Linux ARM64 platform, creates the execution role with &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerJobRuntimeAccess&lt;/code&gt; attached, and deploys through AWS CloudFormation. We recommend it over uploading a code package on the console, where it’s common to get the roughly 700 MB of compiled dependencies or the policy attachment wrong. Note the runtime ARN in the output for the training job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-2.png" alt="Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console showing the Wordle agent runtime with status Ready" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Wordle agent runtime in the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console with status Ready&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The runtime reads two environment variables from &lt;code&gt;agentcore.json&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;REASONING_EFFORT=low&lt;/code&gt; sets &lt;code&gt;reasoning_effort&lt;/code&gt; on every request to the policy model. In our evaluation jobs, low effort used 63 to 83 percent fewer sample tokens per game than medium. &lt;code&gt;REASONING_PROMPT_HINT=true&lt;/code&gt; also adds “Reasoning: low” to the system prompt, which is how gpt-oss reads the setting in its Harmony chat format. Neither variable gives the model any hint about the secret word.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-3.png" alt="AgentCore console environment variables panel with REASONING_EFFORT set to low" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Runtime environment variables showing REASONING_EFFORT set to low&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent is one file, &lt;code&gt;main.py&lt;/code&gt;. Simplified, its entrypoint looks like the following. For your own task, you change the task parsing, the tools, and the reward:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;app = BedrockAgentCoreApp()

@app.entrypoint  # outermost: starts the HTTP server AgentCore invokes
@sagemaker_rft_handler  # reports the returned reward to SageMaker AI
def handle_rollout(payload):
    prompt, answer = parse_task(payload["prompt"])
    game = WordleGame(answer)

    @tool
    def guess_word(guess: str) -&amp;gt; str:
        """Submit a 5-letter Wordle guess and get feedback."""
        return game.guess(guess)

    agent = Agent(model=build_model(), system_prompt=SYSTEM_PROMPT, tools=[guess_word])
    agent(prompt)
    return {"reward": game.final_reward()}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;code&gt;sagemaker_rft_handler&lt;/code&gt; decorator does not start an HTTP server by itself. Wrap it with &lt;code&gt;BedrockAgentCoreApp&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;@app.entrypoint&lt;/code&gt;, with the entrypoint decorator outermost. Without this, every invocation times out after 30 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-3-verify-the-runtime"&gt;Step 3: Verify the runtime&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before spending GPU time, confirm the whole chain works with a payload shaped like the one the training service sends. The repository includes one as Wordle/&lt;code&gt;payload.json&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "prompt": "{\"prompt\": \"Guess the 5-letter word\", \"answer\": \"beach\", \"id\": \"smoke-test\"}",
  "metadata": {
    "jobArn": "arn:aws:sagemaker:us-east-1:111122223333:job/AgentRFT/smoke-test",
    "rolloutId": "r1"
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fake job ARN is expected to fail, and the way it fails is the proof. Put your own account ID into it first: for a job ARN in another account, the service returns a 403 before it checks whether the job exists, which looks like a broken IAM setup. From the Wordle directory, run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
sed "s/111122223333/$ACCOUNT/" payload.json &amp;gt; /tmp/payload.json

aws bedrock-agentcore invoke-agent-runtime \
  --agent-runtime-arn &amp;lt;RUNTIME_ARN&amp;gt; \
  --runtime-session-id smoke-test-$(date +%s)000000000000000 \
  --payload fileb:///tmp/payload.json /dev/stdout&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the runtime’s Amazon CloudWatch logs, you see the agent parse the prompt, authenticate to the Job Runtime endpoint, and receive a 400 error reading “Could not find job.” That failure confirms that parsing, authentication, and error reporting work. The command itself prints {“reward”: -1.5}, because the agent never reached the policy model. A 403 reading “not authorized to access this resource” means the job ARN still names another account. A 30-second timeout instead means the HTTP server is not starting (see the Important note in Step 2).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-4-prepare-the-dataset"&gt;Step 4: Prepare the dataset&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MTRL dataset has no target responses, transcripts, or labels. Each row is one task for the agent to attempt. Here are the first three rows of the training file:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"prompt": "{\"prompt\": \"Guess the 5-letter word\", \"answer\": \"riser\", \"id\": \"wordle_train_0000\"}"}
{"prompt": "{\"prompt\": \"Guess the 5-letter word\", \"answer\": \"swoon\", \"id\": \"wordle_train_0001\"}"}
{"prompt": "{\"prompt\": \"Guess the 5-letter word\", \"answer\": \"dirty\", \"id\": \"wordle_train_0002\"}"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The service reads the &lt;code&gt;prompt&lt;/code&gt; column (or the first column) and passes the string to your agent verbatim, without parsing it. We made the string a small JSON object with three fields:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;prompt:&lt;/strong&gt; the instruction the agent shows the policy model, identical on every row.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;answer:&lt;/strong&gt; the secret word. Only the environment and the reward function use it. The policy model never sees it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;id:&lt;/strong&gt; a row ID for tracing a rollout in the logs back to its source row.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample repository’s &lt;code&gt;make_dataset.py&lt;/code&gt; draws 640 training words (five full batches of 128) and 128 validation words from the answer list with no overlap, so the validation solve rate measures generalization. The service also accepts Apache Parquet, JSON arrays, and CSV. Upload both files to Amazon S3:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run python make_dataset.py
aws s3 cp training-data.jsonl s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/train/
aws s3 cp validation-data.jsonl s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/validation/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the service never inspects the &lt;code&gt;prompt&lt;/code&gt; string, a production task can pack a full message list, tool configuration, or reward specification into it. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-assets.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MTRL documentation&lt;/a&gt; for examples, including how to protect sensitive content in the string.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-5-launch-the-training-job"&gt;Step 5: Launch the training job&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The launcher uses the &lt;code&gt;MultiTurnRLTrainer&lt;/code&gt; class from the &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-train&lt;/code&gt; SDK. Point it at the runtime ARN from Step 2, the role from Step 1, the datasets from Step 4, and an MLflow app for metrics. The MLflow app is required, so if you don’t have one, create it first with &lt;code&gt;aws sagemaker create-mlflow-app&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run python run_mtrl_training.py \
  --model openai-reasoning-gpt-oss-20b \
  --agent-runtime-arn &amp;lt;RUNTIME_ARN&amp;gt; \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:role/&amp;lt;JOB_ROLE&amp;gt; \
  --train-dataset s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/train/training-data.jsonl \
  --val-dataset s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/validation/validation-data.jsonl \
  --s3-output-path s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/output/ \
  --mlflow-app-arn arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:mlflow-app/&amp;lt;APP_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The launcher overrides three hyperparameters and leaves every other one at the gpt-oss-20b default. It sets &lt;code&gt;sampling_max_tokens&lt;/code&gt; to 8192, the service maximum, because at the default of 4096 gpt-oss often ran out of tokens while reasoning, before its first tool call. It sets &lt;code&gt;max_epochs&lt;/code&gt; to 20 and &lt;code&gt;max_steps&lt;/code&gt; to 100: 640 rows at the default &lt;code&gt;global_batch_size&lt;/code&gt; of 128 is 5 steps per epoch, and with the default &lt;code&gt;group_size&lt;/code&gt; of 8, each step plays 1,024 games. The job moves through Starting, Downloading, and Pending before reaching Training. After it’s training, your AgentCore runtime begins receiving rollout invocations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; Don’t redeploy the agent while a job is running. Redeploying restarts the runtime, in-flight rollouts see no sampling requests, and the service fails the entire job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-6-monitor-training-in-mlflow"&gt;Step 6: Monitor training in MLflow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The service logs reward, token, and turn statistics at every step and runs validation every &lt;code&gt;val_every&lt;/code&gt; steps. The metric to watch is &lt;code&gt;val/reward/pass_at_1&lt;/code&gt;, the fraction of held-out words solved. Every solve scores at least 1.01, so it clears the service’s &lt;code&gt;success_threshold&lt;/code&gt; of 1.0 and pass@1 counts it. The following chart is built from this run’s MLflow history:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-4.png" alt="Two line charts tracking mean reward per game and validation solve rate over 100 training steps" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Training curves for the 100-step run, with mean reward per game on the left and validation solve rate on the right&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training reward climbs to about +0.57 within 15 steps and holds there. Validation reward stays lower, mostly because of temperature 1.0 versus 0 rather than overfitting (see Step 7). The validation solve rate jumps from 2 to 31 percent in the first 10 steps, then moves between 35 and 48 percent. With 128 words, those swings are mostly noise. We did not test whether more steps help at serving settings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The runtime’s OpenTelemetry traces in CloudWatch record every model turn and tool call. Here is one game the trained model played during an evaluation job (medium effort, temperature 1.0), with the reasoning trimmed. The secret word was PASTE:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;[model] Reasoning: [...] "CRANE" has 5 distinct letters: C,R,A,N,E. [...]
We'll guess "CRANE".
Tool call: guess_word(guess="CRANE")
[tool] Guess 1/6: CRANE -&amp;gt; C=gray, R=gray, A=yellow, N=gray, E=green
Current Knowledge:
- Correct Position (Green): _ _ _ _ E
- In Word, Wrong Position (Yellow): 'A' (at least 1)
- Not in Word (Gray): C, N, R
- Guesses Remaining: 5
[model] Reasoning: [...] "ALIVE": ends with E, includes A. Letters not C,N,R.
Tool call: guess_word(guess="ALIVE")
[tool] Guess 2/6: ALIVE -&amp;gt; A=yellow, L=gray, I=gray, V=gray, E=green
[model] Reasoning: [...] So positions not 1 or 3 for A. Positions left: 2,4
for A. [...] We might guess "HASTE"? [...]
Tool call: guess_word(guess="HASTE")
[tool] Guess 3/6: HASTE -&amp;gt; H=gray, A=green, S=green, T=green, E=green
[model] Reasoning: We have the word: _ASTE. [...] "PASTE" is a word. PASTE
fits. Also "WASTE" also fits [...] Choose "PASTE" maybe.
Tool call: guess_word(guess="PASTE")
[tool] Guess 4/6: PASTE --- all green. Correct, you solved it!&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A trained-model rollout from the AgentCore runtime traces. The model keeps green letters in place, never reuses a gray letter, and uses each yellow letter to rule out positions, though nothing in the training data showed it how.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-7-evaluate-the-trained-model-at-serving-settings"&gt;Step 7: Evaluate the trained model at serving settings&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the job completes, its page in SageMaker AI Studio shows the status and the model package group that holds the trained model. &lt;code&gt;DescribeJob&lt;/code&gt;, with &lt;code&gt;JobCategory&lt;/code&gt; set to &lt;code&gt;AgentRFT&lt;/code&gt;, returns the output model package ARN and the billable token counts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-5.png" alt="SageMaker AI Studio Jobs page showing the completed multi-turn RL training job" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The completed training job in SageMaker AI Studio on the Jobs, Training, Multi-turn RL tab&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-evaluation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;evaluation job&lt;/a&gt; (&lt;code&gt;JobCategory&lt;/code&gt; set to &lt;code&gt;AgentRFTEvaluation&lt;/code&gt;) plays your evaluation prompts through the same AgentCore runtime and reward, on the base model or the trained package, and logs &lt;code&gt;eval/reward/pass_at_1&lt;/code&gt; to MLflow. Unlike training validation, you can set the temperature. The repository’s launcher runs evaluations back to back, because the default quota allows one evaluation job at a time (&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-quotas.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;you can request an increase&lt;/a&gt;):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run python run_mtrl_eval.py --runs base:1.0 trained:1.0 \
  --model-package-arn &amp;lt;OUTPUT_MODEL_PACKAGE_ARN&amp;gt; \
  --agent-runtime-arn &amp;lt;RUNTIME_ARN&amp;gt; \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:role/&amp;lt;JOB_ROLE&amp;gt; \
  --dataset s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/validation/validation-data.jsonl \
  --s3-output-path s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/eval/ \
  --mlflow-app-arn arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:mlflow-app/&amp;lt;APP_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-6.png" alt="Reasoning effort and sampling temperature at each stage: training low and 1.0, validation low and 0 fixed by the service, evaluation jobs at four settings with serving at medium and 1.0." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Reasoning effort and sampling temperature at each stage, where the training job fixes validation at temperature 0 and the deployed effort while evaluation jobs let you choose&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each 128-word evaluation took four to seven minutes. Reasoning effort comes from the agent’s environment variables, so to change it you edit &lt;code&gt;REASONING_EFFORT&lt;/code&gt;, redeploy, and evaluate. We evaluated both models at low and medium effort and at temperature 0 and 1.0:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/12/ML-22014-7.png" alt="Bar charts comparing base and trained model solve rate and mean reward across four decoding settings" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Base model versus trained model under four decoding settings, with solve rate on the left and mean reward per game on the right&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gray segment of each bar is the base model and the blue segment is the gain from training. The low-effort, temperature-0 pair is the training job’s validation at steps 0 and 100. The medium-effort, temperature-1.0 trained result is the mean of two runs (80 and 88 percent).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At temperature 0, both models often end the game without a solve, at either effort. With sampling on, medium effort adds 15 points for the trained model and 46 for the base model. The adapter adds 14–54 points in every setting, so what training taught transfers across settings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The training job’s own validation curve in MLflow told a different story: it peaked at 48 percent at step 90 and ended at 37 percent, scored at temperature 0. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl-hyperparams.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;hyperparameters reference&lt;/a&gt; recommends temperature 0 for evaluation because greedy decoding makes curves deterministic and comparable across runs. We report temperature 1.0 because that’s how we serve the model. At medium effort and temperature 1.0, the trained model solves 84 percent of held-out words, up from 61 percent for the base model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tip:&lt;/strong&gt; Evaluate at the settings you will serve at. The training curve says this adapter ends at 37 percent. At serving settings it solves 84 percent. The evaluation jobs that show this cost about $1.20 in tokens and took about an hour.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-8-clean-up"&gt;Step 8: Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Training and evaluation jobs stop billing when they complete, but the AgentCore runtime, its IAM role, the MLflow app, and the S3 objects persist. From the project directory:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# 1. Agent runtime and its execution role. remove all resets the project config;
# the next deploy sees the empty state and tears down the deployed resources.
cd sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle/Wordle
agentcore remove all
agentcore deploy

# 2. MLflow app
aws sagemaker delete-mlflow-app --arn arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:mlflow-app/&amp;lt;APP_ID&amp;gt;

# 3. Datasets, job output, and evaluation output
aws s3 rm s3://&amp;lt;YOUR_BUCKET&amp;gt;/wordle-mtrl/ --recursive&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because &lt;code&gt;agentcore remove all&lt;/code&gt; rewrites the files under &lt;code&gt;agentcore/&lt;/code&gt;, run &lt;code&gt;git checkout -- agentcore/&lt;/code&gt; if you want to redeploy later. Deleting the &lt;code&gt;AgentCore-Wordle-default&lt;/code&gt; CloudFormation stack removes the same resources and leaves the files alone. The training job role and the model packages are not billed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deep-dive-designing-the-reward"&gt;Deep dive: Designing the reward&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our reward needed one revision, and the mistake is a common one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first design summed the per-guess scoring from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/charbull/wordle-lora-rl" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;wordle-lora-rl&lt;/a&gt; project over the whole game. That scoring works there because each guess is an independent training sample. Summed into one reward per game, it produced two inversions we found only by reading traces:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A lost game paid positive reward.&lt;/strong&gt; Six turns of bonuses added up to about +0.49.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Solving on turn 1 scored below solving on turn 3.&lt;/strong&gt; A first-guess solve earned +0.99 and a third-guess solve earned +1.61, because the extra turns collected more bonuses.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fix separates a dominant outcome term from a small, bounded shaping term:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;reward = outcome + w * clip(shaping, -1, 1), w = 0.04 if solved, else 0.15&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The outcome term is set entirely by how the game ended:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Outcome&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Solved on guess n&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.05 + 0.5 * (6 – n) / 5, so 1.55 on guess 1 down to 1.05 on guess 6&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Played all six guesses, unsolved&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;-0.5&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Never made a valid guess&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;-1.5&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The shaping term is that per-guess scoring, summed over the game:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Penalties for contradicting a known green, yellow, or gray letter, for repeating a guess, and for words outside the dictionary.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An entropy bonus for a strong opening word.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Bonuses for trying new letters and for shrinking the set of remaining possibilities.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sum is clipped to [-1, 1] and scaled by 0.04 for a solve and 0.15 for a loss, so it separates games with the same outcome but never moves a game into another tier. With typical shaping values:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reward&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Solve on guess 1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+1.550&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Solve on guess 3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+1.375&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Solve on guess 6&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+1.069&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Lost after six guesses&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;-0.427&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Never made a valid guess&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;-1.500&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A faster solve now always beats a slower one, every solve beats every loss, and never playing is the worst outcome. Our first fix used a single weight of 0.15, which let a guess-3 solve with full shaping (+1.45) outscore a guess-2 solve with the minimum (+1.25). Adjacent guess counts are 0.1 apart, so a smaller solve weight of 0.04 removes the overlap, and the worst solve (+1.01) still clears the 1.0 success threshold. Shaping still matters because GRPO learns from reward variance within a group, so two lost games that differ in clue consistency still teach the model something. As the SageMaker AI documentation advises, start with an outcome-based reward, add shaping sparingly, and check the reward on real trajectories.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deep-dive-what-went-wrong-in-the-order-you-will-hit-it"&gt;Deep dive: What went wrong, in the order you will hit it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of these cost at least one training run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bearer tokens under concurrency.&lt;/strong&gt; Minting a token with a fresh botocore session per call starved the credential provider at 32 concurrent rollouts, and caching tokens for an hour returned 403 errors as credentials rotated. The fix is one self-refreshing session, a short cache, and a re-sign on 403.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A rollout that never reaches the policy model fails the whole job.&lt;/strong&gt; When every model call in a rollout failed authentication, the service ended the job with “No sampling requests were received.” Returning an error status fails it too, so always return a reward. A game where the model is sampled but never makes a valid guess is fine: it scores -1.5, and the 100-step run had 51 of them in about 105,000 rollouts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool calls drift into plain text.&lt;/strong&gt; At too high a learning rate, the model wrote tool calls as text, every rollout got the same floor reward, and the gradient vanished. The agent now recovers guesses from raw text with a small formatting penalty.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The plateau. Training reward stopped rising after about step 15 and validation stopped improving after about step 30, though at temperature 0 validation understates the model (Step 7).&lt;/strong&gt; A curriculum that seeds games with prior guesses, or more rollouts per word than the default &lt;code&gt;group_size&lt;/code&gt; of 8, is what we would try next.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MTRL on SageMaker AI trains a model on the outcome of a whole episode. That fits agentic tasks, where the right trajectory is unknown and only the result can be scored. We used a single episode-level reward. When intermediate steps can be scored too, MTRL also accepts one reward per turn through the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_job_runtime_UpdateReward.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateReward API&lt;/a&gt;. In this post, an open-weight 20B model reached an 84 percent solve rate at serving settings, up from 61 percent, for about $339 in training tokens and about $79 in AgentCore runtime usage. The 37 percent the training curve reports is an artifact of how the training job scores validation, so evaluate at the settings you will serve at.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same pipeline applies to internal procedures, tools, and workflows that public models were never trained on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-turn-rl-wordle" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;, follow the preceding steps, and run the smoke test in Step 3 before committing GPU time. For the underlying APIs, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/model-customize-mtrl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI MTRL documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Orchestrating SageMaker AI endpoint auto scaling with AWS CDK</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/orchestrating-sagemaker-ai-endpoint-auto-scaling-with-aws-cdk/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Lauren Eterno]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 23 Oct 2026 16:30:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">99e1f40843e42eddcc6ecfc9f758bee6b1d7c9a3</guid>

					<description>Updating a SageMaker AI endpoint that has Application Auto Scaling registered makes AWS CloudFormation and AWS CDK deployments fail. This post shows how to build a reusable AWS CDK construct with CloudFormation custom resources that deregisters and re-registers auto scaling automatically, so you can update the endpoint and its scaling in a single deployment.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Updating the Amazon Sagemaker AI real-time endpoint configuration (changing the instance type, instance count, or environment variables) causes deployments through AWS CloudFormation or the AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) to fail. SageMaker AI requires you to deregister the scalable target before updating the endpoint, but when you deregister it, CloudFormation can’t track the AWS Application Auto Scaling resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to use a reusable CDK construct with AWS CloudFormation custom resources to orchestrate deregister and register auto scaling automatically during endpoint updates. You can update your endpoint configuration and auto scaling parameters in a single deployment without manual intervention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-this-problem-occurs"&gt;Why this problem occurs&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you deploy a SageMaker AI endpoint with native CloudFormation auto scaling resources, then change the endpoint configuration and redeploy, several failure modes emerge. The specific failure depends on what you change and how you work around the issue.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="changing-the-endpoint-configuration-with-autoscaling-registered"&gt;Changing the endpoint configuration with auto scaling registered&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CloudFormation tries to update the endpoint while the scalable target is still registered. SageMaker AI rejects the update with the following error:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;The variant(s) "[AllTraffic]" must be deregistered as scalable targets with Application Auto Scaling before they can be removed or have their instance type updated.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI blocks the endpoint update before CloudFormation reaches the auto scaling resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="manually-deregistering-autoscaling-then-changing-only-the-endpoint-configuration"&gt;Manually deregistering auto scaling, then changing only the endpoint configuration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deregister the scalable target through the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) and redeploy with only an endpoint configuration change, the deployment succeeds. However, CloudFormation doesn’t register auto scaling even though it is in the CloudFormation template. Because the auto scaling parameters in the CloudFormation template haven’t changed, CloudFormation doesn’t register the scalable target or re-apply the scaling policy. The endpoint is now running without auto scaling, and there is no error to indicate anything went wrong.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="manually-deregistering-autoscaling-then-changing-autoscaling-parameters"&gt;Manually deregistering auto scaling, then changing auto scaling parameters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deregister the scalable target through the AWS CLI and redeploy with auto scaling parameter changes, CloudFormation tries to update the ScalableTarget resource that no longer exists:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;Resource of type 'AWS::ApplicationAutoScaling::ScalableTarget' with identifier 'endpoint/&amp;lt;name&amp;gt;/variant/AllTraffic|sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount|sagemaker' was not found.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This failure is notable because the rollback also fails (UPDATE_ROLLBACK_FAILED), leaving the stack in a broken state that requires manual intervention in the CloudFormation console to recover.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use this CDK construct to replace the native &lt;code&gt;AWS::ApplicationAutoScaling::ScalableTarget&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;AWS::ApplicationAutoScaling::ScalingPolicy&lt;/code&gt; CloudFormation resources with three custom resources. These custom resources deregister and register auto scaling around the endpoint update with explicit dependency ordering, enforced by &lt;code&gt;node.add_dependency&lt;/code&gt; calls in CDK. The full sequence is:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deregister&lt;/strong&gt; (custom resource): Removes the scalable target before the endpoint update begins. On first deploy, no scalable target exists yet, so the handler handles the error gracefully and continues.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Endpoint update&lt;/strong&gt; (CDK-managed): CDK updates the endpoint resource normally, swapping to the new endpoint configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Register Scalable Target&lt;/strong&gt; (custom resource): Waits for the endpoint to reach &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt; status, then registers the scalable target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attach Scaling Policy&lt;/strong&gt; (custom resource): Waits for the scalable target to be queryable, then applies the target tracking policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 573px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-20841-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-20841-1.png" alt="Four-step orchestration the construct runs during an endpoint update: deregister the scalable target, update the endpoint, register the scalable target, and attach the scaling policy" width="563"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Custom resource orchestration of SageMaker AI endpoint auto scaling around a CDK-managed endpoint update.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Register Scalable Target and Attach Scaling Policy use the CDK provider’s asynchronous completion pattern. Each custom resource has an &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; handler that returns immediately and an &lt;code&gt;is_complete&lt;/code&gt; handler. The provider’s waiter re-invokes the &lt;code&gt;is_complete&lt;/code&gt; handler on an interval (every 30 seconds) until its readiness check passes. The provider’s &lt;code&gt;total_timeout&lt;/code&gt; (1 hour for Register Scalable Target, 15 minutes for Attach Scaling Policy) bounds the wait, so it isn’t limited by the timeout of any single Lambda invocation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;code&gt;ConfigHash&lt;/code&gt; property on each custom resource forces CloudFormation to re-invoke the Lambda functions whenever a configuration value changes. The endpoint configuration and auto scaling parameters are defined together because a single &lt;code&gt;ConfigHash&lt;/code&gt; drives the auto scaling orchestration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While the construct defines three custom resources, the deployed stack contains more than three Lambda functions. Each custom resource is backed by a CDK &lt;code&gt;cr.Provider&lt;/code&gt;, and the provider framework generates its own helper Lambda functions. A synchronous provider (Deregister) adds one framework function. An asynchronous provider (Register Scalable Target and Attach Scaling Policy) adds three (&lt;code&gt;onEvent&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;isComplete&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;onTimeout&lt;/code&gt;) plus an AWS Step Functions state machine that drives the waiter. As a result, the deployed stack contains three custom resources, two Step Functions state machines, and twelve Lambda functions. Five of the resources hold the construct’s own handler code (one for Deregister, and an &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;is_complete&lt;/code&gt; pair for each asynchronous resource), with the remaining seven generated by the provider framework. You don’t write or maintain the framework-generated functions, but they are present in the stack and worth accounting for when you review the deployed resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this walkthrough, you deploy a SageMaker AI endpoint with auto scaling using the provided example stack, then verify that configuration updates work correctly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough includes the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the repository and install dependencies.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure your endpoint and auto scaling settings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy and verify.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Test an update.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The source code is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-endpoint-autoscaling" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;linked GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this walkthrough, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions for Amazon SageMaker AI, AWS Lambda, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), Application Auto Scaling, and Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cdk/v2/guide/getting_started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CDK v2&lt;/a&gt; installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.python.org/downloads/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Python 3.12&lt;/a&gt; or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.astral.sh/uv/getting-started/installation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;uv&lt;/a&gt; for Python dependency management.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A SageMaker AI model artifact in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and a container image in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), or a SageMaker AI built-in algorithm container.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="clone-the-repository-and-install-dependencies"&gt;Clone the repository and install dependencies&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the repository: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-sagemaker-endpoint-autoscaling.git
cd sample-sagemaker-endpoint-autoscaling&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Install dependencies: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv sync&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="configure-your-endpoint-and-autoscaling-settings"&gt;Configure your endpoint and auto scaling settings&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint and auto scaling settings are defined together in config/&lt;code&gt;config.yaml&lt;/code&gt; because a single &lt;code&gt;config_hash&lt;/code&gt; drives the auto scaling orchestration. A change to either section triggers the full sequence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Edit config/&lt;code&gt;config.yaml&lt;/code&gt; with your values:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;endpoint:
  endpoint_name: "my-model-endpoint"
  image: "123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/my-model:latest"
  model_data_url: "s3://my-bucket/models/model.tar.gz"
  instance_type: "ml.m5.large"
  initial_instance_count: 1
  environment: {}
autoscaling:
  min_capacity: 1
  max_capacity: 10
  target_value: 100
  scale_in_cooldown: 600
  scale_out_cooldown: 120&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The endpoint parameters map to the SageMaker AI &lt;code&gt;CreateModel&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;CreateEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt; APIs. The auto scaling parameters map to the Application Auto Scaling &lt;code&gt;RegisterScalableTarget&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;PutScalingPolicy&lt;/code&gt; APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-and-verify"&gt;Deploy and verify&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Bootstrap your AWS environment if you haven’t already: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run cdk bootstrap&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy the stack: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run cdk deploy InferenceStack&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CloudFormation creates the SageMaker AI model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint, then runs the three custom resources in sequence to register auto scaling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify that the deployment registered the scalable target: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws application-autoscaling describe-scalable-targets
--service-namespace sagemaker
--resource-ids endpoint/my-model-endpoint/variant/AllTraffic&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify that the deployment applied the scaling policy: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws application-autoscaling describe-scaling-policies
--service-namespace sagemaker
--resource-id endpoint/my-model-endpoint/variant/AllTraffic&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="test-an-update"&gt;Test an update&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Change a value in config/&lt;code&gt;config.yaml&lt;/code&gt;, for example, update the instance type from &lt;code&gt;ml.m5.large&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;ml.m5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, and redeploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run cdk deploy InferenceStack&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the deployment completes, verify that auto scaling is still registered and has the updated values:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws application-autoscaling describe-scalable-targets
--service-namespace sagemaker
--resource-ids endpoint/my-model-endpoint/variant/AllTraffic&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="integrating-with-an-existing-stack"&gt;Integrating with an existing stack&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the example stack if you’re starting fresh or want a reference implementation. Use the construct directly if you already have a CDK stack with SageMaker AI resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To integrate with an existing stack, copy the &lt;code&gt;sagemaker_autoscaling/constructs/&lt;/code&gt; directory into your project and add the construct alongside your endpoint resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json
from sagemaker_autoscaling.constructs.sagemaker_autoscaling_construct import (
    AutoscalingConfig,
    SagemakerAutoscalingConstruct,
    SagemakerAutoscalingProps,
)

# Both endpoint and auto scaling values must be in config_hash so that
# a change to either triggers the auto scaling orchestration.
config_hash = json.dumps({
    "endpoint": {
        "image": "123456789012.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/my-model:latest",
        "modelDataUrl": "s3://my-bucket/models/model.tar.gz",
        "instanceType": "ml.m5.large",
        "initialInstanceCount": 1,
    },
    "autoscaling": {
        "minCapacity": 1,
        "maxCapacity": 10,
        "targetValue": 100,
        "scaleInCooldown": 600,
        "scaleOutCooldown": 120,
    },
})

SagemakerAutoscalingConstruct(
    self, "Autoscaling",
    props=SagemakerAutoscalingProps(
        endpoint=your_endpoint,  # your CfnEndpoint
        autoscaling=AutoscalingConfig(
            min_capacity=1,
            max_capacity=10,
            target_value=100,
            scale_in_cooldown=600,
            scale_out_cooldown=120,
        ),
        config_hash=config_hash,
    ),
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-the-construct-works"&gt;How the construct works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section walks through the key implementation details of &lt;code&gt;SagemakerAutoscalingConstruct&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deregister"&gt;Deregister&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Deregister custom resource calls &lt;code&gt;deregister_scalable_target&lt;/code&gt; before the endpoint update. It handles &lt;code&gt;ObjectNotFoundException&lt;/code&gt; for first deployments where no scalable target exists:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def handler(event, context):
    resource_id = event["ResourceProperties"]["ResourceId"]
    if event["RequestType"] in ("Create", "Update"):
        try:
            client.deregister_scalable_target(
                ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
                ResourceId=resource_id,
                ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
            )
        except client.exceptions.ObjectNotFoundException:
            pass  # No scalable target on first deploy
    return {"PhysicalResourceId": f"deregister-{resource_id}"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The dependency is established in CDK:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;props.endpoint.node.add_dependency(deregister)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="register-scalable-target"&gt;Register Scalable Target&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Even though CloudFormation treats the endpoint resource as complete once the update is accepted, the scalable target can only be registered after the endpoint reaches &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt;. Rather than block a single Lambda invocation on that wait, Register Scalable Target uses the provider’s asynchronous completion pattern. The &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; handler returns immediately, and the provider’s waiter re-invokes the &lt;code&gt;is_complete&lt;/code&gt; handler on &lt;code&gt;query_interval&lt;/code&gt; until the endpoint is &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt;. The provider’s &lt;code&gt;total_timeout&lt;/code&gt; (1 hour) bounds the wait, not the timeout of any single Lambda invocation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def handler(event, context):
    props = event["ResourceProperties"]
    resource_id = props["ResourceId"]
    endpoint_name = resource_id.split("/")[1]
    if event["RequestType"] == "Delete":
        return {"IsComplete": True}
    status = sm_client.describe_endpoint(EndpointName=endpoint_name)["EndpointStatus"]
    if status == "Failed":
        raise Exception("Endpoint in Failed state")
    if status != "InService":
        return {"IsComplete": False}
    as_client.register_scalable_target(
        ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
        ResourceId=resource_id,
        ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
        MinCapacity=int(props["MinCapacity"]),
        MaxCapacity=int(props["MaxCapacity"]),
    )
    return {"IsComplete": True}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On stack deletion, the &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; handler deregisters the scalable target to clean up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="attach-scaling-policy"&gt;Attach Scaling Policy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There is a brief window after &lt;code&gt;RegisterScalableTarget&lt;/code&gt; returns where the target isn’t yet queryable. Attach Scaling Policy uses the same asynchronous completion pattern: the &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; handler returns immediately, and the &lt;code&gt;is_complete&lt;/code&gt; handler reports completion only once the scalable target is queryable, at which point it applies the policy. The provider’s &lt;code&gt;total_timeout&lt;/code&gt; (15 minutes) bounds the wait:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def handler(event, context):
    props = event["ResourceProperties"]
    resource_id = props["ResourceId"]
    if event["RequestType"] == "Delete":
        return {"IsComplete": True}
    resp = client.describe_scalable_targets(
        ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
        ResourceIds=[resource_id],
        ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
    )
    if not resp.get("ScalableTargets"):
        return {"IsComplete": False}
    client.put_scaling_policy(
        PolicyName=props["PolicyName"],
        ServiceNamespace="sagemaker",
        ResourceId=resource_id,
        ScalableDimension="sagemaker:variant:DesiredInstanceCount",
        PolicyType="TargetTrackingScaling",
        TargetTrackingScalingPolicyConfiguration=json.loads(props["ScalingPolicyConfig"]),
    )
    return {"IsComplete": True}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On stack deletion, the &lt;code&gt;on_event&lt;/code&gt; handler deletes the scaling policy to clean up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid incurring future charges, delete the resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run cdk destroy InferenceStack&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This removes the SageMaker AI endpoint, endpoint configuration, model, Lambda functions, IAM roles, Amazon CloudWatch alarms created by the target tracking scaling policy, and deregisters the scalable target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed you how to use a reusable CDK construct to deregister and register auto scaling automatically to orchestrate SageMaker AI endpoint updates. The construct uses three CloudFormation custom resources with explicit dependency ordering and a &lt;code&gt;ConfigHash&lt;/code&gt; property to keep auto scaling intact across deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, clone the &lt;code&gt;sample-sagemaker-endpoint-autoscaling&lt;/code&gt; repository and follow the setup instructions. The repository includes an example stack you can deploy directly, or you can integrate the construct into your existing CDK stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information about Application Auto Scaling for SageMaker AI, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/endpoint-auto-scaling.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automatically scale Amazon SageMaker AI models&lt;/a&gt;. For more information about CloudFormation custom resources, see Custom resources in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/lauren-eterno/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Lauren&lt;/a&gt; is an Associate Delivery Consultant with Amazon Web Services specializing in AI/ML. She works with customer teams to design, build, deploy, and scale machine learning and generative AI solutions. She holds a B.S. in Data Science &amp;amp; Analytics and an M.S. in Computer Science from Case Western Reserve University.&lt;/p&gt;
