			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Visualize points and routes with GeoJSON in Amazon Quick Sight layer maps</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/visualize-points-and-routes-with-geojson-in-amazon-quick-sight-layer-maps/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Amit Makashir]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 23 Oct 2026 16:24:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Sight]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">b00ee9ac34cfc25b65e7db16ebf768122bad4a1b</guid>

					<description>Amazon Quick Sight layer maps now support Point and LineString geometry from the same GeoJSON workflow, so you can plot store locations and the routes between them. This post covers the dataset and GeoJSON structure layer maps require, when to choose points or lines, and techniques for viewport control, layering geometries, and dynamic GeoJSON updates.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Quick Sight layer maps, you can now plot points and routes, like store locations across a city or flights leaving an airport. Geospatial maps help businesses spot patterns that are tied to location, like how a region is performing or where customers are concentrated. Amazon Quick Sight supports this with multiple map types, including point maps, filled maps, and layer maps. When &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/business-intelligence/create-custom-shape-maps-in-amazon-quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;layer maps first launched&lt;/a&gt;, you could upload a GeoJSON file to draw custom shape layers, such as sales territories or districts, and join those shapes to a dataset. That handled regions and boundaries well, but it didn’t cover the individual locations inside them or the routes running between them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Layer maps now support two more geometry types from the same GeoJSON workflow: &lt;em&gt;Point&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;LineString&lt;/em&gt;. Points mark specific locations, such as individual store or facility sites. A LineString traces the route between locations, like a delivery path or a flight between airports.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/quicksight/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Sight&lt;/a&gt;, the business intelligence (BI) capability of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;, is a unified BI service. It delivers modern interactive dashboards, natural language querying, pixel-perfect reports, machine learning (ML) insights, and embedded analytics at scale. Amazon Quick brings together AI agents for business insights, research, and automation in one integrated experience, helping you work smarter and faster while maintaining security and access policies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the dataset and GeoJSON structure that layer maps require. We also explain how points and lines work in a layer map visual and when to choose each. Finally, we cover advanced techniques for controlling the viewport, layering geometries, and updating GeoJSON files programmatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="dataset-structure-considerations"&gt;Dataset structure considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A layer map gets its shapes from a GeoJSON file and its metrics from a Quick Sight dataset, and a shared key joins the two. How you structure each one determines what the map can show. This section covers what you need before you start and how a GeoJSON file is organized.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following are the prerequisites that you need before creating a layer map in Quick Sight:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A GeoJSON file describing your geometry.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Quick Sight dataset holding the metrics and dimensions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A join key shared between the Quick Sight dataset and the GeoJSON file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The GeoJSON file must be saved in .geojson format, with a maximum supported file size of 100 MB.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="structure-of-the-geojson-file"&gt;Structure of the GeoJSON file&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GeoJSON is a JSON-based format for describing geographic features such as points and lines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "type": "FeatureCollection",
  "features": [{
    "type": "Feature",
    "geometry": {
      "type": "Point",
      "coordinates": [88.4, 22.6]
    },
    "properties": { "prop0": "value0" }
  }, {
    "type": "Feature",
    "geometry": {
      "type": "LineString",
      "coordinates": [
        [88.2, 22.4],
        [88.9, 23.1],
        [89.5, 22.8],
        [90.1, 23.6]
      ]
    },
    "properties": { "prop0": "value0" }
  }]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The structure of the file is as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;FeatureCollection&lt;/strong&gt; is the whole file. It has a type of &lt;strong&gt;FeatureCollection&lt;/strong&gt; and a &lt;strong&gt;features&lt;/strong&gt; array.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Feature&lt;/strong&gt; is a single map element. It carries a &lt;strong&gt;geometry&lt;/strong&gt; (the shape) and a &lt;strong&gt;properties&lt;/strong&gt; object, which holds the join key.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;geometry&lt;/strong&gt; has a &lt;strong&gt;type&lt;/strong&gt; (Point, LineString, or Polygon) and its &lt;strong&gt;coordinates&lt;/strong&gt;. The &lt;strong&gt;coordinates&lt;/strong&gt; hold a single &lt;strong&gt;[longitude, latitude]&lt;/strong&gt; position for a &lt;strong&gt;Point&lt;/strong&gt;, and an ordered list of positions for a &lt;strong&gt;LineString&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configuring-the-quick-sight-dataset-and-its-geojson"&gt;Configuring the Quick Sight dataset and its GeoJSON&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Quick Sight, when you configure a layer in a layer map visual, there are two primary settings to consider. The first is the &lt;strong&gt;Geometry type to visualize&lt;/strong&gt;, which sets which geometry from the GeoJSON file renders: Points, Lines, or Polygons. A layer renders one geometry type at a time, so when a file mixes types like the preceding example, only Features of the selected type appear on the map. The second is matching the &lt;strong&gt;Shape file key field&lt;/strong&gt; to the &lt;strong&gt;Dataset key field&lt;/strong&gt;, which links each Feature in the GeoJSON to a row in your Quick Sight dataset. The field names don’t need to be identical, but the values do. The following two examples show this end to end, first a &lt;strong&gt;LineString&lt;/strong&gt; layer and then a &lt;strong&gt;Point&lt;/strong&gt; layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="example-linestring-layer"&gt;Example: LineString layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider example data showing a flight from JFK to a destination city like Los Angeles. The GeoJSON structure for a single datapoint is configured as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "type": "Feature",
  "geometry": {
    "type": "LineString",
    "coordinates": [
      [-73.7781, 40.6413],
      [-85.5627, 40.6407],
      [-97.1415, 39.4569],
      [-108.1612, 37.1753],
      [-118.4085, 33.9416]
    ]
  },
  "properties": { "route_id": "JFK-LAX" }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The corresponding row in the Quick Sight dataset contains the following information:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-1.png" alt="Quick Sight dataset row for the JFK-LAX route with its route_id join key" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Dataset row for the JFK-LAX route, keyed by route_id&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;route_id&lt;/strong&gt; field is used to join the Quick Sight dataset with the GeoJSON file. The GeoJSON holds one such feature per route, and the dataset one row per route. The Geometry type to visualize is set to Lines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-2.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-2.jpg" alt="Layer map rendering the JFK to Los Angeles flight path as a line" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The JFK-LAX route drawn as a LineString layer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="example-point-layer"&gt;Example: Point layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider example data showing a retail store location like Long Island City. The GeoJSON structure for a single datapoint is configured as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "type": "Feature",
  "geometry": {
    "type": "Point",
    "coordinates": [-73.9490, 40.7447]
  },
  "properties": { "store_id": "S-109" }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The corresponding row in the Quick Sight dataset contains the following information:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-3.png" alt="Quick Sight dataset row for a store with its store_id join key" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Dataset row for a store, keyed by store_id&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;store_id&lt;/strong&gt; field is used to join the Quick Sight dataset with the GeoJSON file. The GeoJSON holds one such feature per store, and the dataset one row per store. The Geometry type to visualize is set to Points.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-4.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-4.jpg" alt="Layer map rendering a retail store location as a point" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: A store plotted as a Point layer&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lines-on-layer-map-visuals"&gt;Lines on layer map visuals&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Layer map visuals now support &lt;strong&gt;LineString&lt;/strong&gt; geometry, connecting an ordered list of coordinates into a single path. As covered in the data structure section, each route is one feature, and its coordinates are the ordered positions the map connects in sequence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use lines when the relationship between locations is what you want to show, such as flight paths, delivery routes, shipping lanes, or pipeline segments. The visual draws each path from the coordinates in your GeoJSON file rather than generating it from your data at load time. A field from your joined dataset can determine the line color encoding, so you can group routes by whatever that field represents. In addition to color, you can customize lines with width and opacity controls to adjust visual prominence and clarity on the map. The join key in the GeoJSON properties object ties each LineString to the dataset row behind its tooltip and color.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, an airport operations team might map the flights leaving New York (JFK) on a given day. The GeoJSON file holds one LineString per route, keyed by a route ID that joins to the dataset. Each line starts at JFK and ends at its destination, such as Los Angeles, Chicago, or Miami. The dataset supplies the origin, destination, and carrier that show up in the tooltip.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-5.png" alt="Layer map showing multiple flight paths radiating from JFK to destination cities" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Flights leaving JFK drawn as LineString routes to multiple destinations&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="points-on-layer-map-visuals"&gt;Points on layer map visuals&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick Sight offers a native &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/point-maps.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;point map visual type&lt;/a&gt; that geocodes location names automatically and refreshes with your SPICE schedule or Direct Query connection. It supports dynamic point sizing, color encoding, and point styles such as cluster points and heatmap density gradients. It works best with data that changes frequently and where you want the map to respond to those changes visually.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the added support for GeoJSON point layers in layer map visuals, Quick Sight now offers a second point mapping capability with a few key differences. Layer map visual points use a uniform marker size and don’t support geocoding, so you provide explicit coordinates in the GeoJSON file. In return, you can control the viewport precisely, locking the map to an exact geographic region, and disabling panning and zooming.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the key differences:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Feature&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Point map&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layer map visual points&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data source&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Latitude/longitude values or geolocation names in your dataset&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GeoJSON Point features joined to your dataset by a shared key&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Geocoding&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Recognizes city, county, state, country, and postal code automatically&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Not available. Must provide explicit coordinates in the GeoJSON file&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Location updates&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mapping updates automatically with SPICE refreshes or Direct Query as location values change&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Visual uses a static GeoJSON file for point locations. Requires manual re-upload or API call to update geometry&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Viewport&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Auto-fits to your data, panning and zooming always enabled&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Auto-fits by default, can configure if panning and zooming is allowed&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Appearance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Size and color can be encoded from the dataset. Point style configuration available (basic, cluster, heatmap)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Customizable uniform marker size, fill opacity, border color and thickness. Color can be encoded from the dataset&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose layer map visual points when:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You require a locked, non-scrollable viewport.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Your locations are fixed and curated (such as regional offices, distribution centers, retail stores) rather than changing with each data refresh.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You plan to combine points with other geometry types (covered in the advanced use cases section).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a retail brand whose regional managers use a Quick Sight dashboard to track store performance across a metro area. The GeoJSON file contains a Point feature for each store with a store ID property that joins to the dataset. The dataset holds the metrics (store type, monthly revenue, daily foot traffic) that drive the tooltip and color encoding. You position the map at the zoom level covering the metro area, then disable map navigation so the map renders at a consistent view on every load. Managers can’t accidentally zoom out to a national or global view.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-6.png" alt="Layer map of retail store points across a metro area with a locked viewport" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Store locations as points with map navigation disabled to lock the metro-area view&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="advanced-use-cases"&gt;Advanced use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The previous sections cover a standard layer map setup. Use the following techniques when you need more control over what readers see, want to combine geometry types in a single view, or need geometry that changes over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="controlling-the-viewport"&gt;Controlling the viewport&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you need to control the viewport in layer map visuals, you can pan and zoom to the view you want manually. Then disable map navigation to lock it in place. For use cases where you need more precise boundaries or programmatic control, you can set the exact viewport coordinates through the visual definition using the Quick Sight APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The layer map visual stores its viewport configuration in the &lt;code&gt;MapState&lt;/code&gt; property within &lt;code&gt;ChartConfiguration&lt;/code&gt;. This property accepts two fields:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bounds&lt;/strong&gt;: The geographic coordinates defining the minimum visible area (North, South, East, West).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MapNavigation&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;ENABLED&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;DISABLED&lt;/code&gt; to control whether readers can pan and zoom.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Although the bounds property is not exposed on the console, you can set these bounds when working with the dashboard definition JSON for programmatic asset management. For example, to define the bounds, modify the &lt;code&gt;MapState&lt;/code&gt; block in the visual definition, then call the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_CreateDashboard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CreateDashboard&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_UpdateDashboard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateDashboard&lt;/a&gt; APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bounds define the minimum area that is guaranteed to be visible. The map zooms to make sure this entire area fits within the visual container. For the tightest fit with minimal extra space added to the map, match the aspect ratio of your bounds to the shape of your visual container.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="layering-geometries"&gt;Layering geometries&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For use cases that require combining multiple geometry types into a single view, you can layer visuals on top of each other. For example, the following screenshot demonstrates plotting customer locations as points within US regional boundaries defined by polygons. This composition shows which territories have the highest customer density and can be used to correlate with other metrics present in the dataset like performance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-7.png" alt="Layer map combining customer location points with US regional polygon boundaries" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Customer points layered within polygon regional boundaries&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This second example demonstrates combining points as stops along a delivery route path. The LineString draws the planned route, and Point features mark each scheduled stop along the way.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-8.png" alt="Layer map combining a delivery route line with point markers for each stop" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: A delivery route drawn as a LineString with Point markers for each stop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To layer geometries, you stack multiple layer map visuals on top of each other, following these steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use free-form layout:&lt;/strong&gt; Change the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/types-of-layout.html#free-form-layout.title" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sheet layout setting to free-form&lt;/a&gt; so that visuals can overlap and stack.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create two layer map visuals:&lt;/strong&gt; Create the two layer map visuals that you combine into a single view, such as one using a Point GeoJSON file and one using a LineString GeoJSON file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use matching bounds:&lt;/strong&gt; Using the approach previously described for controlling the viewport, verify all layer map visuals use identical bounds to have the same underlying map view.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Disable panning and zooming:&lt;/strong&gt; For each layer map visual, disable the panning and zooming under Map options so that alignment stays the same.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hide the base map and background on upper layer:&lt;/strong&gt; Set the upper visual’s background to hidden under Display settings and disable the base map under Map options.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stack the visuals:&lt;/strong&gt; Move the top visual on top of the base layer using drag-and-drop or by matching precise size and position coordinates under Display settings.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Layer ordering follows the visual stacking order in the free-form layout. To change a visual’s position in the stack, choose the menu icon (three dots) on the visual, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Send backward&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Bring forward&lt;/strong&gt;. Readers can access the tooltip of the topmost visual, so place the layer that readers need details from on top. Each sheet supports up to five layer map visuals.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="dynamically-updating-geojson"&gt;Dynamically updating GeoJSON&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Unlike other Quick Sight visuals where data refreshes through SPICE or Direct Query, layer map geometry lives in the GeoJSON file itself. When your dataset refreshes on schedule, the metrics update (for example, colors change and tooltip values refresh), but the geometry remains at its original position.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To update what appears on the map, like a new route, new point locations, or updated boundaries, you need to update the GeoJSON file and republish the dashboard. You can replace a GeoJSON file manually through the Quick Sight console by editing the layer map visual within the Analysis and uploading a new file. This works for infrequent changes such as updating territory boundaries quarterly or adding a new store location.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For automated, on-demand, or dynamic updates, the process involves Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and the Quick Sight APIs outlined in the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Upload the new GeoJSON to Amazon S3:&lt;/strong&gt; Generate your updated geometry and save it to an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt; bucket. Custom solutions can use other AWS services such as &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/location/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Location Service&lt;/a&gt; to create the GeoJSON files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reference the file on the dashboard definition:&lt;/strong&gt; The dashboard definition contains a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_StaticFile.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;StaticFiles&lt;/a&gt; array with a &lt;code&gt;SpatialStaticFile&lt;/code&gt; entry for each GeoJSON file. Modify the file reference to the S3 location of your updated file entry for each GeoJSON file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Call the UpdateDashboard API:&lt;/strong&gt; Submit the updated definition with the new file reference using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_UpdateDashboard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateDashboard&lt;/a&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Call the UpdateDashboardPublishedVersion API:&lt;/strong&gt; Publish the change so it is visible to dashboard readers with the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quicksight/latest/APIReference/API_UpdateDashboardPublishedVersion.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UpdateDashboardPublishedVersion&lt;/a&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example uses this pattern to support on-demand map updates in an embedded dashboard to generate optimal routes between locations and waypoints. Although outside the scope of this post, this represents an advanced use case that combines each of the preceding approaches.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-9.gif" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ML-21612-9.gif" alt="Animated demo of an embedded dashboard generating optimal routes between waypoints on demand" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: On-demand route generation in an embedded dashboard&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to use Point and LineString geometry in Amazon Quick Sight layer maps to plot specific locations and the routes between them. We covered the GeoJSON structure and dataset join that layer maps require, and compared layer map points with the native point map. We also walked through advanced techniques for controlling the viewport, layering separate visuals into one view, and refreshing GeoJSON files with Amazon S3 and the Quick Sight APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about layered maps, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/layered-maps.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the Amazon Quick Sight layered maps documentation&lt;/a&gt;. Visit the &lt;a href="https://community.amazonquicksight.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Community&lt;/a&gt; to connect with peers and stay updated on the newest features and resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Bayer Crop Science built an agentic decision engine on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with AWS Unified Operations</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-bayer-crop-science-built-an-agentic-decision-engine-on-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-with-aws-unified-operations/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Subbu Dandamudi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 23 Oct 2026 16:21:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">b1f9be62942b6b77e09ee44eda01d25e5a58cfc4</guid>

					<description>Bayer Crop Science built the Agentic Decision Engine (ADE), a multi-agent platform on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore that lets breeders, analysts, and researchers ask natural language questions about their analytics platform. This post covers the ADE architecture, five design decisions, and how the team prepared it for production with AWS Unified Operations.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Plant breeding decisions depend on data that spans genomics, field trials, imagery, environmental conditions, and model outputs. Bayer Crop Science runs hundreds of machine learning (ML) model configurations against this data across crops, regions, and teams, and each run needs to be repeatable and governed. To meet that requirement, Bayer built the Connected Decision Engine (CDE), a system for deploying, orchestrating, monitoring, and reusing ML workflows. CDE gives breeders, analysts, researchers, data scientists, and data engineers a consistent way to run analytics while preserving execution history and operational controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As adoption grew, the bottleneck moved from running workflows to understanding them. Explaining a result meant piecing together context from APIs, documentation, metadata stores, dashboards, and logs. A user who wanted to know why a workflow failed, what a model did, or where a result came from still had to know which system to inspect. To close that gap, Bayer built the Agentic Decision Engine (ADE), a multi-agent, natural language interface over CDE. A supervisor agent routes each question to a specialist agent, the specialist retrieves context from the relevant system, and the user receives an answer without navigating the operational stack. In its first phase, ADE resolves about 80 percent of user questions without an engineer in the loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ADE runs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, an AWS service to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model. AgentCore let the team focus on agent behavior instead of building and operating its own agent hosting, session isolation, and tool access layer. Three AgentCore capabilities drove that choice: a managed runtime that isolates each user session, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)-scoped tool access through AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, that keeps authentication and authorization outside agent code, and a framework-agnostic design that let the team bring its own LangGraph orchestration and choice of models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To prepare for launch and to operate ADE afterward, Bayer engaged &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/premiumsupport/plans/unified-operations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Unified Operations&lt;/a&gt;. The engagement provided a designated Technical Account Manager (TAM) and a generative AI Domain Specialist Engineer (DSE), who worked as an extension of the ADE team from design through production. The TAM, as Bayer’s strategic advisor, brought an established understanding of Bayer’s operating model and business priorities. The DSE brought generative AI domain expertise on AWS. In three technical standups each week, the team reviewed and made design decisions. The DSE built working knowledge of the ADE workload and how the Bayer team operates, so technical guidance reflected both AWS service behavior and Bayer’s context. This post follows that journey: the ADE architecture, five design decisions made during the build, the work to prepare the platform for production, and how it is operated after launch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ADE is organized into four layers: the user experience, agent orchestration, specialist agents, and the tools and data sources those agents call (Figure 1). Users reach ADE through a React web front end, and requests pass through an identity proxy for Microsoft Entra ID before they reach the agents. GitHub Actions builds the agent container images, pushes them to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/a&gt;, and deploys them to AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime hosts the agents, and because the runtime is framework-agnostic, the team runs its own LangGraph-based orchestration on it directly. The team chose LangGraph for its explicit graph-based control flow and checkpointing for multi-step workflows. LangGraph orchestrates the workflow, with a Supervisor Agent as the entry point that classifies each request, selects a specialist, and combines results when a question spans domains. The data plane is separated by responsibility. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; stores LangGraph checkpoint state. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/aurora/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Aurora&lt;/a&gt; PostgreSQL stores session records, user feedback, and analytics. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticache/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon ElastiCache&lt;/a&gt; caches agent responses with a time to live (TTL). Domain content in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; is embedded with Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 and indexed in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon OpenSearch&lt;/a&gt; Serverless for Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG). &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; captures runtime and application telemetry. AgentCore Gateway exposes backend APIs to agents as Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools and keeps authentication and authorization outside agent code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21834-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21834-1.png" alt="Agentic Decision Engine architecture: user experience, orchestration, specialist agents, and tools and data source layers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Agentic Decision Engine architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ADE runs 22 specialist agents in production, each scoped to one domain with its own tools and data sources. For example, the Domain Expert Agent answers conceptual questions through RAG. The Operational Agent retrieves live workflow status and results from CDE. The Troubleshooting Agent analyzes operational telemetry. The Visualization Agent generates charts or returns existing dashboards. A customer-specific &lt;em&gt;Models as Skill&lt;/em&gt; pattern converts model code and documentation into skill definitions stored in a central registry, so agents answer model-specific questions from the model’s implementation instead of generic model knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a breeder asks, “Why did my phenotyping workflow fail yesterday?”, the supervisor classifies the intent and routes the request to the Troubleshooting Agent. That agent reads the workflow’s telemetry in CloudWatch and its status from the CDE API through AgentCore Gateway, identifies the failure point, and returns a diagnosis through the supervisor (Figure 2). The breeder doesn’t search logs directly. With the Troubleshooting Agent, a diagnosis that once took over an hour of manual log-hunting and waiting for engineer support now takes under 10 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21834-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img title="Figure 2. ADE request flow: intent classification, cache lookup, specialist routing, and response" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/22/ML-21834-2.png" alt="Request flow diagram for the Agentic Decision Engine. A user question enters the Supervisor Agent, which classifies the intent, checks the Amazon ElastiCache response cache, and routes the request to the appropriate specialist agent. The specialist retrieves grounded context from the relevant system, such as the CDE API through AgentCore Gateway or telemetry in Amazon CloudWatch, and returns a composed answer to the user." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: ADE request flow: intent classification, cache lookup, specialist routing, and response&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="five-design-decisions-on-the-path-to-production"&gt;Five design decisions on the path to production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Unified Operations TAM and DSE took part in the following decisions as members of the working team, not as an escalation point after the fact. That let Bayer test each design against AWS service behavior before committing to it, while keeping ownership of the architecture and the final call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="externalize-orchestration-state-without-treating-the-runtime-as-stateless"&gt;1. Externalize orchestration state without treating the runtime as stateless&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime provides isolated sessions that preserve execution context across invocations within a session’s lifecycle. That context isn’t a durable store for a multi-step workflow. ADE writes LangGraph checkpoints to DynamoDB so the graph can resume after scale-out events, restarts, or session boundaries without depending on one execution environment remaining available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; Use the runtime session for active execution, and persist the state required to resume or reconstruct the workflow in a durable service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="separate-enterprise-identity-gateway-access-and-downstream-authorization"&gt;2. Separate enterprise identity, gateway access, and downstream authorization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bayer standardizes on Microsoft Entra ID for user identity, while ADE’s services authorize each other with AWS-native mechanisms. Rather than force one token across every trust boundary, the team gave each boundary its own mechanism.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Entra ID remains the source of user identity. AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) authorizes service-to-service calls. AgentCore Gateway outbound authorization handles access to downstream CDE services. A request interceptor AWS Lambda function performs custom validation and passes the authorization attributes that downstream tools require. The team worked through the token contract at each hop with the DSE.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; Identity failures in agent systems cluster at the transitions between user identity, agent identity, and tool identity. Define the issuer, audience, claims, scopes, and credential lifetime at every hop instead of reusing a token that is valid for one boundary at the next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="treat-model-selection-as-an-interface-contract"&gt;3. Treat model selection as an interface contract&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;During the build, the team evaluated a newer model version with reasoning mode enabled. For that version, the combination of tool use and structured output that ADE depends on was not compatible with how its agents were implemented. The DSE confirmed the constraint, and the team retained the current model for the release.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; For tool-using agents, model choice is constrained by the full interface: tool calling, structured output, streaming behavior, reasoning features, latency, quota availability, and model-specific request semantics. A newer model is not automatically a drop-in replacement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="control-the-tool-surface-before-it-controls-the-prompt"&gt;4. Control the tool surface before it controls the prompt&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CDE manages more than 500 model configurations. A dedicated MCP server per API would have added deployment, authentication, and version management overhead. ADE instead registers OpenAPI specifications with AgentCore Gateway, which exposes the REST operations as MCP tools. Adding or changing an API becomes a schema and gateway configuration change instead of a new server deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tool schemas and descriptions also consume input tokens and compete with the user’s request and retrieved context. ADE scopes tools by specialist agent instead of giving every agent the full catalog. For larger catalogs, AgentCore Gateway supports semantic tool search, so an agent can find relevant tools without loading the entire catalog on every turn.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; Scope the tool catalog per agent. Every tool definition adds tokens and widens the model’s decision space.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="promote-complex-capabilities-from-tools-to-agents"&gt;5. Promote complex capabilities from tools to agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Visualization started as a tool invoked by other agents. As it grew, it needed its own data retrieval, chart selection logic, retries, error handling, and a decision about whether to generate a chart or return an existing dashboard. The team moved it into a specialist Visualization Agent. The agent generates on-demand charts with matplotlib and Plotly in AgentCore Code Interpreter, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore that provides a sandboxed code execution environment, and exchanges data with it through Amazon S3. When a prebuilt dashboard is the better answer, the agent returns the existing Amazon Quick or Power BI asset.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; A tool performs a bounded operation. When a capability starts to own state, retries, planning, policy, or presentation decisions, model it as an agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="preparing-ade-for-production"&gt;Preparing ADE for production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As the build moved toward launch, the focus shifted from design to operability: detecting agent failures, attributing inference cost, and hardening the deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="observability-measure-task-failures-not-only-exceptions"&gt;Observability: Measure task failures, not only exceptions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A supervisor architecture turns one user request into a chain of model calls, tool calls, and agent handoffs. The final answer can be wrong even when every HTTP request succeeded. ADE encountered this directly: application exception handlers caught agent errors and returned responses that monitoring counted as successful. After reproducing the issue with the DSE, the team added custom CloudWatch metrics inside failure paths and alarms on agent-specific conditions: tool call errors, throttling, model timeouts, and structured output validation failures. Traces and invocation logs follow each request across the supervisor and specialists.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle:&lt;/strong&gt; Distinguish transport success from task success. Agent observability should show whether the task completed, which agent or tool changed the outcome, and whether the response used the expected source.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cost-attribution-combine-billing-attribution-with-request-level-telemetry"&gt;Cost attribution: Combine billing attribution with request-level telemetry&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One ADE request can invoke several models and tools, so account-level Amazon Bedrock spend doesn’t explain unit economics. The team designed the attribution approach with the DSE using three mechanisms that answer different questions. Amazon Bedrock application inference profiles attribute billed model usage to an application or workload. Request metadata and model invocation logging associate token usage with a specific agent, feature, or request. IAM principal attribution adds an identity dimension. Together they let the team estimate cost per interaction, identify expensive agents, and determine whether prompts, retrieved context, or tool definitions drive token use. In practice, the Visualization and Operational agents, which rely on MCP tools and multiple tool calls, consumed the most tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same telemetry surfaced a workload during launch preparation with a large repeated input prefix and no prompt cache activity. For supported models, placing stable instructions and tool definitions in a cacheable prompt prefix reduces repeated input processing, which lowers latency and token cost. For ADE, caching the stable prompt prefix cut input-token cost by 20–30 percent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-launch-readiness"&gt;Security and launch readiness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;IAM roles are scoped to each agent’s responsibility. The Troubleshooting Agent can read the operational data it needs for diagnosis but can’t modify workflows. The Domain Expert Agent can query its retrieval index but cannot call unrelated CDE APIs. In production today, ADE’s agent responses are governed by least privilege, read-only tool access scoped to each agent, structured output validation on agent responses, and answers grounded in retrieved CDE data and telemetry rather than open-ended generation. Bayer also plans to evaluate Amazon Bedrock Guardrails as an additional layer on these controls, complementing the custom safety checks already used in CDE multi-turn workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before launch, Unified Operations ran a Critical Workload Review and a Well-Architected review with the Generative AI Lens alongside the ADE team. Because the DSE already knew the workload, both reviews targeted ADE’s specific failure modes, capacity risks, and deployment gaps rather than generic best practices. Findings included gaps in failure visibility across the supervisor and specialist agents, mutable container image tags, limited image traceability, and missing vulnerability scanning. The team responded with stronger alarms, immutable versioned images, scan on push in Amazon ECR, and lifecycle policies for unused images.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The DSE also reviewed scaling and quota requirements with the team. Load testing validated both the application and its service quotas and exposed a networking failure mode that returned empty responses, which the team fixed before the production event. By launch, the team had tracked 35 blockers to resolution with Unified Operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="launch-operations-and-next-steps"&gt;Launch, operations, and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bayer launched ADE to a gated group rather than opening it to the whole organization at once. Since launch, more than 100 users across Bayer Crop Science have used ADE, including breeders, analysts, researchers, data scientists, and engineers. Alongside its 22 specialist agents, ADE exposes about 20 API tools and can host third-party agents as it expands. Exposing those APIs through AgentCore Gateway rather than a dedicated server per API cut per-API integration overhead by more than 99 percent. In its first phase, ADE has resolved about 80 percent of user questions without an engineer in the loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CDE remains the governed execution layer for analytics. ADE adds a natural language layer over it, so users can ask about workflow status, results, model behavior, and failures without learning the location or interface of each underlying system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engagement then shifted from launch readiness to ongoing operations. The DSE continues to meet with the team, so issue triage and operational reviews start from existing knowledge of ADE instead of a new ramp-up. ADE’s critical CloudWatch alarms, including the agent-specific alarms described earlier, are onboarded to AWS Incident Detection and Response. When one fires, AWS Incident Management Engineers engage within 5 minutes and start from the workload’s context rather than from scratch. On the security side, AWS Security Incident Response engineers support the team during potential or active security events, so ADE has AWS monitoring and response coverage for both operational and security issues.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With agent-to-agent communication already in place, Bayer is focusing next on hybrid cloud agent interoperability. The team is exploring how agents running in different environments can discover and communicate with each other without manual configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bayer didn’t replace its analytics platform with ADE. It made CDE easier to query and operate. Most of the production work sat outside the prompts: durable state, identity boundaries, tool scoping, task-level observability, cost attribution, and launch readiness. Bayer owned the architecture and each of those decisions. Working with a designated DSE and TAM through AWS Unified Operations, the team tested those decisions against AWS service behavior during design and carried the same context into production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, explore &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-management.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock cost attribution&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/generative-ai-lens/generative-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Generative AI guidance&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awssupport/latest/user/unified-operations-intro.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Unified Operations&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The authors would like to thank Sijie Shi, Jorge Gomes, Naveen Reddy, Iago Fonseca, Mateus Silva, and Sushimitha Jarugula for their contributions to the design, development, and launch of ADE.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Amazon Polly delivers a major upgrade to its Generative engine</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-polly-delivers-a-major-upgrade-to-its-generative-engine/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Suyin Wang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 22 Oct 2026 18:06:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Polly]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8dd8e6bc35ad32eff8fd57bbe04f9c56274f46ed</guid>

					<description>The Amazon Polly Generative engine gets a major upgrade: more expressive, more accurate speech with low latency, plus 14 new voices across 9 locales and general availability of Hindi and Finnish. This drop-in upgrade requires no code changes and is now generally available in production.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Amazon Polly Generative engine now offers more natural and accurate speech output. We’re introducing an upgrade to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/polly/latest/dg/generative-voices.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly Generative engine&lt;/a&gt; that delivers more expressive, more accurate speech output with low latency. The upgrade adds 14 new voices across 9 locales, including general availability of Generative voices in Hindi and Finnish for the first time. The upgrade is now generally available in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Importantly, this is a drop-in upgrade for existing generative voices. Voice names and API calls are unchanged, so your existing applications continue to work as they do today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/polly/?trk=b671ff34-bbc7-4bfb-810d-1d5dd86754c8&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=CjwKCAjwxeHWBhBPEiwAiqtQ-T9vdBDVcvj307u_rytRQbtnrsBUgG615Vad1q2eqCjNWSGgbYPK2hoCVQ4QAvD_BwE:G:s&amp;amp;gads_camp=24332379316&amp;amp;gads_ag=206560268051&amp;amp;gads_ad=795877020857&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20polly&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9033313&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=24332379316&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp8_G1AzTEjHOKxoh8gOtuJJ2&amp;amp;gclid=CjwKCAjwxeHWBhBPEiwAiqtQ-T9vdBDVcvj307u_rytRQbtnrsBUgG615Vad1q2eqCjNWSGgbYPK2hoCVQ4QAvD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly&lt;/a&gt; is a fully managed AWS service that converts text into natural-sounding speech for contact centers, large language model (LLM)-based agentic applications, media and entertainment, e-learning, accessibility, and other applications that require reliable voice output at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="hear-the-difference"&gt;Hear the difference&lt;/h2&gt;

Ja sitten, uskomatonta kyllä, se vilkutti takaisin hänen nimensä. (The lighthouse keeper hadn’t seen a ship in eleven days. Then, just after midnight, a single light blinked on the horizon. Once. &lt;h2 id="new-voices-languages-and-region-availability"&gt;New voices, languages, and Region availability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A larger catalog overall for Amazon Polly Generative engine&lt;/strong&gt; – Totaling 57 generative voices across 11 languages and 25 locales.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;14 new voices&lt;/strong&gt; across 9 locales – US English (Kenneth, Nate), Australian English (Russell, Curtis), Indian English (Kiara, Arjun), German (Tina), US Spanish (Carlos), Brazilian Portuguese (Leo, Carolina), Korean (Hyunduk), Hindi (Kiara, Arjun) and Finnish (Suvi).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Net new languages generally available in Generative engine –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Hindi and Finnish.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Region expansion&lt;/strong&gt; to AWS GovCloud (US-West) – Totaling 11 AWS Regions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-for-production-ready-speech-workflows-for-enterprise"&gt;Built for production-ready speech workflows for enterprise&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The upgraded Amazon Polly Generative engine uses a standard Amazon Polly API call to build your speech applications. With Amazon Polly, you can continue to build with familiar AWS patterns: call the same Amazon Polly API, manage access with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), use the output in your existing applications and contact-center flows, and monitor the workloads that synthesize speech for your customers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This launch is designed to elevate the speech output quality that you can hear with your existing Amazon Polly implementation. No changes to your code are required to get the upgraded voices. For applications that already use Amazon Polly Generative voices, the upgrade happens in place. For new applications, the same &lt;code&gt;SynthesizeSpeech&lt;/code&gt; API gives you access to a larger set of generative voice catalogs. With the Bi-Di Streaming API &lt;code&gt;StartSpeechSynthesisStream&lt;/code&gt;, you can stream text to Amazon Polly and receive synthesized audio back simultaneously. This is well-suited for real-time applications like voice agents and bespoke characters in games.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="competitive-price-performance"&gt;Competitive price-performance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Polly Generative engine offers competitive pricing on a pay-as-you-go basis. With this model upgrade, you get improved voice quality and accuracy at the same price. There’s no cost increase, no new SKU, and no changes to your existing integration. You can cache and replay synthesized static prompts with no additional cost. Refer to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/polly/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly pricing&lt;/a&gt; for complete cost information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Polly Generative engine now delivers more natural, expressive speech alongside low-latency streaming for real-time applications, Speech Synthesis Markup Language (SSML) support for fine-grained control, and the operational reliability of a fully managed AWS service. This combination of quality, performance, and cost efficiency supports a wide range of production use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-you-can-build"&gt;What you can build&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Contact centers.&lt;/strong&gt; Accuracy matters most in customer service conversations. When a caller checks an order, a voice that reads back the tracking number, address, and confirmation code correctly helps resolve the call in self-service rather than escalating to a human agent. Amazon Polly Generative voice’s accurate entity pronunciation and low-latency generation make automated interactive voice response (IVR) interactions sound more conversational.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cascaded voice agents.&lt;/strong&gt; Using Amazon Polly in a voice agent framework, you can convert LLM response text into clear, natural-sounding speech and stream it back to users in near real time over WebSockets or telephony channels. This makes live conversational voice possible. The primary benefits are low-latency playback, high scalability, and cost efficiency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Media, localization, and dubbing.&lt;/strong&gt; Convert scripts into finished audio for video, advertising, podcasts, product overviews, and audiobooks. You can use one voice or many, without a studio session. More natural intonation reduces post-production. The expanded locale coverage, including first-time generative Hindi and Finnish, means you can localize a single piece of content into more markets from the same pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;E-learning and training.&lt;/strong&gt; Narrate courseware, onboarding, and knowledge-based articles at scale, and update them as soon as the source text changes. No re-recording is required. More expressive delivery supports engagement through long-form material.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accessibility.&lt;/strong&gt; Provide a clear, natural spoken version of articles, documents, and applications so more people can consume your content by listening: read aloud, on the go, or with a screen reader.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Interactive and transactional experiences.&lt;/strong&gt; Voice-enable games, kiosks, IVR menus, and IoT devices with responses generated on demand. Use Amazon Polly when the voice needs to sound natural and read names, numbers, product details, addresses, and confirmations reliably.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“At Sanoma Media Finland, we use Amazon Polly to turn our real-time news articles into audio, giving the listener the choice to listen whenever it suits them better. Today we publish over 10,000 articles per month using Polly’s Finnish neural voice. Based on our testing, Suvi’s new generative voice is a clear improvement over the previous neural voice – it sounds noticeably more natural and makes for a more enjoyable experience for the listener.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Justus Reinikainen, Product Manager, Sanoma Media Finland&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can access the new voices using the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Management Console&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt;, or the AWS SDKs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Active AWS account with Amazon Polly access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) installed and configured.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10+.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Boto3 installed: &lt;code&gt;pip install boto3&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions: &lt;code&gt;polly:SynthesizeSpeech&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here’s a quick example using the AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) with the &lt;code&gt;SynthesizeSpeech&lt;/code&gt; API and a generative voice:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

polly = boto3.client("polly", region_name="us-east-1")

response = polly.synthesize_speech(
    Text="Hello! This is Amazon Polly's next-generation generative voice.",
    VoiceId="Kenneth",  # any generative voice in the catalogue
    Engine="generative",
    OutputFormat="mp3",
)

with open("speech.mp3", "wb") as f:
    f.write(response["AudioStream"].read())&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also make the same request with the AWS CLI:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws polly synthesize-speech \
  --region us-east-1 \
  --voice-id Kenneth \
  --engine generative \
  --output-format mp3 \
  --text "Hello! This is Amazon Polly's next-generation generative voice." \
  speech.mp3&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To retrieve the full, current voice list programmatically, call &lt;code&gt;DescribeVoices&lt;/code&gt;. You can use this in deployment checks to confirm that a selected generative voice is available in the Region where your application runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="availability"&gt;Availability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next-generation Generative engine and the new voices are now generally available. The 14 new Generative voices are available in the following AWS Regions: US East (N. Virginia), US West (Oregon), Europe (Frankfurt), Europe (Zurich), Europe (London), Asia Pacific (Sydney), Asia Pacific (Seoul), Asia Pacific (Singapore), Asia Pacific (Tokyo), Canada (Central), and AWS GovCloud (US-West). Getting started, by audience:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Developers&lt;/strong&gt; – Call &lt;code&gt;synthesize_speech&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;Engine="generative"&lt;/code&gt; in your existing application. No other changes are required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Contact-center teams&lt;/strong&gt; – The improvements reach &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Connect&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;Genesys&lt;/strong&gt; flows automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Everyone&lt;/strong&gt; – Access any voice in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/polly/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few things to know:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Standard and neural voices are unaffected.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Speech marks are not available on generative voices&lt;/strong&gt; (unchanged from today). If your application requires them, standard and neural voices continue to support them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generative engine continues to support SSML tags.&lt;/strong&gt; See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/polly/latest/dg/supportedtags.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported SSML tags&lt;/a&gt; for more details.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use current voice availability at runtime.&lt;/strong&gt; Call &lt;code&gt;DescribeVoices&lt;/code&gt; or check the console when deploying across Regions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next-generation Generative engine of Amazon Polly is available today, delivering more natural and more accurate voices, adding 14 new voices, including generative voices generally available in Hindi and Finnish for the first time, and requires no change to your code. See &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/polly/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly pricing page&lt;/a&gt; for pricing information. To get started, open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/polly/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly console&lt;/a&gt; to try the voices, and see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/polly/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Polly documentation&lt;/a&gt; to build with them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
