  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Log retention + masking&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Retention policy, S3 Object Lock, PII masking&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;164.316(b) documentation retention&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AI guardrails + built-in screening&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Blocked words/phrases, content safety checks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supports minimum-necessary and integrity goals&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-amazon-quick-does-not-do"&gt;What Amazon Quick doesn’t do&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While Amazon Quick offers the preceding controls, this section addresses what Amazon Quick doesn’t offer and how to bridge this gap. Amazon Quick governs access, retrieval, and interaction. It isn’t a data-engineering or data-lake governance platform. In a HIPAA context, several needs fall outside Amazon Quick and are met by other AWS services upstream of it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;De-identification and transformation.&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Quick doesn’t de-identify PHI or reshape source data. When your use case calls for a limited data set or a de-identified extract, prepare it before it reaches Amazon Quick using appropriate AWS services, for example, with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/features/databrew/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue DataBrew&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of AWS Glue, for visual data preparation and redaction, or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt; for extract, transform, and load (ETL) pipelines.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data lake governance and fine-grained table permissions.&lt;/strong&gt; For structured data that Amazon Quick Sight queries through a data lake, column- and row-level governance on the lake itself is the role of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lake-formation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lake Formation&lt;/a&gt;, not Amazon Quick. Amazon Quick Sight enforces its own row-level and column-level security at the analytics layer, but the authoritative lake-level grants live in Lake Formation and the AWS Glue Data Catalog.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The administrative and physical safeguards.&lt;/strong&gt; Workforce training, risk analysis, and breach procedures remain yours regardless of tooling.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A general best practice is to delineate responsibilities and allow the upstream services to decide what data exists and who can query it at the source. Let Amazon Quick decide who can ask questions of it and what the agent may do with the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="anycompany-health-plan-a-sample-use-case"&gt;AnyCompany Health Plan: A sample use case&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AnyCompany Health Plan, a fictional health plan, illustrates a use case and pattern. AnyCompany Health Plan is a covered entity that wants two things: let its care-management team ask questions of policy and member-appeals documents in natural language, and give operations leaders self-service dashboards over claims data. Both touch PHI, so AnyCompany needs to confirm the solution is HIPAA compliant. AnyCompany’s environment has two data paths, and each is architected and governed by distinct solutions before Amazon Quick can access the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unstructured path (policy and medical records documents)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;→ Amazon S3 bucket (ACL-aware KB source)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;→ Amazon Quick Knowledge Base (document-level ACLs, fail-closed at query time)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Structured path (claims)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All claims data currently resides in a lake (Amazon S3).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;→ Glue ETL (curate) → Glue DataBrew (de-identify)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;→ Lake Formation (row/column grants and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/glue/latest/dg/catalog-and-crawler.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue Data Catalog&lt;/a&gt;)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;→ Amazon Quick Sight dataset (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;) (row-level and column-level security, run-as role)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both of the preceding data flows for structured and unstructured data converge into an Amazon Quick chat agent with federated SSO, least-privilege space, guardrails, and CloudWatch vended logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here is how AnyCompany’s administrator implements the solution:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sign the BAA and provision through the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/signin/latest/userguide/how-to-sign-in.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Management Console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/strong&gt; The admin accepts the AWS BAA in AWS Artifact against the AWS account, then provisions Amazon Quick from the AWS Management Console with IAM Identity Center. AnyCompany records the agreement ID, timestamp, and account in its compliance register. Only the web version of Amazon Quick, provisioned through the AWS Management Console, can be brought under a signed BAA through AWS Artifact, which is a necessary component for customers building HIPAA-compliant workflows that touch PHI, subject to proper configuration and the customer’s own compliance responsibilities.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Set the irreversible controls on day zero.&lt;/strong&gt; Because AnyCompany’s security policy calls for control of its own encryption keys, the admin chooses to register a customer managed key as the account default before any Amazon Quick AI data is created. The data key is set once and can’t be changed later. (Default AWS managed encryption is also HIPAA-eligible. Using customer managed keys is not a prerequisite for the service to be HIPAA eligible.) The admin also settles the identity model on IAM Identity Center so that inactive-user detection applies. The Amazon Quick data key is &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/customer-managed-keys.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;set once&lt;/a&gt; on first data creation and can never be changed. If the key is deleted, the only recovery is to unsubscribe and resubscribe, which permanently removes all Amazon Quick data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prepare the structured path outside Amazon Quick.&lt;/strong&gt; Before Amazon Quick Sight touches claims, AnyCompany’s data team uses AWS Glue to build the curated claims tables and Glue DataBrew to produce a de-identified extract for the operations dashboards. AWS Lake Formation grants row- and column-level access on those tables through the AWS Glue Data Catalog. As a result, Amazon Quick never sees raw PHI that it does not need.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create an ACL-aware knowledge base for the documents.&lt;/strong&gt; The admin connects the policy-and-appeals S3 bucket as a knowledge base with document-level ACLs enabled at creation. Because ACLs can’t be turned on later, this needs to be configured when you create the S3 bucket. Amazon Quick evaluates each user’s identity at query time and returns no documents when it can’t confirm access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build a dedicated space and a custom chat agent.&lt;/strong&gt; AnyCompany creates a PHI space with restricted membership, separate from general-productivity spaces. The care-management chat agent is scoped to only the ACL-aware knowledge base, with open web search disabled and guardrails configured. Read-only connectors are implemented for anything PHI-adjacent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Connect the dashboards with query-time security.&lt;/strong&gt; The operations dashboards read the de-identified Amazon Quick Sight dataset over Athena, with Amazon Quick Sight row-level and column-level security and a run-as role layered on top of the Lake Formation grants.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Turn on the audit signals before onboarding users.&lt;/strong&gt; The admin creates a CloudTrail trail and configures CloudWatch vended log delivery (CHAT_LOGS, FEEDBACK_LOGS, KB_FILE_SYNC_LOGS, and the rest) to a retained, masked destination. Feedback is treated as a PHI surface.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate, then scale from non-PHI to PHI.&lt;/strong&gt; AnyCompany starts with non-PHI workflows, such as policy Q&amp;amp;A and document search, to confirm governance, tests with adversarial prompts to verify fail-closed ACL behavior, reviews the logs to confirm the events are captured, and only then expands the care-management team into PHI-adjacent work. &lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick enforces identity, retrieval, guardrails, and the audit trail. But the de-identification, the ETL, and the lake-level grants happen upstream in Glue, Glue DataBrew, and Lake Formation. This division is the recommended pattern, and is the practical answer to augmenting Amazon Quick in a federated environment.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-make-it-compliant-best-practices"&gt;How to make it compliant: Best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Eligibility gives you a platform that &lt;em&gt;can&lt;/em&gt; handle PHI. You still must configure it so that it &lt;em&gt;does.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section helps you understand the shared-responsibility decisions involved. Because a few of these decisions are irreversible, make them before you create resources or onboard users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="start-with-the-security-model-for-agentic-ai"&gt;Start with the security model for agentic AI&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick agents don’t wait for instructions at every step. They invoke tools, modify data, and interact with external systems on their own. That changes how you approach securing your environment. AWS &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/four-security-principles-for-agentic-ai-systems/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;published&lt;/a&gt; four security principles for agentic AI in April 2026, and they apply directly here.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle 1 —&lt;/strong&gt; Layer agent safeguards onto your existing security framework. Don’t stand up a parallel security posture for Amazon Quick. Add agent-specific measures (guardrails, topic blocking, and PII redaction) on top of the IAM, network, and encryption policies you already enforce.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle 2 — Trust deterministic controls over prompt-based ones.&lt;/strong&gt; A well-crafted adversarial input can circumvent a prompt instruction. AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies, document-level ACLs, network boundaries, and admin-configured guardrails can’t be talked around. Use those as your primary enforcement. Agent instructions are your second layer, not your only one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle 3 — Apply defense in depth across perceive, reason, and act.&lt;/strong&gt; Map every agent capability to one of three layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Perceive:&lt;/strong&gt; knowledge bases, uploaded files, web search, connector inputs, MCP tool outputs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reason:&lt;/strong&gt; the LLM processing context, controlled by guardrails and topic controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Act:&lt;/strong&gt; Connectors, Quick Flows, Quick Automate, scheduled tasks, MCP tool calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply controls at each layer independently. A gap in one shouldn’t cascade into the others.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note that HIPAA BAA obligations extend to supporting services and solutions like MCP tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Principle 4 — Expand autonomy progressively.&lt;/strong&gt; Start agents in read-only mode against non-PHI data. Add capabilities one at a time. Test for data leakage, prompt-injection resilience, and permission-boundary adherence at each step before moving to the next.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decide-before-you-deploy"&gt;Decide before you deploy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because these three choices are permanent, make these decisions before implementation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Register your KMS key first.&lt;/strong&gt; If your organization requires control of its own encryption keys, register a customer managed key as the account default before creating any Amazon Quick AI resources. The data key is set the first time Quick AI data is created in the account. After that, it can’t be changed. Customer managed key support requires an Enterprise subscription. Note that a customer managed key disables chat memory. Accounts configured with a customer managed key &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/customer-managed-keys.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;do not support&lt;/a&gt; this feature.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choose your identity model deliberately.&lt;/strong&gt; You have four options: IAM Identity Center, Active Directory, IAM federation, and Quick local users. They differ in provisioning, offboarding, and governance behavior. Inactive-user detection, for instance, works only with IAM Identity Center or Active Directory. Switching identity providers after deployment means reprovisioning every user’s permissions and space memberships. Pick once.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Make PHI knowledge bases ACL-aware at creation.&lt;/strong&gt; For supported sources (Amazon S3, Google Drive, SharePoint, Confluence Cloud, and OneDrive), you decide at creation whether a knowledge base enforces document-level ACLs. That setting is permanent. You can’t enable ACLs on a knowledge base that was created without them. For anything PHI-adjacent, create it ACL-aware from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Warning:&lt;/strong&gt; Misconfigured user-store mappings can silently expose restricted documents to the wrong users, without errors or alerts. It’s critical to validate your identity-to-ACL mapping before you ingest PHI content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="region-network-and-account-boundaries"&gt;AWS Region, network, and account boundaries&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Plan your deployment Region based on the following factors:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data residency:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Quick processes and stores data in the Region where you provision it, and you need to confirm that Region satisfies your organization’s data-localization requirements. Note that Amazon Quick is a single-Region service: it benefits from multi-AZ resilience &lt;em&gt;within&lt;/em&gt; a Region, but it doesn’t fail over across Regions. If you support business-critical workflows that require cross-Region resilience, provision a separate Amazon Quick deployment in a second Region, along with its supporting data, for example, replicating S3-based knowledge base sources across Regions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service availability.&lt;/strong&gt; Features are at times launched in different Regions at different times. Verify that everything your agents need, such as knowledge bases, connectors, and Amazon Quick Sight capabilities, is available where you plan to deploy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-account strategy.&lt;/strong&gt; Place Amazon Quick in a dedicated workload account in AWS Organizations. Use Service Control Policies (SCPs) to enforce Region restrictions and block accidental provisioning in out-of-scope Regions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For network boundaries: Amazon Quick is a managed service accessed over HTTPS. You don’t deploy it into a virtual private cloud (VPC). But your data sources (S3 buckets, databases, on-premises systems) might sit behind VPC boundaries. Use VPC endpoints and AWS PrivateLink where available. Make sure DNS resolution and firewall rules let Amazon Quick’s connectors reach your authorized data sources without opening the network wider than necessary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="minimize-the-agents-attack-surface"&gt;Minimize the agent’s attack surface&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick’s own documentation frames adversarial risk as a two-condition problem: risk exists when (1) an agent can read content from outside your trust boundary, &lt;em&gt;and&lt;/em&gt; (2) it can take a consequential action. Narrow either condition and you reduce exposure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Constrain what agents can read (Perceive layer).&lt;/strong&gt; Know every source that feeds an agent’s context: knowledge bases, uploaded files, web search, third-party research providers, connector responses, MCP tool outputs, and retained conversation memory. For PHI workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scope agents to trusted, ACL-aware knowledge bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn off open web search where it isn’t needed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn off file upload if the agent doesn’t require it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Limit conversation-history retention to the minimum useful window.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Constrain what agents can do (Act layer).&lt;/strong&gt; Inventory every outbound path: connectors, MCP tools, web queries, Quick Flows, Quick Automate workflows, scheduled agent runs. For PHI-adjacent agents:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prefer read-only connector access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Be cautious with automated and scheduled workflows, because they run without anyone watching.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Block write actions unless explicitly justified and approved by your security team.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use connector-level permission scoping to restrict which operations an agent can invoke.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Know the identity an action runs as.&lt;/strong&gt; On-demand actions authenticate as the invoking user, bounded by that user’s connector permissions. Automated workflows run without an interactive session. Match the autonomy level to the sensitivity of the action, and document which agents operate in which identity mode.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="enforce-least-privilege-and-data-segregation"&gt;Enforce least privilege and data segregation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Separate PHI-containing knowledge bases and spaces from general-productivity ones. Restrict membership.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grant space and agent access on a need-to-know basis. Review both sharing permissions (who can use a knowledge base) and source-document ACLs (which indexed documents an authorized user can actually retrieve).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The default knowledge-base refresh cycle is 24 hours. Shorten it when your access-change requirements demand faster propagation. Refresh ACLs before reassigning or recycling email addresses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A PHI agent shouldn’t share a space with general-purpose productivity tools. This means you need to isolate use cases at the space level.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review connector permissions regularly. Rotate credentials stored in AWS Secrets Manager on a schedule that matches your credential-management policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="practice-data-minimization"&gt;Practice data minimization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS recommends against entering confidential data into resource names or tags, to avoid accidental mention in billing or diagnostic logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prefer de-identified data wherever the use case allows it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When ingesting structured data from databases or APIs, filter columns and rows at the source before indexing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure agent guardrails to redact PII in responses, hiding Social Security numbers (SSNs), medical record numbers (MRNs), and dates of birth, using the built-in PII detection filters.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="configure-audit-logging-and-retention"&gt;Configure audit logging and retention&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick provides several distinct audit signals. None of them flow by default and you might need to turn them on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Signal&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Source&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it captures&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;API operations&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudTrail&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Admin actions, configuration changes&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Chat interactions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CHAT_LOGS — full conversation records&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User feedback&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;FEEDBACK_LOGS — thumbs up/down with context&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KB sync events&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KB_FILE_SYNC_LOGS — ingestion status&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Operational metrics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Latency, error rates, invocation counts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Usage analytics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick console&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Adoption, top queries, user activity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here is how to set them up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a CloudTrail trail for the accounts and Regions in scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure CloudWatch vended log delivery (CHAT_LOGS, FEEDBACK_LOGS, KB_FILE_SYNC_LOGS) shortly after you turn on Amazon Quick’s AI features.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Apply CloudWatch Logs data-protection masking to chat logs so PHI isn’t stored in cleartext.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set a retention period of at least six years. Consider S3 Object Lock on the log archive.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add CloudWatch alarms for anomalous activity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enable AWS Security Hub for continuous compliance checks.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Route critical alerts to your security information and event management (SIEM) or incident-response platform.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="validate-before-you-scale"&gt;Validate before you scale&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before expanding scope, check the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;BAA acceptance is recorded in your compliance register.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Data classification is documented: Which knowledge bases and spaces contain PHI, which agents can reach them.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Adversarial queries and prompt-injection scenarios have been tested.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Logs are being captured and masking works correctly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A tabletop breach exercise has been completed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;InfoSec has signed off.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="known-limitations"&gt;Known limitations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here are some known limitations of Amazon Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No BAA on the Free or Plus path.&lt;/strong&gt; You must use the path provisioned through the AWS Management Console to get BAA coverage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No cross-Region replication.&lt;/strong&gt; Disaster recovery (DR) is constrained to a single Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrails are probabilistic, not deterministic.&lt;/strong&gt; They reduce risk. They don’t eliminate it. Supplement with the infrastructure-level controls described earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Third-party connectors extend your BAA obligations.&lt;/strong&gt; When a connector sends data to an external system, your compliance responsibility follows the data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conversation memory might contain echoed PHI.&lt;/strong&gt; Set retention policies and apply masking.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-guide"&gt;Implementation guide&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AnyCompany Health Plan example showed how to roll out Amazon Quick. This section turns that pattern into a step-by-step project plan you can hand to your platform team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Execute the BAA (Day 1)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sign in with AWS Artifact permissions and accept the BAA.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record the agreement ID, timestamp, principal ARN, and account ID.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Notify your legal and compliance team.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Set foundational, irreversible controls (Days 1–2)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Register a customer managed key as the account default.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify the key policy grants the required permissions: Encrypt, Decrypt, GenerateDataKey, DescribeKey.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure your identity model (IAM Identity Center is recommended).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the account sits in a dedicated workload organizational unit (OU) with Region-restricting SCPs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3: Create ACL-aware knowledge bases (Days 3–7)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Classify your data sources: PHI, PHI-adjacent, and non-PHI.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create knowledge bases with ACL awareness turned on for PHI and PHI-adjacent sources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Map the identity store to source permissions and validate with multiple test users.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Verify sync logs confirm successful ingestion. Keep PHI knowledge bases separated from general content.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 4: Create spaces (Days 5–8)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Separate PHI-adjacent spaces from general-productivity spaces.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Attach knowledge bases, dashboards, and action connectors. Restrict membership.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that unauthorized users are blocked.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 5: Create custom agents with guardrails (Days 7–12)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define each agent’s purpose narrowly. Attach only the knowledge sources it needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure PII redaction, blocked topics, content filters, and grounding controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Test with normal queries, boundary queries, and adversarial prompts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Document results and remediation steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 6: Configure action connectors (Days 10–14)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start with read-only mode for PHI-adjacent data. Scope each connector to specific resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Store credentials in AWS Secrets Manager with rotation turned on.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Document the justification for any write access. Get security sign-off before turning it on.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Test scope limits to confirm connectors can’t reach resources beyond their intended scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 7: Configure audit, retention, and monitoring (Days 12–16)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enable CloudTrail and CloudWatch vended logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Apply data-protection masking. Set retention to at least six years. Enable S3 Object Lock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure alarms, enable Security Hub, and integrate with your SIEM.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 8: Validate with InfoSec (Days 14–21)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Compile the evidence package: BAA confirmation, KMS configuration, identity setup, data classification, test results, and log samples.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a tabletop breach exercise.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Present to your InfoSec team. Remediate findings. Obtain sign-off.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expansion phase (Weeks 4–8)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with non-PHI workflows: policy Q&amp;amp;A, document search, meeting prep, workflow automation. These let your team build confidence with the platform before PHI enters the picture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then expand to PHI-adjacent workflows: clinical documentation assistance, claims processing, prior authorization, regulatory Q&amp;amp;A, patient communication drafting with human review in the loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Monitor closely: review chat logs weekly, track guardrail trigger rates, and watch access patterns for 30 days after each new workflow goes live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick gives regulated healthcare organizations, and the business associates who serve them, a managed AI workspace that is HIPAA-eligible across its full range of capabilities: agentic AI, knowledge retrieval, business intelligence, automation, and research.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The path to a compliant deployment follows a clear sequence:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="A"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Understand the difference between eligible and compliant, and own your side of shared responsibility.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Execute the BAA on an account provisioned through the AWS Management Console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the irreversible controls first: KMS keys, identity model, ACL-aware knowledge bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Minimize the agent’s read and action surfaces, enforce least privilege, and mask PHI in logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Validate with your InfoSec team, then scale from non-PHI to PHI-adjacent workflows.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compliance expectations are moving as fast as the technology. AWS published its &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/four-security-principles-for-agentic-ai-systems/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;four security principles&lt;/a&gt; for agentic AI in April 2026 and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentic-ai-lens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens&lt;/a&gt; in June 2026. Organizations that build on deterministic, infrastructure-level controls today will likely be well positioned as regulatory expectations catch up to what agentic AI can do.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-to-do-next"&gt;What to do next&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;If you’re evaluating Amazon Quick:&lt;/strong&gt; Request a compliance-focused briefing from your AWS account team. Ask about BAA scope, shared responsibility for agentic AI, and the latest HIPAA-eligible service additions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;If you’re ready to deploy:&lt;/strong&gt; Use the implementation guide as your project plan. The irreversible decisions in Step 2 deserve a dedicated architecture review before you start creating resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;If you’re already running Amazon Quick:&lt;/strong&gt; Audit your deployment against the best practices in this post, especially the agentic security principles and the known limitations list.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For architecture guidance, reach out to your AWS Solutions Architect or the AWS Healthcare and Life Sciences team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Stream low-latency speech from AI agents using Kokoro TTS on Amazon SageMaker AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/stream-low-latency-speech-from-ai-agents-using-kokoro-tts-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Joseph Cottingham]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 22 Oct 2026 17:45:58 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">351e07d8236b8a05fa15e510dcb5b937d7a9dec8</guid>

					<description>Voice agents often wait for an agent's full response before starting speech synthesis, adding latency users feel directly. This post shows how to stream each sentence to a Kokoro-82M text-to-speech model on Amazon SageMaker AI as the agent produces it, and how to get that audio back to a browser over Server-Sent Events or a unified binary transport.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;A customer asks an AI voice agent a question and waits. The agent already has an answer, it’s midway through generating a long, multi-paragraph response, but the customer hears nothing for five seconds. By the time an answer arrives, the customer is frustrated, confused, and no longer paying attention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Text-to-speech integrations create exactly that gap. A one-sentence response might generate in 600 milliseconds, but the full response takes five seconds, and the integration waits for all of it before synthesis starts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to close that gap. We stream each sentence to a hosted Kokoro-82M text-to-speech (TTS) model on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; as soon as an agent produces it, so synthesis begins on sentence one instead of the finished response. Getting that audio back to a browser needs its own solution too, so we also cover both transports for doing it and what each costs in practice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution closes the gap by streaming synthesis alongside generation instead of waiting for it to finish. As the agent produces each sentence, that sentence streams immediately to a hosted Kokoro-82M text-to-speech model, so audio playback can start before the agent finishes speaking its full response. Kokoro-82M holds only 82 million parameters, small enough to run cheaply on a single mid-tier GPU instance. That keeps both latency and inference cost low for a voice agent handling real traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kokoro-82M runs in a custom container on a SageMaker AI real-time endpoint, on an &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; GPU instance. The container exposes two routes: &lt;code&gt;POST /invocations&lt;/code&gt;, which streams a response for one complete input, and &lt;code&gt;WS /invocations-bidirectional-stream&lt;/code&gt;, which keeps one connection open across many messages in both directions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the request path from client to model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-1.png" alt="Client connects to an Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoint on ml.g5.xlarge over InvokeEndpointWithBidirectionalStream. A Starlette ASGI app exposing GET /ping, POST /invocations, and WS /invocations-bidirectional-stream routes the WebSocket to a KPipeline running Kokoro-82M, which returns audio and control frames on the same connection." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Request path from the client to the SageMaker AI real-time endpoint over a bidirectional stream&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create SageMaker AI endpoints; AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore; AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles; and an Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repository, including at minimum the &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:CreateEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:CreateEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:CreateModel&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker:InvokeEndpointWithBidirectionalStream&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt; actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The sample container pins an older PyTorch with a known advisory (&lt;code&gt;PYSEC-2026-2286&lt;/code&gt;, fixed in torch 2.10.0). Review and upgrade the pin before any non-proof-of-value use.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cdk/v2/guide/getting_started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) v2&lt;/a&gt;, installed and bootstrapped in your target account and AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A SageMaker AI real-time endpoint deployed from a container that declares the &lt;code&gt;com.amazonaws.sagemaker.capabilities.bidirectional-streaming=true&lt;/code&gt; Docker label.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.12 or later, &lt;a href="https://docs.astral.sh/uv/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;uv&lt;/a&gt;, and Docker to build the container image (the underlying HTTP/2 client only ships pre-built wheels for Python 3.12 and newer).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS SDK for Python (Boto3) with support for &lt;code&gt;invoke_endpoint_with_bidirectional_stream&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Familiarity with WebSockets and Starlette or a comparable ASGI framework.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The deployment code from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-kokoro-tts-for-ai-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; referenced in this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough first deploys the endpoint and agent, then works through the client-side changes that make incremental streaming work end to end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-the-endpoint-and-agent"&gt;Deploy the endpoint and agent&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Kokoro-82M endpoint and the agent runtime deploy through two CLI tools, each wrapping its own AWS CDK stacks:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run speech-infra deploy kokoro-82m
uv run agent-infra deploy --bedrock-model-id &amp;lt;your-bedrock-model-id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;speech-infra deploy&lt;/code&gt; provisions the Kokoro-82M endpoint. This includes the foundation and model-cache stacks that create the underlying IAM roles and sync the model’s weights into Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3). The endpoint stack then builds the container image and creates the SageMaker AI model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint. &lt;code&gt;agent-infra deploy&lt;/code&gt; provisions the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime hosting the Strands agent, which calls the endpoint you just deployed, so deploy the endpoint first.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-handle-the-websocket-frame-type-sagemaker-ai-actually-sends"&gt;Step 1: Handle the WebSocket frame type SageMaker AI actually sends&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bidirectional transport SageMaker AI uses delivers every piece of a client’s message to your container as a binary WebSocket frame. A common way to write a Starlette WebSocket handler is calling &lt;code&gt;receive_text()&lt;/code&gt;, which reads &lt;code&gt;message["text"]&lt;/code&gt; and raises &lt;code&gt;KeyError&lt;/code&gt; if that key isn’t present. Because every message this client sends arrives as binary, that handler fails on every request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The failure is commonly misdiagnosed. A generic exception handler that forwards &lt;code&gt;str(e)&lt;/code&gt; to the client turns &lt;code&gt;KeyError('text')&lt;/code&gt; into an error frame reading &lt;code&gt;'text'&lt;/code&gt;, which looks like a missing field, not a framing issue. The endpoint itself reports &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt; throughout, since it accepts connections and responds to every message, with an error each time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Accept either frame type explicitly instead of assuming one will show up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from starlette.websockets import WebSocket, WebSocketDisconnect

async def _receive_message(websocket: WebSocket) -&amp;gt; str:
    message = await websocket.receive()
    if message["type"] == "websocket.disconnect":
        raise WebSocketDisconnect(message.get("code", 1000))
    payload = message.get("text")
    if payload is None:
        payload = message.get("bytes", b"").decode("utf-8")
    return payload&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This decodes bytes as UTF-8 when no text field is present, rather than assuming one frame type. Test it by sending a binary frame, because this failure mode produces a well-formed error frame and a healthy endpoint status, not an obvious crash.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-keep-the-session-open-between-messages"&gt;Step 2: Keep the session open between messages&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After your container finishes synthesizing one message’s audio, the instinct is to close the session, the way an HTTP cycle ends. That’s wrong here. After sending a message’s audio and a &lt;code&gt;synthesis_complete&lt;/code&gt; control frame, the container loop should go back to waiting on &lt;code&gt;receive()&lt;/code&gt; for the next message on the same, still-open session. Close the connection only when the client sends an explicit &lt;code&gt;{"type": "close"}&lt;/code&gt; message.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows one session carrying two sentences in sequence, closing only at the end:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 610px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ml-21706-edit-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/20/ml-21706-edit-1.png" alt="One bidirectional session sending two sentences, each followed by synthesis_start, PCM audio, and synthesis_complete frames, ending with an explicit close message." width="600"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: One bidirectional session carrying two sentences in sequence, closing only at the end&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This way, you can send several sentences, one after another, on one persistent session, with no reconnection cost between them. Closing eagerly after one response would force a new connection per sentence, undoing most of the latency benefit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-stream-sentences-as-the-agent-produces-them"&gt;Step 3: Stream sentences as the agent produces them&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the session behavior in place, the client-side fix for the latency problem is a class that normalizes text arriving in two shapes: a complete string known up front, or a live token stream. It produces the same output either way: complete sentences, ready to send as soon as each one is complete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import re

_SENTENCE_RE = re.compile(r"(?&amp;lt;=[.!?])\s+")


class IncrementalSentenceChunker:
    def __init__(self) -&amp;gt; None:
        self._buffer = ""

    def feed(self, fragment: str) -&amp;gt; list[str]:
        self._buffer += fragment
        parts = _SENTENCE_RE.split(self._buffer)
        if len(parts) == 1:
            return []
        self._buffer = parts[-1]
        return [s.strip() for s in parts[:-1] if s.strip()]

    def flush(self) -&amp;gt; str | None:
        remainder = self._buffer.strip()
        self._buffer = ""
        return remainder or None&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;feed()&lt;/code&gt; once per fragment of text as it arrives. It returns any newly completed sentences, holding back trailing text. Call &lt;code&gt;flush()&lt;/code&gt; once, at the end of input, to release whatever partial text remains even without trailing punctuation. This keeps a stream that ends before a period arrives from dropping a final sentence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This isn’t concurrent synthesis. Every bidirectional handler is a strictly sequential loop: receive a message, synthesize its audio, send that audio, send &lt;code&gt;synthesis_complete&lt;/code&gt;, then receive the next message. Sentence two’s audio isn’t generated while sentence one’s still transmits. What incremental sending buys is narrower: synthesis on sentence one begins the instant it exists, rather than waiting for the full response. For a multi-sentence response with real generation time between its first and last sentence, that’s the entire latency win.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We verified this against a live endpoint sending a three-sentence input over one session. All three chunks arrived with audio matching the text sent for each:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chunk&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Text&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audio bytes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Duration (s)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“Hello there.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;73,200&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1.525&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“This is a post deployment check.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;108,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.250&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;“It has three sentences.”&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;98,400&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2.050&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-choose-a-transport-for-getting-audio-back-to-the-client"&gt;Step 4: Choose a transport for getting audio back to the client&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Streaming text into the model incrementally solves half the problem. The other half is getting the resulting audio out to whatever renders it, commonly a browser. Most agent and chat frontends already stream text over Server-Sent Events (SSE): one-directional, and supported natively by most browsers. SSE is a text protocol, not built to carry a raw binary payload. A team adding voice to an existing SSE pipeline naturally reaches for the pattern it already has: base64-encode the audio and wrap it in a JSON event.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We measured the cost of that approach across 24 clips, confirmed with Amazon Transcribe that quality didn’t differ across encodings, and compared MP3 against AAC:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Candidate&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Raw bytes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Base64 bytes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bitrate&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Raw PCM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2,396,400&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3,195,200&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;384 kbps&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MP3 48k&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;308,304&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;411,072&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;49.5 kbps&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AAC 32k fMP4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;258,039&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;344,084&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;41.6 kbps&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Base64 inflates every payload by close to the expected 33%. The subtler cost is frame alignment. SSE events are delimited by a blank line, but the audio inside is delimited into MP3 frames. The two schemes don’t necessarily align with how the transport chunks the data. In one measured response, four logical MP3 frames arrived as six delivery chunks, two ending mid-frame. A consumer that treats each delivery chunk as one complete unit fails on ordinary, healthy traffic. The fix buffers incoming bytes and acts only once a delimiter is found:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;buffer = b""
for event in resp["Body"]:
    if "PayloadPart" not in event:
        continue
    buffer += event["PayloadPart"]["Bytes"]
    while b"\n\n" in buffer:
        frame, buffer = buffer.split(b"\n\n", 1)
        handle(frame)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The alternative used today for the model-facing leg skips base64 and SSE framing entirely. It multiplexes raw binary audio and JSON control frames over the same bidirectional session already carrying incremental text input, and tells them apart with one rule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import json


def _decode_control_frame(chunk: bytes) -&amp;gt; dict | None:
    try:
        decoded = json.loads(chunk.decode("utf-8"))
    except (UnicodeDecodeError, json.JSONDecodeError):
        return None
    return decoded if isinstance(decoded, dict) else None&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A chunk that fails to decode as a JSON object is audio, forwarded as received, with no encoding step anywhere in the path. One edge case matters: raw PCM audio can legitimately begin with byte &lt;code&gt;0x7b&lt;/code&gt;, the ASCII character &lt;code&gt;{&lt;/code&gt;, the same character that opens a JSON object. This rule avoids misclassifying that audio because it requires the entire chunk to parse as valid JSON, not its first byte alone, something a PCM frame will essentially never do.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; If your frontend already has a working SSE pipeline for text, base64-encoded audio inside an SSE event is the pragmatic choice. It costs roughly 33 percent extra payload and a buffer-and-reassemble step, but reuses infrastructure you already operate. If you’re building the voice path fresh, or latency is the priority, the unified binary and JSON approach avoids both costs and reuses the connection already carrying text.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-put-it-together-in-a-real-agent"&gt;Step 5: Put it together in a real agent&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The fuller, end-to-end case is a live, generative agent composing its response one token at a time, with no way to know sentence four until sentence one exists. This example runs as a Strands agent, backed by Amazon Bedrock, deployed to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, calling the Kokoro endpoint with the pattern from Step 4. Three communication legs connect in this design: the browser-facing leg carrying Server-Sent Events back to the caller, the agent’s streaming call to Amazon Bedrock for text generation, and the agent’s bidirectional session with the Kokoro endpoint for synthesis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows those three communication legs, and where the overlap between generation and synthesis happens:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-3.png" alt="Caller sends a request to an agent container built with BedrockAgentCoreApp over leg 1, POST /invocations, SSE-shaped with base64 audio. The container calls Amazon Bedrock over leg 2, a streaming converse call for text deltas, and opens a bidirectional session to the Kokoro endpoint over leg 3, raw PCM with no base64, merging both streams back to the caller as Server-Sent Events." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The three communication legs connecting the caller, Amazon Bedrock, and the Kokoro endpoint&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The request payload carries a &lt;code&gt;mode&lt;/code&gt; field, either &lt;code&gt;"bidi"&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;"batch"&lt;/code&gt;, dispatched to two code paths inside the same entrypoint function. A benchmark between them changes exactly one variable: when synthesis starts. In &lt;code&gt;run_bidi&lt;/code&gt;, two tasks run concurrently. One drains the Bedrock text stream and feeds completed sentences into the chunker. The other drains the Kokoro session and emits audio chunks as they arrive. Both push into one shared queue, so a consumer yields events in true arrival order: audio for an early sentence can reach the caller while Bedrock still generates a later one. &lt;code&gt;run_batch&lt;/code&gt; is the deliberate opposite: it waits for the full response before one synthesis call, kept as a fair baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Going the other direction, the leg from the agent container back to the caller does use base64, for a different reason than Step 4 describes. &lt;code&gt;BedrockAgentCoreApp&lt;/code&gt;’s entrypoint must yield plain, JSON-serializable dictionaries, which its SDK turns into &lt;code&gt;data: {json}\n\n&lt;/code&gt; frames automatically. There’s no way to hand it a raw binary frame. That’s a consequence of the JSON-native managed runtime, not the choice Step 4 measured and moved away from. Both &lt;code&gt;run_bidi&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;run_batch&lt;/code&gt; pay that identical cost on this leg, so the measured difference between them is purely timing: when the first audio chunk is emitted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="testing-and-validation"&gt;Testing and validation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We isolated the TTS transport’s own contribution to time to first audio by sending the same 267-character, four-sentence block of text two ways: as one message (batch), and as only its first sentence (incremental). We ran five trials each against the live endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As measured earlier, sending only the first sentence cut mean time to first audio byte from 329.3 ms to 156.7 ms, a 52.4 percent relative improvement, isolated to the TTS transport itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We then measured the fuller, end-to-end case with a real language model producing the text, running the identical prompt through &lt;code&gt;run_bidi&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;run_batch&lt;/code&gt; against the same live Bedrock model and endpoint, so the only variable is when synthesis starts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using that same methodology, bidi mode’s time to first audio measured 2.4 times faster than batch mode against a local container, and 3.5 times faster against the deployed AgentCore runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following chart shows that gap across both environments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/10/19/ML-21706-4.png" alt="Bar chart comparing relative time to first audio for batch and bidi modes, locally and on a deployed Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. Batch is normalized to 1.0x. Local bidi measures 0.42x (2.4 times faster). Deployed bidi measures 0.29x (3.5 times faster)." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Relative time to first audio for batch and bidi modes, locally and on the deployed runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gap widens, rather than narrows, for the deployed case once a real network hop is added. &lt;code&gt;run_bidi&lt;/code&gt; absorbs that hop inside the overlap it’s already exploiting between generation and synthesis. &lt;code&gt;run_batch&lt;/code&gt; has no equivalent overlap to hide that cost in, since it does one thing at a time, so every added millisecond of network overhead lands directly on its serial wait.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To reproduce this comparison against your own deployment, run the same bidi-vs-batch test the agent container exposes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run agent-infra demo --endpoint-url &amp;lt;your-endpoint-url&amp;gt; --prompt "&amp;lt;your prompt&amp;gt;"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This sends the prompt through both modes and reports the timestamp of the first &lt;code&gt;audio_chunk&lt;/code&gt; event against &lt;code&gt;agent_start&lt;/code&gt; for each. If you see errors instead, check for the &lt;code&gt;KeyError: 'text'&lt;/code&gt; symptom from Step 1. An endpoint reporting &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt; while every request fails signals that bug, not a deployment issue.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, tear down the resources this walkthrough creates in reverse deployment order, agent first, then endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;uv run agent-infra destroy --bedrock-model-id &amp;lt;your-bedrock-model-id&amp;gt;
uv run speech-infra destroy kokoro-82m&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;agent-infra destroy&lt;/code&gt; removes the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime resource. &lt;code&gt;speech-infra destroy&lt;/code&gt; removes the SageMaker AI model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint. Neither empties the Amazon S3 bucket that caches model weights or deletes the Amazon ECR repository holding the container image, since both can be shared across models. If you no longer need them, empty and delete the Amazon S3 bucket and remove the Amazon ECR repository manually, since a stored model checkpoint and container image continue to incur small storage charges until removed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We showed how to remove one source of avoidable latency from a voice agent, waiting for an agent’s full response before starting speech synthesis, and how to quantify a second, quieter cost: base64-encoded audio inside SSE inflates payload by about a third and can misalign with MP3 frame boundaries if you don’t buffer for it. Streaming each sentence into a SageMaker AI bidirectional endpoint as soon as an agent produces it cut time to first audio by more than half in isolation, and by 2.4 to 3.5 times in a deployed agent. Multiplexing binary audio and JSON control frames over that session, instead of encoding audio as text, removes the size inflation and frame-realignment work a naive SSE relay requires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’re adding voice to an agent that already streams text over SSE, start with the base64 approach in Step 4 and revisit the unified transport once latency becomes the priority. To learn more, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI real-time inference documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. If you’d like help applying this pattern to your own architecture, contact your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/scale-reservoir-uncertainty-analysis-with-agile-reservoir-modeling/">Scale reservoir uncertainty analysis with agile reservoir modeling</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="turn-subsurface-uncertainty-into-decision-ready-insight">Turn subsurface uncertainty into decision-ready insight</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Drilling campaigns are among the most expensive and operationally complex activities in the energy industry. Rigs, crews, equipment, and logistics are scheduled months in advance, leaving little room for delay. Every hour of downtime can carry significant costs. Geoscientists and engineers rely on reservoir models to understand the subsurface and guide drilling decisions, often under intense time pressure. Yet the current modeling infrastructure that many companies rely on for these critical decisions was designed for smaller datasets, simpler workflows, and siloed operations. As subsurface data grows in scale and complexity, teams need a faster, more connected way to transform uncertainty into actionable decisions.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Now generally available, <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software/delfi/delfi-solutions/agile-reservoir-modeling">agile reservoir modeling</a> (ARM) from SLB, integrated with <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/data-manager-for-energy/">Azure Data Manager for Energy (ADME)</a>, is co-engineered for cloud scale to close that gap. The combined solution connects ensemble-centric reservoir modeling and simulation with a governed data foundation, elastic <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/high-performance-computing/">Azure high-performance computing</a>, and scalable storage designed for the throughput and concurrency of large ensemble studies.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/data-manager-for-energy/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer noreferrer noopener">Learn more about Azure Data Manager for Energy</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="scale-ensemble-modeling-with-arm-on-azure">Scale ensemble modeling with ARM on Azure</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A single reservoir model represents one plausible interpretation of the subsurface. Ensemble modeling evaluates hundreds of model realizations together, giving asset teams a broader view of potential outcomes and a stronger basis for understanding uncertainty and risk.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The industry has long recognized the value of ensemble modeling for risk-aware decision-making, but adoption has been constrained by infrastructure and software designed for smaller, sequential studies. Moving from a limited model set to hundreds of realizations multiplies the compute, storage, data movement, and results that must be managed. ARM addresses this barrier by distributing realization generation and simulation across elastic Azure high-performance computing, while scalable storage provides the capacity, throughput, and parallel access required by ensembles of different sizes.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="connect-reservoir-workflows-to-a-governed-data-foundation">Connect reservoir workflows to a governed data foundation</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Building and updating reservoir models draws on many systems: seismic, wellbore, interpretation, and simulation data, plus the applications and compute that process them. When these pieces are fragmented, customers must connect and orchestrate them on their own, and large-scale simulation becomes a disconnected, one-off exercise.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">SLB and Microsoft combined their respective technologies, ARM engineered by SLB and ADME engineered by Microsoft, to remove that burden. Rather than a set of loosely connected tools, customers get one integrated workflow in which each component has a clear role:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">Azure Data Manager for Energy is the governed data foundation. OSDU® Technical Standard-aligned services and OSDU Domain Data Management Services (DDMS) manage seismic, wellbore, interpretation, and simulation data.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Petrel™ subsurface software is where engineers define interpretation and uncertainty workflows, connected directly to data in ADME.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Agile reservoir modeling is the ensemble workflow engine. It orchestrates the generation of reservoir realizations and simulation runs and supports discovery and analysis at enterprise scale.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As a result, teams build ensembles from trusted corporate data and publish results back for discovery and reuse, which reduces data movement and improves collaboration across asset teams.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="put-ensemble-modeling-into-practice-with-a-connected-workflow">Put ensemble modeling into practice with a connected workflow</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Beyond integration and scale, ARM distinguishes itself by orchestrating all stages of an ensemble study across modeling, simulation, data management, and analysis. The workflow tightly connects the tools reservoir teams already use with Azure-scale infrastructure and the governed data services provided by Azure Data Manager for Energy.</p>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6ae1f53ea3bee&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6ae1f53ea3bee" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-full wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-22-170942.webp" alt="Agile Reservoir Modeling workflow using cloud HPC to generate and simulate multiple reservoir-model realizations in parallel delivered to web-based interface for analysis.." class="wp-image-54721 webp-format" srcset="" data-orig-src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/10/Screenshot-2026-10-22-170942.webp"><button

<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="a-walkthrough-of-how-this-works-today">A walkthrough of how this works today:</h3>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Reservoir engineers define uncertainty workflows in Petrel software and prepare simulation models for supported <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software">SLB simulators</a>, including the <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software/intersect-reservoir-simulator/intersect">Intersect</a>™ reservoir simulator and <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software/eclipse-industry-reference-reservoir-simulator/eclipse">Eclipse</a>™ reservoir simulator. ARM generates the realizations, prepares the simulation cases, and coordinates their execution across the Azure environment.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Azure high-performance computing executes multiple realizations in parallel. A shared, high-performance <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/files/">Azure Files</a> volume holds model inputs, grids, simulation decks, temporary files, and results so the simulation fleet can read and write concurrently. Because compute capacity, storage capacity, throughput, and IOPS can be scaled independently, the environment can be sized for the concurrency of the ensemble rather than the constraints of fixed infrastructure.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As simulations complete, SLB reservoir engineering services manage the resulting ensembles and use <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/energy-data-services/concepts-index-and-search">OSDU search capabilities</a> on ADME to support discovery, filtering, and sorting across large studies. Teams can locate relevant realizations and supported, published results without manually navigating disconnected project stores.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Engineers can analyze ensembles in the <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software/delfi/delfi-solutions/delfi-reservoir-engineering-workspace">reservoir engineering workspace</a> or load them back into Petrel software for further interpretation, history matching, and reservoir engineering studies. This allows teams to work in the environment and application best suited to each task while maintaining continuity, without having to step outside their workflow to manage orchestration.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">ADME provides entitlement-based access control integrated with Microsoft Entra ID and supports compliance controls such as OSDU legal tags. Connecting ARM workflows to this governed data foundation helps maintain consistent access and compliance as corporate subsurface data is used across modeling and simulation. Because ARM workflows are integrated through Petrel software in ADME, reservoir engineers can perform ensemble-scale studies leveraging their corporate-wide data stores and publish results for discovery, eliminating friction and driving collaboration. Microsoft and SLB engineering collaborated closely to build the integration between Petrel software and ARM for enterprise-scale performance.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For reservoir engineers and asset teams, ARM turns ensemble modeling into a repeatable workflow rather than a bespoke infrastructure project. Teams can stress-test development concepts against calibrated ensembles that honor observed production history, not simply run a larger number of models. As new wells are drilled and new data becomes available, they can update their reservoir understanding without waiting for a major model rebuild. For energy IT and data leaders, the workflow connects simulation to an OSDU standard-aligned architecture with consistent identity, governance, search, and interoperability, reducing isolated data copies and disconnected applications.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-future-of-the-reservoir-lifecycle">The future of the reservoir lifecycle</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Building on this partnership, Microsoft and SLB are working within the <a href="https://osduforum.org/data-def-reservoir/">OSDU community</a> to advance a more cloud-native standard for reservoir data. This work will help models, simulation results, and engineering workflows operate more consistently across applications, improving interoperability while reducing dependence on proprietary formats and silos. A shared, governed data foundation also paves the way for AI and agentic experiences that can discover data, coordinate workflows, and surface insights across the reservoir lifecycle. Combined with Azure-scale compute and storage, this creates a path toward more automated and continuous reservoir modeling.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="get-started-with-arm-on-azure-data-manager-for-energy">Get started with ARM on Azure Data Manager for Energy</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Agile reservoir modeling on Azure Data Manager for Energy is generally available today. Learn more about <a href="https://www.slb.com/products-and-services/delivering-digital-at-scale/software/delfi/delfi-solutions/agile-reservoir-modeling">agile reservoir modeling from SLB</a> and <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/data-manager-for-energy/">Azure Data Manager for Energy</a>. Contact your Microsoft and SLB account teams to identify an initial reservoir workflow and discuss the path to deployment.</p>
